The three kinds of wisdom are: first, the wisdom whose object is conventional truth; second, the wisdom whose object is ultimate truth; and third, the wisdom whose object is benefiting sentient beings.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Citing the Bodhisattva-bhūmi (juan 43), Master Cháng Bǎi explains concentration's blissful-abiding kind as complete freedom from discrimination, generating bodily and mental pliancy, free of arrogance about one's own attainment (since a bodhisattva willingly relinquishes concentration's own pleasure to return to the desire realm rather than clinging to it) and free of any remaining characteristic-bearing appearance whatsoever; the quality-generating kind as the inconceivable, immeasurable states of absorption tied to the ten powers that neither śrāvakas nor pratyekabuddhas can so much as name, together with the liberations, bases of mastery, and other qualities shared with the lesser vehicles; and the benefit-generating kind as the same elevenfold catalogue of active service, now accomplished specifically through the stability concentration provides. Wisdom's three kinds close this seventh sub-topic: the wisdom whose object is conventional truth is decisive skill in the five fields of learning (inner wisdom, logic, medicine, linguistics, and craft) and in three classes of teaching (what leads to benefit, what leads to harm, and what leads to neither); the wisdom whose object is ultimate truth is the threefold progression of wisdom approaching, directly realizing, and following upon direct realization of the wordless nature of dharmas — free of discrimination and discursive elaboration as realization nears, entering the great universal characteristic as realization occurs, and settling into the middle way, free of both exaggeration and denial, once realization has passed — Master Cháng Bǎi noting via the *Sub-commentary* that this ultimate wisdom is elsewhere specified as wisdom regarding the sevenfold suchness itself; and the wisdom whose object is benefiting sentient beings is, once again, the same elevenfold catalogue of active service, now accomplished through wisdom's own discernment. With all six pāramitās' threefold subdivisions now complete, the seventh of the seventeen sub-topics closes in full.

51. Section: Why Called "Reaching the Far Shore" — Avalokiteśvara's Question

World-Honored One! For what reason is a pāramitā called a pāramitā?

Chinese Source

世尊！何因緣故，波羅蜜多說名波羅蜜多？

Ru-Yi Translation

World-Honored One! For what reason is a pāramitā called a pāramitā?

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Avalokiteśvara opens the eighth of the seventeen sub-topics by asking the Buddha to explain, in his own words, the very term this entire section has been using throughout — pāramitā, already glossed at this fascicle's opening as reaching the far shore, an accomplishment brought to its utmost completion. Master Cháng Bǎi notes this question turns from what the six (or ten) pāramitās are and how they are structured to why the name itself is deserved — a question the coming section will answer by specifying exactly which qualities must be present before an act of giving, discipline, or any other virtue actually earns the name "pāramitā" rather than remaining merely its ordinary, unperfected counterpart.

52. Section: Five Reasons — Free of Attachment to What Opposes the Perfections

Good sons! For five reasons: first, because of being free of attachment; second, because of being free of lingering regard; third, because of being free of fault; fourth, because of being free of discrimination; and fifth, because of correct dedication. Being free of attachment means not being attached to whatever opposes the pāramitās.

Chinese Source

善男子！五因緣故。一者、無染著故；二者、無顧戀故；三者、無罪過故；四者、無分別故；五者、正迴向故。無染著者，謂不染著波羅蜜多諸相違事。

Ru-Yi Translation

Good sons! For five reasons: first, because of being free of attachment; second, because of being free of lingering regard; third, because of being free of fault; fourth, because of being free of discrimination; and fifth, because of correct dedication. Being free of attachment means not being attached to whatever opposes the pāramitās.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

The Buddha now answers directly why a pāramitā deserves the name: five qualities must all be present, matching the Decisive-Discussion chapter's own parallel account (juan 75) of what qualifies giving specifically as "giving that reaches the far shore." Being free of attachment means not seeing any real merit or advantage in whatever directly opposes each pāramitā — not seeing merit in the pleasure of wealth and self-indulgence (opposing giving), in license to act, speak, or think however one pleases (opposing discipline), in retaliating against contempt or provocation (opposing patience), in the ease of indulging desire rather than exerting effort (opposing diligence), in the noise and confusion of crowded, scattered activity (opposing meditation), or in the discursive elaboration of what is merely seen, heard, sensed, and known (opposing wisdom) — since seeing genuine advantage in any of these six opposites is precisely what keeps a virtue from ever reaching the far shore. The Decisive-Discussion chapter glosses this as freedom from any obstruction: a bodhisattva who observes the danger in whatever obstructs giving and the rest, seeing it as dependently arisen and without real substance, releases the grasping at giver, gift, and recipient alike, remaining unobstructed by any lingering mental attachment.

53. Section: Free of Lingering Regard — Neither Seeking Reward Nor Craving Gratitude

Being free of lingering regard means the mind is unbound by any of the ripened fruits of the pāramitās, or by any expectation of being repaid.

Chinese Source

無顧戀者，謂於一切波羅蜜多諸果異熟及報恩中，心無繫縛。

Ru-Yi Translation

Being free of lingering regard means the mind is unbound by any of the ripened fruits of the pāramitās, or by any expectation of being repaid.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Every pāramitā yields a favorable result — giving yields wealth, discipline a fortunate rebirth, patience freedom from enmity and abundant joy, diligence leadership among beings, meditation freedom from bodily affliction, and wisdom a wide, flourishing following — yet a bodhisattva must not practice for the sake of any of these results, or for the gratitude of those benefited, the mind remaining entirely unattached to either. Citing the Decisive-Discussion chapter and several parallel translations of the sūtra, Master Cháng Bǎi notes the consistent gloss: a bodhisattva gives purely out of compassion for beings scorched by the fire of craving, never seeking anything returned, and never giving in the hope of a better rebirth for oneself, seeing every conditioned activity as illusory and looking instead only to the excellent qualities a great bodhisattva embodies — a giving untainted by any calculation of what it might bring back.

54. Section: Free of Fault — No Defilement Intermixed, No Merely Material Help Mistaken for Real Benefit

Being free of fault means that, with regard to the pāramitās, no defiling dharma is intermixed with them, and one is free of conduct lacking skillful means.

Chinese Source

無罪過者，謂於如是波羅蜜多，無間雜染法，離非方便行。

Ru-Yi Translation

Being free of fault means that, with regard to the pāramitās, no defiling dharma is intermixed with them, and one is free of conduct lacking skillful means.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi names four defiling intrusions a bodhisattva must keep from intermixing with the pāramitās: applying them without compassion, without correct attention, without constancy, or without earnest care — and warns as well against "conduct lacking skillful means," meaning practice that merely relieves a being's material want without ever raising them toward genuine goodness. He quotes the root text's own vivid illustration directly: giving material relief alone, however much of it, without also establishing the recipient in wholesome practice, is like heaping up filth — no quantity of it, however great or small, can ever be made fragrant; beings suffer inherently by nature, and no amount of material comfort can make that suffering itself into happiness, so only placing a being within the excellent Dharma can properly be called the foremost benefit. Beyond avoiding these four intrusions and this shallow substitute for real benefit, a bodhisattva must also keep the pāramitās free from ten specific secondary afflictions the Decisive-Discussion chapter enumerates — five outwardly directed (pervasive defiled agitation, an unwillingness to relinquish, clinging to what is agreeable, an unfulfilled craving, and an immature quality) and five inwardly directed (a failure to renounce, an inward defiled agitation, a thin and inferior quality, a currently active fall into affliction, and a tendency toward exhaustion) — Master Cháng Bǎi noting the practical rule this yields: a bodhisattva must never provoke greed, hatred, or delusion in a recipient through the very act of giving, and should withhold material support entirely from anyone whose only response to it would be arrogance or contempt toward others.

55. Section: Free of Discrimination, and Correct Dedication — Five Reasons Complete

Being free of discrimination means not clinging, as the words themselves might suggest, to the specific characteristic of the pāramitās. Correct dedication means dedicating whatever pāramitā has been accomplished and accumulated toward seeking the fruit of unsurpassed great enlightenment.

Chinese Source

無分別者，謂於如是波羅蜜多，不如言辭執著自相。正迴向者，謂以如是所作所集波羅蜜多，迴求無上大菩提果。

Ru-Yi Translation

Being free of discrimination means not clinging, as the words themselves might suggest, to the specific characteristic of the pāramitās. Correct dedication means dedicating whatever pāramitā has been accomplished and accumulated toward seeking the fruit of unsurpassed great enlightenment.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Being free of discrimination means practicing the threefold purity of giver, gift, and recipient all being empty — not fixing on "I am giving," "I am keeping precepts," and so on as though each label named something substantially real, transcending verbal designation and discursive elaboration through non-discriminating wisdom itself, since clinging to even the pāramitā's own name and appearance prevents the very purity that lets it reach the far shore. Correct dedication means every act and accumulation of virtue, from its very first instance through its continued growth, is dedicated to unsurpassed great bodhi alone — not toward fine possessions, not even toward an excellent human or heavenly rebirth, but toward buddhahood itself and nothing less. Only when all five qualities — freedom from attachment, from lingering regard, from fault, from discrimination, and correct dedication — are jointly present does a virtuous act actually earn the name pāramitā, reaching the far shore in truth. Master Cháng Bǎi closes by surveying, briefly, how differently other treatises count these same defining marks — the *Mahāyāna-sūtrālaṃkāra*'s four (counteracting the opposing obstruction, joined with non-discriminating wisdom, fulfilling what beings request, and maturing them), the *Abhidharmasamuccaya*'s alternate five (arising from the bodhisattva lineage itself, from the vow, from compassionate intention, from the deed performed, and from the very substance of body, speech, and mind) or its own further scheme of six (arising, habituation, essential nature, and natural outflow), the *Mahāyānasaṃgraha*'s six marks of supremacy (basis, activity, domain, skillful means, dedication, and purity), the *Cheng Weishi Lun*'s sevenfold scheme (abiding in the lineage, resting on bodhicitta, compassion for all beings, engaging fully in every undertaking, being embraced by signless wisdom, dedication to enlightenment, and freedom from either obstruction's interference) — without which, the *Cheng Weishi Lun* states flatly, an act of giving or the rest simply does not reach the far shore at all — and still further eightfold and twelvefold countings elsewhere, all converging, Master Cháng Bǎi notes, on the same essential point already given here in five: nothing less than freedom from attachment, regard, fault, and discrimination, joined with correct dedication, actually completes a pāramitā, closing the eighth of the seventeen sub-topics.

56. Section: What Opposes the Perfections — Six Kinds Named

World-Honored One! What is meant by that which opposes the pāramitās? Good sons! You should know that, in brief, these are six.

Chinese Source

世尊！何等名為波羅蜜多諸相違事？善男子！當知此事略有六種。

Ru-Yi Translation

World-Honored One! What is meant by that which opposes the pāramitās? Good sons! You should know that, in brief, these are six.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Having established what completes a pāramitā, Avalokiteśvara now asks the ninth of the seventeen sub-topics — the six specific things that oppose it, already gestured at in the account of freedom from attachment (the pleasure of wealth opposing giving, license opposing discipline, and so on) but not yet spelled out one by one. Master Cháng Bǎi notes this begins a run of four further, more detailed sub-topics — what opposes the pāramitās, and, as the coming sections will show, the specific benefits, mutual relationships, and further distinctions each pāramitā involves — the Buddha's answer to this particular question naming six specific opposing vices to be examined individually next.

57. Section: Six Opposing Things — Seeing False Merit in Desire, License, and Idle Discourse

First, seeing deep merit and advantage in the pleasures of desire, wealth, and mastery over enjoyment; second, seeing deep merit and advantage in acting however one pleases in body, speech, and mind; third, seeing deep merit and advantage in being unable to bear another's contempt; fourth, seeing deep merit and advantage in failing to diligently cultivate and instead clinging to pleasure; fifth, seeing deep merit and advantage in dwelling amid the confused, scattered activity of a noisy world; and sixth, seeing deep merit and advantage in the discursive elaboration of what is seen, heard, sensed, and known.

Chinese Source

一者、於喜樂欲財富自在諸欲樂中，深見功德及與勝利；二者、於隨所樂縱身語意而現行中，深見功德及與勝利；三者、於他輕懱不堪忍中，深見功德及與勝利；四者、於不勤修著欲樂中，深見功德及與勝利；五者、於處憒鬧世雜亂行，深見功德及與勝利；六者、於見聞覺知言說戲論，深見功德及與勝利。

Ru-Yi Translation

First, seeing deep merit and advantage in the pleasures of desire, wealth, and mastery over enjoyment; second, seeing deep merit and advantage in acting however one pleases in body, speech, and mind; third, seeing deep merit and advantage in being unable to bear another's contempt; fourth, seeing deep merit and advantage in failing to diligently cultivate and instead clinging to pleasure; fifth, seeing deep merit and advantage in dwelling amid the confused, scattered activity of a noisy world; and sixth, seeing deep merit and advantage in the discursive elaboration of what is seen, heard, sensed, and known.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

The *Sub-commentary* confirms these six correspond, in order, to what opposes giving through wisdom: taking pleasure in and craving the desirable forms, sounds, smells, tastes, and touches the six senses meet opposes giving directly; delighting in freely acting out killing or other unwholesome deeds — believing, say, that slaughtering a sheep leads to heaven — opposes discipline; being unable to bear contempt or provocation, lacking all three kinds of patience already distinguished, opposes patience; treating idleness itself as praiseworthy, mistaking laziness for ease, opposes diligence; delighting in noisy, crowded, scattered activity opposes meditative concentration — Master Cháng Bǎi cataloguing here the traditional five or six kinds of mental distraction (distraction of essential nature through the five senses, outward distraction chasing sense objects, inward distraction through elation or dullness, distraction of coarseness through self-clinging, distraction of intention toward the lesser vehicle, and — in the sixfold count — distraction of characteristics through feigned stillness) already treated at length in this treatise's earlier account of the chapter distinguishing yoga; and clinging to discursive elaboration built on what is merely seen, heard, sensed, and known — every verbal proliferation the six senses and consciousnesses generate — opposes wisdom. A bodhisattva seeking to actually complete any pāramitā must recognize none of these six as containing any real merit whatsoever.

58. Section: Six Ripened Fruits — Great Wealth, Favorable Rebirth, and a Great Following

World-Honored One! What is the ripened fruit of all these pāramitās? Good sons! You should know that, in brief, this too is of six kinds: first, attaining great wealth; second, being reborn in a favorable destiny; third, being free of enmity and injury, and abounding in joy; fourth, becoming a leader among beings; fifth, a body free of affliction and harm; and sixth, having a great following.

Chinese Source

世尊！如是一切波羅蜜多，何果異熟？善男子！當知此亦略有六種。一者、得大財富；二者、往生善趣；三者、無怨無壞，多諸喜樂；四者、為眾生主；五者、身無惱害；六者、有大宗葉。

Ru-Yi Translation

World-Honored One! What is the ripened fruit of all these pāramitās? Good sons! You should know that, in brief, this too is of six kinds: first, attaining great wealth; second, being reborn in a favorable destiny; third, being free of enmity and injury, and abounding in joy; fourth, becoming a leader among beings; fifth, a body free of affliction and harm; and sixth, having a great following.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Avalokiteśvara opens the tenth of the seventeen sub-topics with a question about ripened fruit specifically — one of five fruits the Decisive-Discussion chapter's own broader account distinguishes (freedom from bondage, human agency, ever-increasing outflow, dominant result, and ripened fruit), the last of which this passage now details pāramitā by pāramitā: great wealth from giving (part of the first ground's own tenfold mastery, mastery over things); favorable rebirth among gods and humans from discipline; freedom from enmity and abundant joy from patience (fewer enemies made, more affection returned); leadership among beings from diligence (the natural authority tireless effort earns, in any setting); a body free of affliction from meditative concentration (the ease and pliancy concentration brings, sparing both self-inflicted and other-inflicted harm); and a great following, likened to a great tree's flourishing branches and leaves, from wisdom, which draws capable, like-minded companions who can help sustain the whole practice. Master Cháng Bǎi ties this directly back to the fifth of the qualities distinguishing a genuine pāramitā, freedom from lingering regard: a bodhisattva does not practice for the sake of any of these six fruits or for the gratitude of those helped, and clinging to any of them as the goal would itself already disqualify the practice from reaching the far shore at all.

59. Section: Four Kinds of Application That Defile — Lacking Compassion, Principle, Constancy, and Earnest Care

World-Honored One! What is meant by the defiling dharmas that intermix with the pāramitās? Good sons! You should know that, in brief, this comes through four kinds of application: first, application lacking compassion; second, application not in accordance with principle; third, application that is not constant; and fourth, application lacking earnest care. Application not in accordance with principle means that, while cultivating one pāramitā, one neglects and thereby damages the others.

Chinese Source

世尊！何等名為波羅蜜多間雜染法？善男子！當知略由四種加行。一者、無悲加行故；二者、不如理加行故；三者、不常加行故；四者、不殷重加行故。不如理加行者，謂修行餘波羅蜜多時，於餘波羅蜜多遠離失壞。

Ru-Yi Translation

World-Honored One! What is meant by the defiling dharmas that intermix with the pāramitās? Good sons! You should know that, in brief, this comes through four kinds of application: first, application lacking compassion; second, application not in accordance with principle; third, application that is not constant; and fourth, application lacking earnest care. Application not in accordance with principle means that, while cultivating one pāramitā, one neglects and thereby damages the others.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Avalokiteśvara opens the eleventh sub-topic with a question about defiling dharmas — what actually spoils practice already underway. Master Cháng Bǎi maps the Buddha's fourfold answer directly onto the *Abhidharmakośa*'s (juan 27) account of what makes a buddha's own accumulated causal virtue complete: application lacking compassion obstructs sustained cultivation across the full three incalculable eons, since only the unbearable urgency of true compassion for suffering beings can sustain effort across a span that long; application not in accordance with principle obstructs cultivation without omission, meaning here specifically the failure to cultivate every pāramitā together rather than fixating on just one (favoring wisdom alone while neglecting merit, or the reverse) as though only half the path — wisdom and merit both being needed, as the tradition says, like the "two-legged honored one" — mattered; application that is not constant obstructs uninterrupted cultivation, a newly resolved bodhisattva's practice starting and stopping by turns, diligent when circumstances favor it and slack the moment they do not; and application lacking earnest care obstructs cultivation carried out with genuine reverence, meaning practice performed carelessly, going through the motions rather than genuinely valuing both the self-benefiting work of ending one's own affliction and the other-benefiting work of helping beings. Master Cháng Bǎi singles out the second for closer illustration: a bodhisattva who gives, for instance, by handing out communal monastic property without regard for whether doing so violates discipline has practiced giving at the expense of discipline — precisely this kind of lopsided practice, favoring one pāramitā while damaging another, is what "application not in accordance with principle" names, the corrective being to cultivate compassion and wisdom, and all six pāramitās, jointly rather than in isolation.

60. Section: Conduct Lacking Skillful Means — Filth Cannot Be Made Fragrant However Much Is Piled Up

World-Honored One! What is meant by conduct lacking skillful means? Good sons! If bodhisattvas, in benefiting sentient beings through the pāramitās, are satisfied merely with gathering material wealth to benefit them, without causing them to leave what is unwholesome and be established in what is wholesome, this is called conduct lacking skillful means. Why is this so? Good sons! To do only this for sentient beings is not what is called truly benefiting them. It is like filth: whether much or little, it can never be made fragrant and pure. In just the same way, because sentient beings are subject to the suffering of formations, their very nature is suffering, and there is no skillful means by which material benefit alone, however temporarily helpful, could turn this into true happiness. Only by establishing them within the wondrous, excellent Dharma can this be called the foremost benefit.

Chinese Source

世尊！何等名為非方便行？善男子！若諸菩薩，以波羅蜜多饒益眾生時，但攝財物饒益眾生便為喜足，而不令其出不善處，安置善處，如是名為非方便行。何以故？善男子！非於眾生唯作此事名實饒益。譬如糞穢，若多、若少，終無有能令成香潔。如是眾生由行苦故，其性是苦，無有方便但以財物暫相饒益可令成樂。唯有安處妙善法中，方可得名第一饒益。

Ru-Yi Translation

World-Honored One! What is meant by conduct lacking skillful means? Good sons! If bodhisattvas, in benefiting sentient beings through the pāramitās, are satisfied merely with gathering material wealth to benefit them, without causing them to leave what is unwholesome and be established in what is wholesome, this is called conduct lacking skillful means. Why is this so? Good sons! To do only this for sentient beings is not what is called truly benefiting them. It is like filth: whether much or little, it can never be made fragrant and pure. In just the same way, because sentient beings are subject to the suffering of formations, their very nature is suffering, and there is no skillful means by which material benefit alone, however temporarily helpful, could turn this into true happiness. Only by establishing them within the wondrous, excellent Dharma can this be called the foremost benefit.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Avalokiteśvara's twelfth question asks about conduct lacking skillful means — what the *Mahāsaṃnipāta-sūtra* defines by its opposite, "guiding beings so that all turn toward unsurpassed enlightenment," making its negation simply guidance that stops short of this. A bodhisattva who relieves material want — food, shelter, clothing — and considers the work complete has not yet actually helped, since only the giving of the Dharma itself, spoken without self-interest to reveal true meaning and lead beings out of unwholesome ways into wholesome ones, constitutes real benefit. Master Cháng Bǎi lingers over the sūtra's own image: filth remains filth no matter how much or little is gathered, never becoming fragrant regardless of quantity — the *Sub-commentary* glossing filth as the suffering of formations itself (the subtle, pervasive unease built into every conditioned moment, arising, abiding, changing, and ceasing without a single instant of true rest) and fragrance as nirvāṇa, unborn, unceasing, tranquil, and unconditioned. Citing the Decisive-Discussion chapter's own definition of the suffering of formations (juan 66) — the coarseness inherent in every conditioned activity produced by karma and affliction, present within neutral feeling most conspicuously of all, since neither pleasure nor pain masks it there — Master Cháng Bǎi draws the conclusion plainly: no quantity of material relief can transform this underlying suffering into genuine happiness; only placing beings within the pāramitās and the wholesome Dharma itself, establishing right view, subduing affliction, and turning them toward every good and away from every evil, deserves to be called the foremost benefit, and only a bodhisattva's practice free of both this shortfall and the defiling application just discussed can properly be called a pāramitā at all. With this, the four sub-topics gathered under "further elaboration on what is difficult" — what opposes the pāramitās, their ripened fruit, defiling application, and conduct lacking skillful means — come to their complete close.

61. Section: No Purity Beyond the Five — Yet to Be Explained Both Together and Separately

World-Honored One! How many kinds of purity do all these pāramitās have, taken both together and separately? Good sons! I have never taught that a pāramitā has any purity beyond the five marks already given. Yet, in dependence on these same matters, I shall now explain the marks of a pāramitā's purity, both taken together and separately.

Chinese Source

世尊！如是一切波羅蜜多，有幾清淨？善男子！我終不說波羅蜜多除上五相有餘清淨，然我即依如是諸事，總別當說波羅蜜多清淨之相。

Ru-Yi Translation

World-Honored One! How many kinds of purity do all these pāramitās have, taken both together and separately? Good sons! I have never taught that a pāramitā has any purity beyond the five marks already given. Yet, in dependence on these same matters, I shall now explain the marks of a pāramitā's purity, both taken together and separately.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Avalokiteśvara opens the thirteenth of the seventeen sub-topics by asking how many kinds of purity the pāramitās have. The Buddha's answer is deliberately anchored to what has already been established: nothing beyond the five marks distinguished earlier — freedom from attachment, from lingering regard, from fault, from discrimination, and correct dedication — counts as genuine purity; what follows is simply a further unpacking of those same five marks, first as they apply to all six pāramitās together, then as each pāramitā's own seven distinctive marks of purity in turn.

62. Section: Seven Marks Shared by Every Perfection — Not Seeking Recognition, Free of Doubt and Envy

As to the marks of purity for all the pāramitās taken together, there should be known to be seven. What are the seven? First, bodhisattvas do not seek to have these very qualities known by others; second, having seen these qualities, they do not become attached to them; third, they never doubt whether these very qualities can bring about great enlightenment; fourth, they never praise themselves or disparage others, treating them with contempt; fifth, they are never arrogant or negligent; sixth, they are never satisfied merely because some small attainment has been reached; and seventh, they never give rise, on account of these qualities, to envy or stinginess toward others.

Chinese Source

總說一切波羅蜜多清淨相者，當知七種。何等為七？一者、菩薩於此諸法不求他知；二者、於此諸法見已不生執著；三者、即於如是諸法不生疑惑，謂為能得大菩提不；四者、終不自讚毀他，有所輕懱；五者、終不憍傲放逸；六者、終不少有所得便生喜足；七者、終不由此諸法，於他發起嫉妒慳吝。

Ru-Yi Translation

As to the marks of purity for all the pāramitās taken together, there should be known to be seven. What are the seven? First, bodhisattvas do not seek to have these very qualities known by others; second, having seen these qualities, they do not become attached to them; third, they never doubt whether these very qualities can bring about great enlightenment; fourth, they never praise themselves or disparage others, treating them with contempt; fifth, they are never arrogant or negligent; sixth, they are never satisfied merely because some small attainment has been reached; and seventh, they never give rise, on account of these qualities, to envy or stinginess toward others.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi walks through the seven briefly. Not seeking recognition means practicing the six pāramitās for their own sake, never for reputation or worldly gain; not becoming attached means holding the pāramitās themselves free of the very discrimination already discussed, refusing to cling to their own verbal characteristics through three-fold empty giver, gift, and recipient; being free of doubt means unwavering conviction that this practice genuinely leads to great enlightenment, since doubt itself is often enough to halt practice before it bears fruit; not praising oneself while disparaging others guards against the specific arrogance of assuming one's own capacity is unique; freedom from arrogance and negligence guards against complacent self-satisfaction at any success already achieved; not being satisfied with a small attainment keeps a bodhisattva pressing further rather than resting content with whatever modest progress has been made; and freedom from envy or stinginess toward others' own progress in the same pāramitās means rejoicing in another's success rather than begrudging it. Together these seven overarching marks close the first part of the thirteenth sub-topic — the shared purity of all six pāramitās at once — before the coming sections turn to each pāramitā's own seven distinctive marks in turn.

63. Section: Seven Marks of Pure Giving — What Is Given, and Being Free of Stinginess and Hoarding

As to the marks of purity for each pāramitā taken separately, there are likewise seven for each. Namely: bodhisattvas practice giving in accordance with the seven marks of pure giving that I now teach. First, practicing pure giving through the purity of what is given; second, through the purity of discipline; third, through the purity of view; fourth, through the purity of mind; fifth, through the purity of speech; sixth, through the purity of wisdom; and seventh, through the purity of being free of defilement. These are called the seven marks of pure giving.

Chinese Source

別說一切波羅蜜多清淨相者，亦有七種。謂諸菩薩，如我所說七種布施清淨之相，隨順修行。一者、由施物清淨，行清淨施；二者、由戒清淨，行清淨施；三者、由見清淨，行清淨施；四者、由心清淨，行清淨施；五者、由語清淨，行清淨施；六者、由智清淨，行清淨施；七者、由垢清淨，行清淨施。是名七種施清淨相。

Ru-Yi Translation

As to the marks of purity for each pāramitā taken separately, there are likewise seven for each. Namely: bodhisattvas practice giving in accordance with the seven marks of pure giving that I now teach. First, practicing pure giving through the purity of what is given; second, through the purity of discipline; third, through the purity of view; fourth, through the purity of mind; fifth, through the purity of speech; sixth, through the purity of wisdom; and seventh, through the purity of being free of defilement. These are called the seven marks of pure giving.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi notes the Decisive-Discussion chapter (juan 74) unpacks each of these seven into a further ten aspects apiece, more than can be repeated here in full, but the outline itself is clear enough: purity of what is given means the gift itself is lawfully obtained, fine, and appropriate to the recipient's actual need; purity of discipline means the giving itself never violates the precepts, as when property belonging to the monastic community is given away without regard for whether doing so is lawful; purity of view means giving free of any distorted view about karma or its result; purity of mind means giving with a mind free of anger, resentment, or any other affliction at the moment of giving; purity of speech means accompanying the gift with kind, encouraging words rather than a grudging or contemptuous manner; purity of wisdom means giving with clear discernment of what, how, and to whom to give, without regret afterward; and purity of freedom from defilement means being free of the two specific stains the *Sub-commentary* names — stinginess (attachment to the object itself, unable to let it go) and hoarding (attachment to its future use, accumulating far more than one could ever need) — since a giver still bound by either stain cannot give generously enough to actually meet a recipient's real need. Only when all seven are jointly present does an act of giving qualify as genuinely pure giving.

64. Section: Seven Marks of Pure Discipline — Knowing Every Precept, and Renewing What Has Been Broken

Further, bodhisattvas who are able to skillfully know every precept established within the code of restraint, who are able to skillfully know how to be released from any transgression, who possess constant morality, firm morality, morality actively practiced, morality continually renewed, and who undertake to learn every one of the precepts — these are called the seven marks of pure discipline.

Chinese Source

又諸菩薩，能善了知制立律儀一切學處，能善了知出離所犯，具常尸羅，堅固尸羅，常作尸羅，常轉尸羅，受學一切所有學處，是名七種戒清淨相。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, bodhisattvas who are able to skillfully know every precept established within the code of restraint, who are able to skillfully know how to be released from any transgression, who possess constant morality, firm morality, morality actively practiced, morality continually renewed, and who undertake to learn every one of the precepts — these are called the seven marks of pure discipline.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi unpacks each mark in turn, citing the Decisive-Discussion chapter (juan 82) throughout. Skillfully knowing every precept means genuinely studying the vinaya — why each rule was established, when it may be relaxed, when it must be strictly upheld — rather than merely receiving ordination without ever learning what it actually entails. Skillfully knowing release from transgression means knowing the proper means of confession and repentance for whatever level of offense has occurred, whether through visualization, formal ritual, or the realization of non-arising, so that a lapse is never simply left unaddressed. Constant morality means never abandoning the precepts once received, upheld to the end of this life for a monastic and to the end of all future lives for a bodhisattva's own vow. Firm morality means holding the precepts even at the cost of one's own life rather than surviving through their violation. Morality actively practiced means positive cultivation alongside mere restraint — not only refraining from killing but actively saving life, not only refraining from theft but actively giving, treating the precepts as something to be lived out rather than merely avoided. Morality continually renewed means that even a lapse, once it occurs, is quickly mended through sincere repentance and a renewed resolve never to repeat it, the precepts' own protective power restored rather than permanently forfeited. And undertaking to learn every precept means genuinely studying, without omission, everything a teacher instructs regarding both the letter and the spirit of the training. Master Cháng Bǎi closes by noting the *Sub-commentary*'s own tidy gloss on four of these terms: constant morality is simply never abandoning what has been learned; firm morality is never violating it; morality actively practiced is keeping it entirely intact, without a single breach; and morality continually renewed is precisely the swift mending of a breach once it occurs — together these seven form the complete standard of pure discipline.

65. Section: Seven Marks of Pure Patience — Faith in Karma, and Never Abandoning a Kindness Once Begun

If bodhisattvas place deep, reliant faith in their own karmic ripening, so that whenever anything unbeneficial confronts them, they do not become indignant, do not retaliate with abuse, anger, blows, threats, or mockery; if they do not repay any kind of unbeneficial act by harming in return; if they harbor no lingering resentment; if, when admonishing or instructing another, they do not provoke resentment or distress; if they do not wait for another to come and admonish or instruct them first; if they do not practice patience out of fear or out of a defiled, attached mind; and if they do not abandon a kindness once done — these are called the seven marks of pure patience.

Chinese Source

若諸菩薩，於自所有業果異熟深生依信，一切所有不饒益事現在前時，不生憤發，亦不反罵，不瞋、不打、不恐、不弄；不以種種不饒益事反相加害；不懷怨結；若諫誨時不令恚惱；亦復不待他來諫誨；不由恐怖有染愛心而行忍辱；不以作恩而便放捨。是名七種忍清淨相。

Ru-Yi Translation

If bodhisattvas place deep, reliant faith in their own karmic ripening, so that whenever anything unbeneficial confronts them, they do not become indignant, do not retaliate with abuse, anger, blows, threats, or mockery; if they do not repay any kind of unbeneficial act by harming in return; if they harbor no lingering resentment; if, when admonishing or instructing another, they do not provoke resentment or distress; if they do not wait for another to come and admonish or instruct them first; if they do not practice patience out of fear or out of a defiled, attached mind; and if they do not abandon a kindness once done — these are called the seven marks of pure patience.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi walks through all seven. The first — deep faith in one's own karmic ripening, meeting abuse, anger, blows, threats, or mockery without indignation or retaliation — rests on genuinely accepting that whatever harm now arrives is simply the fruit of one's own past action, an acceptance ordinary instinct, which craves retaliation and correction of the offender, finds difficult but which patience, grounded in real conviction about karma, makes possible. The second — never repaying unbeneficial treatment with harm in return — rests on the wisdom of selflessness: recognizing neither a substantial self nor a substantial other, a bodhisattva simply has no basis on which to retaliate. The third — harboring no lingering resentment — recognizes that resentment itself compounds suffering, turning another's affliction into one's own. The fourth — admonishing or correcting another's fault skillfully, without provoking further resentment or distress — calls for real tact in how correction is delivered. The fifth — not waiting for another to come and apologize first — means actively working to dissolve a rift oneself, whether by clarifying a misunderstanding or by offering one's own apology first, rather than waiting to be placated. The sixth — patience free of fear or of a defiled, attached motive — distinguishes genuine patience from mere capitulation to a stronger party's power, or from tolerating mistreatment merely to keep receiving some benefit from the one mistreating you; true patience comes from real capacity to overcome the impulse to retaliate, exercised for the sake of both parties, not from being cowed or bought. And the seventh — never abandoning a kindness once done — means sustaining care for a being's benefit continuously, rather than considering one act of help sufficient and withdrawing further support, exactly as the Bodhisattva-bhūmi's own account of pure patience states (juan 42): the wish to benefit remains constantly present, no different before, during, or after a single act of kindness, since benefit given once and then withdrawn was never patience carried through to completion in the first place.

66. Section: Seven Marks of Pure Diligence — The Lute String Neither Too Tight Nor Too Loose

If bodhisattvas penetrate the equal nature of diligence, and do not, through courageous and diligent exertion, exalt themselves over others; if they possess great strength, possess great diligence, have the capacity to endure, are firm and courageous, and never abandon the yoke of every wholesome quality — these are called the seven marks of pure diligence.

Chinese Source

若諸菩薩，通達精進平等之性，不由勇猛勤精進故自舉凌他，具大勢力，具大精進，有所堪能，堅固，勇猛，於諸善法終不捨軛，如是名為七種精進清淨之相。

Ru-Yi Translation

If bodhisattvas penetrate the equal nature of diligence, and do not, through courageous and diligent exertion, exalt themselves over others; if they possess great strength, possess great diligence, have the capacity to endure, are firm and courageous, and never abandon the yoke of every wholesome quality — these are called the seven marks of pure diligence.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi opens with the "equal nature of diligence" — neither too slack nor too forced — through the well-known story of Śroṇa Koṭikarṇa, a lute-master turned monk who drove himself to exhaustion, eyes and ears bleeding from unrelenting effort, and, a month later still without any result, resolved to disrobe and return to lay life as a wealthy patron instead. The Buddha, knowing his heart, asked him whether a lute string pulled too tight or too loose could ever sound sweet — neither, Śroṇa admitted — and explained that diligence works the same way: forcing effort far beyond what the body and mind can actually sustain, to the point of ruining one's health or even shortening one's life, defeats its own purpose just as surely as slackness does. A bodhisattva calibrates diligence to whatever stage of practice is currently reached, neither more nor less, and remains free of the conceit of exalting oneself above others on account of one's own effort. The remaining five marks Master Cháng Bǎi maps directly onto the *Sub-commentary*'s fivefold correspondence with the three kinds of diligence already distinguished earlier in this section: possessing great strength and possessing great diligence both flow from armor-like diligence, the initial resolve that gives diligence its very momentum; the capacity to endure — bearing hunger, thirst, heat, cold, fatigue, and discouragement without ever giving up — and being firm and courageous — undeterred by any threat or provocation meant to turn one from practice — both flow from the diligence that actively accomplishes the pāramitās; and never abandoning the yoke of every wholesome quality, refusing to rest content with even a small attainment already gained, names the diligence that benefits sentient beings, keeping every wholesome practice yoked together like a team pulling as one rather than left to drift apart.

67. Section: Seven Marks of Pure Meditation — From Skillful Method to the Immeasurable Samādhis of the Mahāyāna

If bodhisattvas have the meditative concentration that skillfully penetrates characteristics, the meditative concentration that is fully perfected, the meditative concentration possessing both aspects together, the meditative concentration in active operation, the meditative concentration without any support, the meditative concentration that is well cultivated and refined, and the meditative concentration of immeasurable states cultivated through hearing and reflecting on the bodhisattva canon — these are called the seven marks of pure meditative concentration.

Chinese Source

若諸菩薩，有善通達相三摩地靜慮，有圓滿三摩地靜慮，有俱分三摩地靜慮，有運轉三摩地靜慮，有無所依三摩地靜慮，有善修治三摩地靜慮，有於菩薩藏聞緣修習無量三摩地靜慮，如是名為七種靜慮清淨之相。

Ru-Yi Translation

If bodhisattvas have the meditative concentration that skillfully penetrates characteristics, the meditative concentration that is fully perfected, the meditative concentration possessing both aspects together, the meditative concentration in active operation, the meditative concentration without any support, the meditative concentration that is well cultivated and refined, and the meditative concentration of immeasurable states cultivated through hearing and reflecting on the bodhisattva canon — these are called the seven marks of pure meditative concentration.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi walks through all seven, citing both the *Sub-commentary* and the *Yogācārabhūmi Commentary*. Skillfully penetrating characteristics means knowing exactly when to apply calming, when to apply uplifting, and when to apply equanimity — calming a restless mind, uplifting a dull one, and simply resting in equanimity when neither fault is present — together with mastery of when and how to enter, abide in, and emerge from concentration at will. Full perfection means having actually attained the root concentrations themselves, the first through fourth dhyānas reached through the seven applications of attention already treated earlier in this treatise. Possessing both aspects together means a concentration accompanied by inner luminosity together with a clear discernment of its object — the *Sub-commentary* here recalling the five marks a meditation teacher instills in a student (weariness with the world, delight in practice, awareness of saṃsāra's dangers, an inwardly cultivated luminosity, and clear discernment of the object itself), luminosity and discernment together being what "both aspects" names. Active operation means calm-abiding and insight now functioning together spontaneously, no longer two separate, alternating practices. Being without support names the concentration joined with root wisdom itself — the three uncontaminated samādhis of emptiness, wishlessness, and signlessness, where no discriminated object, faculty, or verbal designation remains to be depended upon; Master Cháng Bǎi notes a bodhisattva's own version of these three differs subtly from a śrāvaka's, targeting the emptiness of the imagined nature specifically, the signlessness of the dependent nature apart from mere verbal designation, and freedom from any wish to be reborn again into the imagined nature's own grasping. Being well cultivated and refined names the concentration joined with subsequently attained wisdom, used to remove remaining obstacles to concentration itself through the eight liberations, eight bases of mastery, and ten bases of totality already treated at length in this treatise's earlier account of inducing majestic power, from which supernormal powers such as transforming great into small then follow. And the immeasurable states cultivated through the bodhisattva canon names the boundless range of Mahāyāna-specific samādhis — accessible through any object whatsoever, human, heavenly, or otherwise — cultivated through the threefold progression of hearing, reflection, and cultivation applied specifically to the Mahāyāna's own scriptures. Together these seven complete the marks of pure meditative concentration, the fifth of the six pāramitās now finished within this thirteenth sub-topic.

68. Section: Wisdom's Purity Begins — The Middle Way, and the Three Gates of Liberation

If bodhisattvas are free of both the extreme of exaggeration and the extreme of denial, and walk the middle way, this is called wisdom. Because of this wisdom, they truly know the meaning of the gates of liberation, namely the three gates of liberation: emptiness, wishlessness, and signlessness.

Chinese Source

若諸菩薩，遠離增益、損減二邊，行於中道，是名為慧。由此慧故，如實了知解脫門義，謂空、無願、無相三解脫門。

Ru-Yi Translation

If bodhisattvas are free of both the extreme of exaggeration and the extreme of denial, and walk the middle way, this is called wisdom. Because of this wisdom, they truly know the meaning of the gates of liberation, namely the three gates of liberation: emptiness, wishlessness, and signlessness.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi opens wisdom's seven marks — the last and, appropriately, the most doctrinally dense of all six pāramitās' purities — with the middle way itself. The extreme of exaggeration is clinging to the imagined nature, verbally designated, as though it were substantially real; the extreme of denial is denying that the wordless dependent and perfected natures genuinely exist at all. Walking the middle way means holding, correctly, that the imagined nature has no substance and is therefore "not existent," while the dependent nature exists like an illusion and the perfected nature is true emptiness that is also wondrously existent, both therefore "not empty" — a formula this treatise has already unpacked at length in its earlier citation of the Chapter on the Characteristics of All Dharmas. A bodhisattva who denies dependent origination altogether in the name of emptiness has fallen into what this tradition calls a ruinous misreading of emptiness itself, no less mistaken than exaggeration; only the middle way, holding both together, deserves the name wisdom. Founded on this middle way, a bodhisattva's wisdom then truly comprehends the meaning of the three gates of liberation — emptiness (no self and nothing belonging to a self), signlessness (every conditioned characteristic itself being empty and thus without true mark), and wishlessness (no longing for future rebirth into what is thereby seen through) — Master Cháng Bǎi noting the *Yogācārabhūmi*'s own careful distinction between merely abiding in these three as concentrations (common to contaminated and uncontaminated states alike) and abiding in them as actual gates of liberation, which belong to the supramundane path alone, and further distinguishing a bodhisattva's own reading of the three (targeting specifically the imagined nature's emptiness, the dependent nature's signlessness apart from verbal designation, and freedom from wishing for renewed entanglement in the imagined nature) from a śrāvaka's more straightforward application to the five aggregates directly.

69. Section: The Three Natures That Exist, and the Three Kinds of Lacking a Nature of One's Own

They truly know the meaning of the natures that exist, namely the three natures: the imagined, the dependent, and the perfected. They truly know the meaning of the lack of any nature, namely the three kinds of lacking a nature of one's own: the lack of a nature in characteristic, in arising, and in the ultimate.

Chinese Source

如實了知有自性義，謂遍計所執、若依他起、若圓成實三種自性。如實了知無自性義，謂相、生、勝義三種無自性性。

Ru-Yi Translation

They truly know the meaning of the natures that exist, namely the three natures: the imagined, the dependent, and the perfected. They truly know the meaning of the lack of any nature, namely the three kinds of lacking a nature of one's own: the lack of a nature in characteristic, in arising, and in the ultimate.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Wisdom's third and fourth marks recall, in condensed form, the entire teaching this treatise has already devoted a full chapter to citing at length: the three natures and the three kinds of lacking a nature of one's own. The imagined nature is whatever is established through verbal designation and conventional name alone, its apparent substance and distinctions nowhere actually found; the dependent nature is the self-arising of every dharma from the four conditions (causal, dominant, immediately antecedent, and objective-support), the ninety-four dharmas of mind, mental factors, form, and dharmas dissociated from mind that make up conditioned existence, upon which the sixfold suchness is also provisionally established; and the perfected nature is suchness itself — beyond the reach of language and mental activity alike, the very object a noble one's correct wisdom takes as its field across past, present, and future, whose realization brings liberation, freedom from the bondage of both the imagined and dependent natures together with the coarseness of both obstructions, and every excellent quality besides. The three kinds of lacking a nature of one's own restate the same three natures from their negative side: lacking a nature in characteristic is simply the imagined nature itself, having no true characteristic since it is established by name alone rather than by any characteristic of its own; lacking a nature in arising is the dependent nature, arising entirely through the power of conditions rather than of its own accord, and therefore lacking any independent nature of arising; and lacking a nature in the ultimate sense is read two ways — as the dependent nature considered relative to the truly ultimate (impermanent, conditioned dharmas simply not being what ultimate reality itself consists in), and as the perfected nature itself, whose very freedom from verbal designation and from both the arising and characteristic just discussed is precisely what makes it "ultimate" in the fullest sense — the whole framework restated here in miniature as wisdom's third and fourth marks of purity.

70. Section: Conventional Truth — The Five Fields of Learning, Beginning with Inner Wisdom and Logic

They truly know the meaning of conventional truth, namely with regard to the five fields of learning.

Chinese Source

如實了知世俗諦義，謂於五明處。

Ru-Yi Translation

They truly know the meaning of conventional truth, namely with regard to the five fields of learning.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Wisdom's fifth mark turns from ultimate truth to conventional truth, specified as mastery of the five traditional fields of learning: inner wisdom (Buddhist doctrine itself), logic, medicine, linguistics, and craft. Master Cháng Bǎi details inner wisdom through the Hearing-Ground's own fourfold outline (juan 13): the establishment of its subject matter (everything the scriptures, discipline, and treatises address); the establishment of its distinct modes of expression (the names, phrases, and syllables through which both the sentient and physical worlds, and the causes and natures underlying them, are conveyed); the establishment of what comprehends the teaching as a whole (categories that hold the entire doctrine together); and the establishment of what a Buddhist practitioner ought to know (organized numerically, one point through ten) — reducible, per the Bodhisattva-bhūmi's own summary (juan 38), to two essential functions: demonstrating the correct relationship between cause and effect, and demonstrating that no deed, once done, goes without result, and no result arises without a deed behind it. Logic (hetuvidyā) he explains through the Hearing-Ground's own sevenfold analysis (juan 15) of what "investigating meaning" actually involves: the substance of an argument itself, the appropriate setting in which to debate it, the grounds on which it rests (direct perception, inference, and reliable testimony, from which a thesis's supporting reason and analogy are properly constructed), and — as the coming section will continue — several further categories governing how a sound argument is actually built and defended, a discipline a bodhisattva masters not for its own sake but as one of the five fields of learning any teacher who would guide beings skillfully must have genuinely studied.

71. Section: Logic's Remaining Categories, and the Fields of Linguistics, Medicine, and Craft

The fifth mark of pure wisdom, truly knowing conventional truth, continues through logic's remaining categories and the three further fields of learning — linguistics, medicine, and craft.

Chinese Source

如實了知世俗諦義，謂於五明處。（義如前釋，續明因明、聲明、醫方明、工業明。）

Ru-Yi Translation

They truly know the meaning of conventional truth, namely with regard to the five fields of learning — logic, linguistics, medicine, and craft now continuing the account already begun.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi completes logic's sevenfold analysis: proper grounds for a sound argument (direct perception, inference, and reliable testimony, from which a supporting reason and analogy are constructed); rhetorical adornment, the skill to present one's own position and refute another's persuasively while genuinely benefiting both parties; the risk of losing a debate through an argument that only appears sound or only appears to refute the opponent; freedom from affliction in debating at all, meaning one first considers whether a given argument is even worth making and genuinely beneficial before entering into it, rather than debating merely to win; and the great usefulness that follows once all six are mastered together, benefiting both oneself and others at once. He then turns to linguistics (śabdavidyā), outlined through six categories from the Hearing-Ground (juan 15): how words, phrases, and syllables are structured (Sanskrit's root-stems, affixes, and the fourteen vowels and thirty-three consonants that build a syllable); how meaning itself is classified (comparable to a Chinese lexicon like the *Erya*, expanded by later Indian and Tibetan scholars into hundreds of categories); grammatical person and gender; tense; number (singular, dual, and plural); and the grammatical treatises themselves, root texts and their commentaries. Medicine (cikitsāvidyā) follows the four noble truths' own logic: skill in recognizing the marks of illness (suffering), skill in its causes (origination), skill in curing an illness once arisen (cessation), and skill in preventing its return once cured (the path) — Master Cháng Bǎi noting that behind every mundane illness lies the deeper affliction of having a body at all, itself the fruit of saṃsāra's own root cause, ignorance, so that medicine in its fullest sense points toward the very cure the Buddha's teaching provides. Craft (śilpasthānavidyā) covers twelve traditional occupations — farming, trade, government service, accounting and record-keeping, divination, incantation, construction, production, security, reconciliation, refinement, and music — mastery of which, Master Cháng Bǎi notes, equips a bodhisattva to meet beings on whatever practical ground they actually stand, since a teacher who can also cook, organize, lecture, write, and perform ritual chant is simply better equipped to gather and guide an assembly than one who cannot; this fivefold mastery is itself a bodhisattva's own hidden wealth, carried forward life after life though invisible to the eye, closing the fifth of wisdom's seven marks — knowledge of conventional truth in its fullest range.

72. Section: The Sevenfold Suchness, and Accomplishing Practice — Seven Marks of Wisdom Complete

They truly know the meaning of ultimate truth, namely with regard to the sevenfold suchness. Further, because they dwell extensively in a single, unmixed principle free of discrimination and discursive elaboration, because immeasurable comprehensive dharmas serve as their object, and because of insight itself, they are able to skillfully accomplish practice in accordance with the Dharma. These are called the seven marks of pure wisdom.

Chinese Source

如實了知勝義諦義，謂於七真如。又無分別、離諸戲論、純一理趣多所住故，無量總法為所緣故，及毗鉢舍那故，能善成辦法隨法行。是名七種慧清淨相。

Ru-Yi Translation

They truly know the meaning of ultimate truth, namely with regard to the sevenfold suchness. Further, because they dwell extensively in a single, unmixed principle free of discrimination and discursive elaboration, because immeasurable comprehensive dharmas serve as their object, and because of insight itself, they are able to skillfully accomplish practice in accordance with the Dharma. These are called the seven marks of pure wisdom.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Wisdom's sixth mark turns from conventional to ultimate truth, named here as the sevenfold suchness already treated at length in this treatise's own citation of the Chapter Distinguishing Yoga: the suchness of flow (no beginning or end to the selfless, dharma-less turning of birth and death), of characteristics (the twofold emptiness of self and dharmas), of consciousness-only (every object simply what consciousness itself manifests), and the four truths themselves under their alternate names — suffering as the suchness of establishment, origination as the suchness of deviant conduct, cessation as the suchness of purity, and the path as the suchness of correct practice. Where knowing conventional truth required mastery of language and the five fields of learning shared with the wider world, knowing ultimate truth requires instead the non-discriminating wisdom that directly realizes suchness itself, apart from any verbal designation. Wisdom's seventh and final mark, accomplishing practice, describes wisdom actually put to work: dwelling extensively in the single, unmixed principle of true reality (the first of six approaches to principle already introduced earlier in this fascicle), taking the immeasurable totality of dharmas as a single, gathered object — as when the five aggregates are together understood as empty of self and of what belongs to a self — and, through insight thus perfected, actually accomplishing "practice in accordance with the Dharma," which Vasubandhu glosses two ways: the Dharma as nirvāṇa itself and its accompanying practice as the eightfold path, or the Dharma as the scriptures themselves and its accompanying practice as living exactly as they teach. Only wisdom capable of accomplishing this practice directly is fit to purify the other five pāramitās as well, closing all seven marks of wisdom's purity — and with them, all six pāramitās' own sevenfold purity — bringing the thirteenth of the seventeen sub-topics, the purity of the pāramitās taken together and separately, to its complete close.

73. Section: Five Functions — Diligence Without Negligence, and a Cause That Reaches Forward

World-Honored One! What function does each of these five marks perform? Good sons! You should know that these marks have five functions. Namely: because bodhisattvas are free of attachment, in this very life, with regard to the pāramitās they cultivate, they constantly and earnestly apply diligent effort, free of negligence. Because they are free of lingering regard, they gather in the cause of freedom from negligence in the future as well.

Chinese Source

世尊！如是五相，各有何業？善男子！當知彼相有五種業。謂諸菩薩無染著故，於現法中，於所修習波羅蜜多，恆常殷重勤修加行，無有放逸。無顧戀故，攝受當來不放逸因。

Ru-Yi Translation

World-Honored One! What function does each of these five marks perform? Good sons! You should know that these marks have five functions. Namely: because bodhisattvas are free of attachment, in this very life, with regard to the pāramitās they cultivate, they constantly and earnestly apply diligent effort, free of negligence. Because they are free of lingering regard, they gather in the cause of freedom from negligence in the future as well.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Avalokiteśvara opens the fourteenth of the seventeen sub-topics by asking what each of the five defining marks of a genuine pāramitā actually accomplishes. Freedom from attachment functions, in this very life, to keep a bodhisattva earnestly, uninterruptedly applying effort to the pāramitās — since seeing no real merit in what opposes them is precisely what removes the temptation to slacken. Freedom from lingering regard functions to secure the very same freedom from negligence into future lives as well: a bodhisattva who practices without craving any particular fruit or repayment carries that same unattached diligence forward, life after life, rather than losing momentum once immediate reward is absent.

74. Section: Utmost Perfection, Swift Completion, and Inexhaustible Fruit All the Way to Buddhahood

Because they are free of fault, they are able to correctly cultivate the pāramitās to the point of utmost perfection, utmost purity, and utmost brightness. Because they are free of discrimination, the pāramitā of skillful means is swiftly brought to completion. Because of correct dedication, the pāramitās and their agreeable ripened fruits, in every place of rebirth, become inexhaustible, continuing all the way to unsurpassed, perfect, complete enlightenment.

Chinese Source

無罪過故，能正修習極善圓滿、極善清淨、極善鮮白波羅蜜多。無分別故，方便善巧波羅蜜多速得圓滿。正迴向故，一切生處波羅蜜多，及彼可愛諸果異熟皆得無盡，乃至無上正等菩提。

Ru-Yi Translation

Because they are free of fault, they are able to correctly cultivate the pāramitās to the point of utmost perfection, utmost purity, and utmost brightness. Because they are free of discrimination, the pāramitā of skillful means is swiftly brought to completion. Because of correct dedication, the pāramitās and their agreeable ripened fruits, in every place of rebirth, become inexhaustible, continuing all the way to unsurpassed, perfect, complete enlightenment.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Freedom from fault functions to bring the pāramitās to utmost perfection (thoroughly complete), utmost purity (no affliction remaining, either in oneself or provoked in the one being helped), and utmost brightness (this purity sustained continuously rather than achieved only once) — precisely because a practice free of defiling application and of conduct lacking skillful means resolves both a being's material need and their underlying confusion together. Freedom from discrimination functions to bring the pāramitā of skillful means specifically to swift completion: understanding emptiness thoroughly enough — the imagined nature simply not existing, the dependent nature arising and ceasing by conditions rather than fixed rules — frees a bodhisattva to adapt method to circumstance the way anyone who has truly grasped an underlying principle can adjust to unfamiliar situations with ease, where someone who only memorizes fixed procedures cannot; Master Cháng Bǎi observes that this kind of flexible skillfulness has less to do with how much one has studied than with how deeply one has actually understood emptiness itself, since excessive attachment to the letter of what is learned can just as easily obstruct it. And correct dedication functions to make the pāramitās and their agreeable fruit inexhaustible in whatever place of rebirth a bodhisattva takes, all the way to buddhahood itself — since the scope of dedication determines the scope of the resulting merit: a bodhisattva who dedicates the fruit of tireless cultivation toward some limited worldly goal, the Buddha elsewhere warns, has traded gold for dung, while dedication toward unsurpassed enlightenment, itself without limit, yields fruit that is likewise without limit. With all five functions now explained, the fourteenth of the seventeen sub-topics closes in full.

75. Section: Which Is Most Vast? — Freedom from Attachment, Regard, and Correct Dedication

World-Honored One! Among the pāramitās just described, which is most vast? Which is free of defilement? Which is most brilliant? Which is unshakeable? And which is most pure? Good sons! The nature of being free of attachment, the nature of being free of lingering regard, and the nature of correct dedication are what is most vast.

Chinese Source

世尊！如是所說波羅蜜多，何者最廣大？何者無染汙？何者最明盛？何者不可動？何者最清淨？善男子！無染著性、無顧戀性、正迴向性，最為廣大。

Ru-Yi Translation

World-Honored One! Among the pāramitās just described, which is most vast? Which is free of defilement? Which is most brilliant? Which is unshakeable? And which is most pure? Good sons! The nature of being free of attachment, the nature of being free of lingering regard, and the nature of correct dedication are what is most vast.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Avalokiteśvara opens the fifteenth of the seventeen sub-topics by asking the Buddha to rank the same five defining marks against five further superlatives — vastness, freedom from defilement, brilliance, unshakeability, and utmost purity — beginning with what is most vast. Freedom from attachment is vast because it keeps effort flowing uninterrupted toward every pāramitā without exception; freedom from lingering regard is vast because it detaches merit from any single, limited expectation of return, leaving the resulting virtue free to expand without bound; and correct dedication is vast because its object — unsurpassed enlightenment itself — has no limit at all, so that whatever is dedicated toward it likewise becomes limitless. Together these three, rather than freedom from fault or freedom from discrimination, are what the Buddha names most vast, the first of five superlatives the coming sections will continue to distinguish.

76. Section: Free of Defilement, Most Brilliant, Unshakeable, and Most Pure — Five Superlatives Complete

The nature of being free of fault and the nature of being free of discrimination are what is free of defilement. What is accomplished through discerning reflection is what is most brilliant. What has entered the ground of non-regression is called unshakeable. What is contained within the ten grounds or the Buddha ground is called most pure.

Chinese Source

無罪過性、無分別性，無有染汙。思擇所作，最為明盛。已入無退轉法地者，名不可動。若十地攝、佛地攝者，名最清淨。

Ru-Yi Translation

The nature of being free of fault and the nature of being free of discrimination are what is free of defilement. What is accomplished through discerning reflection is what is most brilliant. What has entered the ground of non-regression is called unshakeable. What is contained within the ten grounds or the Buddha ground is called most pure.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi closes the remaining four superlatives briskly. Freedom from defilement belongs to freedom from fault and freedom from discrimination together, since avoiding both defiling application and conduct lacking skillful means, while also releasing any clinging to the pāramitās' own verbal characteristics, is what actually keeps affliction from tainting the practice. Brilliance belongs to whatever is accomplished through genuinely discerning reflection — a bodhisattva of strong conviction in the Mahāyāna and deep insight into beings' own capacities makes the best possible judgment in any situation, and it is this discernment itself that makes the resulting pāramitā brilliant. Unshakeability belongs to a bodhisattva who has already entered the ground of non-regression, traditionally the eighth ground onward, where the pāramitās can no longer be disturbed by anything whatsoever. And utmost purity belongs to what is contained within the tenth ground or the Buddha ground alone — the tenth ground's own perfected Dharma-body, and the Buddha ground's fully accomplished one, the Buddha's purity exceeding even the tenth ground's own. With all five superlatives — vastness, freedom from defilement, brilliance, unshakeability, and utmost purity — now distinguished, the fifteenth of the seventeen sub-topics distinguishing the pāramitās closes in full.

77. Section: Inexhaustible Fruit and Inexhaustible Practice — Each Depending on the Other Without Interruption

World-Honored One! For what reason are the agreeable fruits and ripened results a bodhisattva attains through the pāramitās forever inexhaustible, and the pāramitās themselves likewise inexhaustible? Good sons! Because their arising and their cultivation depend on one another in turn, without interruption.

Chinese Source

世尊！何因緣故，菩薩所得波羅蜜多諸可愛果，及諸異熟常無有盡，波羅蜜多亦無有盡？善男子！展轉相依，生起、修習無間斷故。

Ru-Yi Translation

World-Honored One! For what reason are the agreeable fruits and ripened results a bodhisattva attains through the pāramitās forever inexhaustible, and the pāramitās themselves likewise inexhaustible? Good sons! Because their arising and their cultivation depend on one another in turn, without interruption.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Avalokiteśvara opens the sixteenth of the seventeen sub-topics by asking why a bodhisattva's favorable fruit and continued practice never run dry. Master Cháng Bǎi cites the *Sub-commentary*'s gloss on the eight kinds of ripened excellence this fruit involves (juan 36) — full lifespan, fine appearance, noble lineage, personal autonomy, credible speech, great influence, human dignity, and physical strength, each arising from a specific virtuous cause: refraining from harming others yields a long life, offering light and fine clothing yields a fine appearance, humility toward one's own kind yields noble birth, generosity to the needy yields autonomy and following, truthful and gentle speech yields credibility, reverence toward the Three Jewels and one's elders yields influence, freeing others from an unwanted or endangered body yields dignity, and material support offered to sustain others' own strength yields strength in turn — a well-favored rebirth that itself makes tireless further practice of the pāramitās easier to sustain. The two, fruit and practice, thus feed one another continuously: abiding in the favorable fruit already attained, a bodhisattva practices the pāramitās all the more, and that practice in turn yields still more favorable fruit to come — Master Cháng Bǎi citing the Bodhisattva-bhūmi's own striking image of most beings simply squandering their beginningless wandering through birth and death without any real benefit gained, where a bodhisattva alone turns that same wandering into something genuinely meaningful, each life more excellent than the last rather than merely repeating the same emptiness — closing the sixteenth sub-topic in full.

78. Section: Five Reasons Bodhisattvas Love the Perfections Themselves, Not Their Fruit

World-Honored One! For what reason do bodhisattvas place deep faith in and delight in the pāramitās themselves, rather than in the agreeable fruits and ripened results the pāramitās produce? Good sons! For five reasons: first, because the pāramitās are the most superior cause of joy; second, because the pāramitās are the cause of ultimately benefiting both oneself and others; third, because the pāramitās are the cause of those very agreeable fruits and ripened results in future lives; fourth, because the pāramitās are never the basis for any defilement; and fifth, because the pāramitās are never subject to ultimate decay.

Chinese Source

世尊！何因緣故，是諸菩薩深信愛樂波羅蜜多，非於如是波羅蜜多所得可愛諸果異熟？善男子！五因緣故。一者、波羅蜜多是最增上喜樂因故；二者、波羅蜜多是其究竟饒益一切自他因故；三者、波羅蜜多是當來世彼可愛果異熟因故；四者、波羅蜜多非諸雜染所依事故；五者、波羅蜜多非是畢竟變壞法故。

Ru-Yi Translation

World-Honored One! For what reason do bodhisattvas place deep faith in and delight in the pāramitās themselves, rather than in the agreeable fruits and ripened results the pāramitās produce? Good sons! For five reasons: first, because the pāramitās are the most superior cause of joy; second, because the pāramitās are the cause of ultimately benefiting both oneself and others; third, because the pāramitās are the cause of those very agreeable fruits and ripened results in future lives; fourth, because the pāramitās are never the basis for any defilement; and fifth, because the pāramitās are never subject to ultimate decay.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Avalokiteśvara opens the seventeenth and final sub-topic distinguishing the pāramitās with a question already anticipated by the fifth of the earlier five defining marks, freedom from lingering regard: why does a bodhisattva genuinely love the pāramitās themselves rather than what they produce? First, the pāramitās are the most superior cause of joy, since they alone carry a bodhisattva into the first ground's own utmost joy, tasting the Dharma's flavor directly — a bodhisattva fears the cause, Master Cháng Bǎi notes, exactly where an ordinary being fears only the fruit already ripened. Second, they ultimately benefit both self and other, the first three pāramitās perfecting benefit to others and the last three perfecting benefit to oneself, together completing both at once. Third, they are themselves the very cause of the favorable fruit in future lives already discussed, so that valuing the fruit without valuing its cause would be senseless. Fourth, far from being the basis for any defilement, each pāramitā actively counteracts a specific affliction — giving overcoming stinginess, discipline overcoming transgression, patience overcoming resentment, diligence overcoming laziness, concentration overcoming distraction, and wisdom overcoming wrong views — so that practicing them is itself already liberating, independent of whatever fruit later follows. And fifth, unlike an ordinary ripened fruit, which is simply consumed and then gone, the pāramitās are never subject to ultimate decay: cultivated to completion, they culminate in the Mahāyāna's own unlocated nirvāṇa, mastery over every dharma that continues, without exhaustion, benefiting beings for as long as time itself endures. Together these five reasons explain why a bodhisattva's devotion attaches to the practice itself rather than to any of its passing rewards, closing the seventeenth and final sub-topic of this extended examination.

79. Section: Four Kinds of Supreme Majestic Power in Every Perfection

World-Honored One! How many kinds of supreme majestic power does each of these pāramitās have? Good sons! You should know that each of the pāramitās has four kinds of supreme majestic power: first, in correctly practicing this pāramitā, one is able to relinquish stinginess, transgression, resentment, laziness, distraction, and wrong views, each counteracted in turn; second, in correctly practicing it, it becomes a genuine provision for unsurpassed, perfect, complete enlightenment; third, in correctly practicing it, in this very life one is able to gather oneself in and benefit sentient beings; and fourth, in correctly practicing it, in future lives one attains vast and inexhaustible agreeable fruits and ripened results.

Chinese Source

世尊！如是一切波羅蜜多，各有幾種最勝威德？善男子！當知一切波羅蜜多，各有四種最勝威德。一者、於此波羅蜜多正修行時，能捨慳吝、犯戒、心憤、懈怠、散亂、見趣所治；二者、於此正修行時，能為無上正等菩提真實資糧；三者、於此正修行時，於現法中，能自攝受、饒益有情；四者、於此正修行時，於未來世能得廣大無盡可愛諸果異熟。

Ru-Yi Translation

World-Honored One! How many kinds of supreme majestic power does each of these pāramitās have? Good sons! You should know that each of the pāramitās has four kinds of supreme majestic power: first, in correctly practicing this pāramitā, one is able to relinquish stinginess, transgression, resentment, laziness, distraction, and wrong views, each counteracted in turn; second, in correctly practicing it, it becomes a genuine provision for unsurpassed, perfect, complete enlightenment; third, in correctly practicing it, in this very life one is able to gather oneself in and benefit sentient beings; and fourth, in correctly practicing it, in future lives one attains vast and inexhaustible agreeable fruits and ripened results.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

This further question on majestic power gathers together threads already established across the preceding sub-topics into a single fourfold summary applying to every pāramitā alike: first, each pāramitā counteracts its own specific affliction, exactly as the previous section detailed; second, each serves as a genuine provision toward unsurpassed enlightenment, part of the merit and wisdom already discussed at length; third, each benefits both practitioner and other beings simultaneously in this very life; and fourth, each yields vast, inexhaustible favorable fruit in lives to come.
