Master Cháng Bǎi treats this section as a deliberate summary rather than new material — drawing together the counteracting function, the provisioning function, the dual-benefit function, and the fruit-bearing function already distinguished piece by piece across the preceding several sub-topics into one compact, fourfold statement of what makes the pāramitās majestic altogether.

80. Section: Cause, Fruit, and Benefit — The Final Sub-Topic Distinguishing the Pāramitās Opens

World-Honored One! What is the cause of all these pāramitās? What is their fruit? And what benefit do they bring?

Chinese Source

世尊！如是一切波羅蜜多，何因？何果？有何義利？

Ru-Yi Translation

World-Honored One! What is the cause of all these pāramitās? What is their fruit? And what benefit do they bring?

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With the seventeen numbered sub-topics distinguishing the pāramitās now essentially complete, Avalokiteśvara raises one further clarifying question — asking after their cause, fruit, and benefit together — that will draw the entire seventh section of the ground-pāramitā chapter, on the matters a bodhisattva must learn, to its proper close. Master Cháng Bǎi treats this as the final consolidating question before the chapter turns to its eighth and last section: the hidden intent behind the Buddha teaching only a single vehicle rather than three.

81. Section: Great Compassion the Cause, Benefiting Beings the Fruit, Buddhahood the Great Benefit

Good sons! You should know that great compassion is the cause of all the pāramitās; their fruit is the wondrous, agreeable ripened results that benefit every sentient being; and their great benefit is the perfected, unsurpassed, vast enlightenment.

Chinese Source

善男子！當知一切波羅蜜多，大悲為因，微妙可愛諸果異熟饒益一切有情為果，圓滿無上廣大菩提為大義利。

Ru-Yi Translation

Good sons! You should know that great compassion is the cause of all the pāramitās; their fruit is the wondrous, agreeable ripened results that benefit every sentient being; and their great benefit is the perfected, unsurpassed, vast enlightenment.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

The Buddha's answer states the entire Mahāyāna path's own foundational formula in miniature: the bodhi-mind as root, great compassion in the lead, and skillful means grounded in non-attainment — echoing the very same formula this treatise's parallel Avataṃsaka tradition teaches. Great compassion is the cause from which every pāramitā springs; the wondrous, agreeable ripened results already discussed throughout this section — benefiting oneself and every other being alike — are their fruit; and unsurpassed, perfect, complete enlightenment, buddhahood itself, is their ultimate benefit, the final destination toward which the entire cause-and-fruit structure has been building since this fascicle's very first question.

82. Section: Why Are Beings Still Poor? — It Is Their Own Karma, Not the Bodhisattva's Fault

World-Honored One! If bodhisattvas possess every kind of inexhaustible wealth and have accomplished great compassion, for what reason are beings in the world still found to be destitute? Good sons! This is the fault of these very beings' own karma. Were it otherwise — since bodhisattvas constantly hold in mind the wish to benefit others, and constantly possess inexhaustible wealth — if beings had no unwholesome karma of their own to obstruct them, how could there be any poverty or suffering to be found in the world at all?

Chinese Source

世尊！若諸菩薩具足一切無盡財寶，成就大悲，何緣世間現有眾生貧窮可得？善男子！是諸眾生自業過失。若不爾者，菩薩常懷饒益他心，又常具足無盡財寶，若諸眾生無自惡業能為障礙，何有世間貧苦可得。

Ru-Yi Translation

World-Honored One! If bodhisattvas possess every kind of inexhaustible wealth and have accomplished great compassion, for what reason are beings in the world still found to be destitute? Good sons! This is the fault of these very beings' own karma. Were it otherwise — since bodhisattvas constantly hold in mind the wish to benefit others, and constantly possess inexhaustible wealth — if beings had no unwholesome karma of their own to obstruct them, how could there be any poverty or suffering to be found in the world at all?

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Avalokiteśvara raises the obvious objection: if bodhisattvas are everywhere, wealthy beyond measure, and moved by boundless compassion, why does poverty still exist at all? The Buddha's answer is direct — it is beings' own karmic obstruction, not any failure of compassion or generosity on the bodhisattva's part — and Master Cháng Bǎi unpacks this into five specific reasons a bodhisattva may, quite deliberately, withhold material giving from a particular being: karmic obstruction itself simply prevents the being from ever encountering the bodhisattva or receiving the gift at all; giving might obstruct the arising of a wholesome quality that poverty itself currently sustains, such as a poor person's freedom from arrogance; giving might remove the very hardship that is currently cultivating renunciation, since ease can just as easily entrench attachment to worldly pleasure; giving might instead fund the accumulation of further unwholesome deeds, wealth in the wrong hands financing further harm; and giving might simply arm a being to oppress others once resources and influence are in hand. He cites a well-known verse — a mother nurses her infant tirelessly for a full month, yet if the infant's own throat stays closed, what can the mother possibly do? — the mother standing for the bodhisattva, the infant for the being whose own karma keeps the gift from ever truly reaching them. He also recounts the story, from the āgama literature, of the monk Losaka-tissa, who went six days without receiving alms and, facing death on the seventh, was helped in turn by a compassionate fellow monk, then by Maudgalyāyana using his own supernormal power, and finally by Śāriputra himself — yet the food turned to mud in Maudgalyāyana's hands, and even when Śāriputra's own offering reached Losaka-tissa's very lips intact, his jaw simply would not open to receive it, so severe was his own karmic obstruction that death could not be averted by any outside power whatsoever — a stark illustration that no quantity of generosity, however sincere, can override a being's own karma.

83. Section: The Hungry Ghost and the Dried-Up Ocean — Closing the Door of Cause and Fruit

It is like a hungry ghost, its body afflicted by great heat and thirst, who sees the great ocean itself entirely dried up — this is no fault of the ocean, but the fault of that very hungry ghost's own karma. In just the same way, the wealth a bodhisattva gives is like the great ocean itself, without any fault; it is these very beings' own karma that is at fault, exactly as it is a hungry ghost's own unwholesome karmic power that leaves it without any result.

Chinese Source

譬如餓鬼，為大熱渴逼迫其身，見大海水悉皆涸竭，非大海過，是諸餓鬼自業過耳。如是菩薩所施財寶，猶如大海，無有過失，是諸眾生自業過耳，猶如餓鬼自惡業力，令無有果。

Ru-Yi Translation

It is like a hungry ghost, its body afflicted by great heat and thirst, who sees the great ocean itself entirely dried up — this is no fault of the ocean, but the fault of that very hungry ghost's own karma. In just the same way, the wealth a bodhisattva gives is like the great ocean itself, without any fault; it is these very beings' own karma that is at fault, exactly as it is a hungry ghost's own unwholesome karmic power that leaves it without any result.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi cites the earlier Ground of Application's own threefold typology of a hungry ghost's obstruction (juan 4): obstruction from without, where guardian spirits or demons drive the ghost away from food before it can ever be reached; obstruction from within, where the ghost's own throat, narrow as a needle's eye, cannot swallow even food it does manage to obtain; and no obstruction at all in the ordinary sense, yet food that reaches the ghost's very mouth transforms into flame or filth the instant it is touched — the same class of ghost that, tormented by heat and thirst, gazes upon the vast ocean only to see it appear entirely dried up, an illusion produced by the ghost's own karmic vision rather than any actual change in the ocean itself. He adds the *Yogācārabhūmi Commentary*'s own parallel images: thunder crashes, yet the deaf hear nothing, and it is no fault of the thunder; the sun and moon blaze with light, yet the blind see nothing, and it is no fault of the sun and moon — in every case, the obstruction lies entirely with the perceiver, never with what is perceived. The Buddha's closing application makes the point explicit: a bodhisattva's wealth to give is genuinely as vast and inexhaustible as the ocean itself, entirely without fault; whatever poverty persists in the world belongs entirely to the karmic obstruction of the beings experiencing it, exactly as the hungry ghost's own unwholesome karma, and not any lack in the ocean, is what leaves it perpetually unable to drink. With this vivid closing image, the extended sub-topic on the cause, fruit, and benefit of the pāramitās comes to its complete close.

84. Section: Wisdom Alone Apprehends the Lack of a Nature — Yet Never by Grasping It as an Object

World-Honored One! By which pāramitā do bodhisattvas apprehend the lack of any nature of their own in all dharmas? Good sons! It is by the pāramitā of wisdom that one is able to apprehend the lack of any nature of their own in all dharmas. World-Honored One! If the pāramitā of wisdom is able to apprehend the lack of any nature of their own in all dharmas, why does it not instead apprehend that they have a nature of their own? Good sons! I have never taught that the lack of a nature of one's own apprehends the lack of a nature of one's own. Yet since the lack of any nature of one's own is realized inwardly, apart from all words and letters, and cannot be taught without recourse to verbal expression, I therefore say that the pāramitā of wisdom is able to apprehend the lack of any nature of their own in all dharmas.

Chinese Source

世尊！菩薩以何等波羅蜜多取一切法無自性性？善男子！以般若波羅蜜多能取諸法無自性性。世尊！若般若波羅蜜多能取諸法無自性性，何故不取有自性性？善男子！我終不說以無自性性取無自性性，然無自性性離諸文字自內所證，不可捨於言說文字而能宣說，是故我說般若波羅蜜多能取諸法無自性性。

Ru-Yi Translation

World-Honored One! By which pāramitā do bodhisattvas apprehend the lack of any nature of their own in all dharmas? Good sons! It is by the pāramitā of wisdom that one is able to apprehend the lack of any nature of their own in all dharmas. World-Honored One! If the pāramitā of wisdom is able to apprehend the lack of any nature of their own in all dharmas, why does it not instead apprehend that they have a nature of their own? Good sons! I have never taught that the lack of a nature of one's own apprehends the lack of a nature of one's own. Yet since the lack of any nature of one's own is realized inwardly, apart from all words and letters, and cannot be taught without recourse to verbal expression, I therefore say that the pāramitā of wisdom is able to apprehend the lack of any nature of their own in all dharmas.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Avalokiteśvara asks which pāramitā actually apprehends the three kinds of lacking a nature of one's own already treated in wisdom's own fourth mark of purity, and the Buddha answers plainly: wisdom alone. But Avalokiteśvara immediately presses the natural follow-up — if wisdom can be said to "apprehend" this lack of nature, does that not already smuggle in the very subject-object structure the teaching is meant to dissolve, apprehending it as though it had some nature after all? The Buddha's answer, per the *Sub-commentary*'s careful gloss, insists this is never literally what happens: wisdom itself is, in ultimate truth, equally without any nature of its own, equally beyond words, so that there is, strictly speaking, no real grasper and no real grasped object involved at all — wisdom does not, in truth, "take" anything as an object any more than emptiness could take emptiness as an object. Yet since this direct, wordless inward realization can never actually be taught to another being without recourse to some verbal expedient, the Buddha says, only for the sake of communicating it at all, that wisdom "apprehends" the lack of a nature — a deliberate, provisional manner of speaking rather than a literal claim. The *Sub-commentary* draws the logical conclusion explicit: were the Buddha instead to grant that wisdom apprehends dharmas as genuinely possessing a nature of their own, this would already contradict what wisdom itself is, since any such apprehension would necessarily involve discrimination, and discrimination is precisely what non-discriminating wisdom, by definition, is free of — hence the deliberate refusal to speak of wisdom "taking" anything as an object in any but this carefully qualified, provisional sense.

85. Section: Three Grades — The Pāramitā, the Near-Pāramitā, and the Great Pāramitā

World-Honored One! As the Buddha has taught, there are the pāramitā, the near-pāramitā, and the great pāramitā. What is the pāramitā? What is the near-pāramitā? And what is the great pāramitā? Good sons! If bodhisattvas, over an immeasurable span of time cultivating giving and the rest, accomplish wholesome dharmas, yet affliction still currently arises and cannot yet be subdued, so that they are instead subdued by it — this, occurring during the weak and middling grades of resolute conviction on the ground of resolute practice, is called the pāramitā.

Chinese Source

世尊！如佛所說波羅蜜多、近波羅蜜多、大波羅蜜多，云何波羅蜜多？云何近波羅蜜多？云何大波羅蜜多？善男子！若諸菩薩，經無量時修行施等，成就善法，而諸煩惱猶故現行，未能制伏，然為彼伏。謂於勝解行地軟中勝解轉時，是名波羅蜜多。

Ru-Yi Translation

World-Honored One! As the Buddha has taught, there are the pāramitā, the near-pāramitā, and the great pāramitā. What is the pāramitā? What is the near-pāramitā? And what is the great pāramitā? Good sons! If bodhisattvas, over an immeasurable span of time cultivating giving and the rest, accomplish wholesome dharmas, yet affliction still currently arises and cannot yet be subdued, so that they are instead subdued by it — this, occurring during the weak and middling grades of resolute conviction on the ground of resolute practice, is called the pāramitā.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Avalokiteśvara opens a fresh line of questioning — the pāramitā graded by three levels of increasing mastery over affliction — beginning with the plain "pāramitā" itself: a bodhisattva still on the ground of resolute practice, in the weak or middling grade of resolute conviction (the strongest grade, on the very threshold of the first ground, is deliberately excluded, since affliction there has already grown too thin to fit this description), who cultivates giving and the rest across countless lifetimes yet still finds affliction currently arising uncontrolled, more often subduing the practitioner than being subdued by him. This is simply where practice actually begins for most bodhisattvas — genuine effort, genuine accumulation of merit, but affliction still very much the upper hand.

86. Section: Near-Pāramitā from the First Ground, Great Pāramitā from the Eighth

Further, over an immeasurable span of time again cultivating giving and the rest, gradually accomplishing wholesome dharmas of ever-increasing excellence, affliction still currently arises, yet is now able to be subdued rather than subduing them — this, from the first ground onward, is called the near-pāramitā. Further, over an immeasurable span of time again cultivating giving and the rest, accomplishing wholesome dharmas of ever more excellent degree, no affliction whatsoever any longer currently arises — this, from the eighth ground onward, is called the great pāramitā.

Chinese Source

復於無量時修行施等，漸復增上成就善法，而諸煩惱猶故現行，然能制伏，非彼所伏。謂從初地已上，是名近波羅蜜多。復於無量時修行施等，轉復增上成就善法，一切煩惱皆不現行。謂從八地已上，是名大波羅蜜多。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, over an immeasurable span of time again cultivating giving and the rest, gradually accomplishing wholesome dharmas of ever-increasing excellence, affliction still currently arises, yet is now able to be subdued rather than subduing them — this, from the first ground onward, is called the near-pāramitā. Further, over an immeasurable span of time again cultivating giving and the rest, accomplishing wholesome dharmas of ever more excellent degree, no affliction whatsoever any longer currently arises — this, from the eighth ground onward, is called the great pāramitā.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi maps these three grades directly onto the three full incalculable eons of bodhisattva practice: the plain pāramitā spans the whole first incalculable eon, the ground of resolute practice where affliction still gets the better of the practitioner; the near-pāramitā spans the second incalculable eon, from the first ground through the seventh, where a noble one now genuinely masters affliction as it arises rather than being mastered by it, drawing steadily nearer to buddhahood; and the great pāramitā spans the third and final incalculable eon, from the eighth ground onward, where the eighth ground's signless, effortless abiding means no affliction whatsoever arises any longer even in its subtlest, most currently active form — the remaining task from here being solely the gradual exhaustion of the cognitive obstruction's innate seeds, culminating at the diamond-like concentration. Judged purely by the strength of the counteracting wisdom involved, then, the pāramitā belongs to weak and middling resolute conviction, the near-pāramitā to the first through seventh grounds, and the great pāramitā to the eighth ground and beyond — three names for three successive stages of how completely a bodhisattva has actually mastered affliction, closing the sub-topic on what each of the three grades counteracts.

87. Section: Dormant Afflictive Tendencies — Three Kinds Across the Grounds

World-Honored One! Within these grounds, how many kinds of dormant afflictive tendency are there? Good sons! In brief, there are three.

Chinese Source

世尊！此諸地中，煩惱隨眠可有幾種？善男子！略有三種。

Ru-Yi Translation

World-Honored One! Within these grounds, how many kinds of dormant afflictive tendency are there? Good sons! In brief, there are three.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Having distinguished the three grades of pāramitā by how thoroughly affliction is mastered, Avalokiteśvara now turns to a more detailed examination of the dormant afflictive tendencies themselves — the seeds underlying whatever still currently arises at each stage — opening a further, more granular sub-topic that the coming section will unpack into its own threefold classification.

88. Section: Three Dormant Tendencies — Harming the Companion, Weakened, and Subtle

First, the dormant tendency that harms its companion, belonging to the first five grounds. Why is this so? Good sons! Non-innate afflictions currently arising serve as companions to the current arising of innate affliction; at this stage they are permanently no more — hence it is called the dormant tendency that harms its companion. Second, the weakened dormant tendency, belonging to the sixth and seventh grounds, arising only subtly and currently, which, once subdued through cultivation, no longer arises at all. Third, the subtle dormant tendency, belonging to the eighth ground and above. From here onward, no affliction whatsoever arises any longer, only the cognitive obstruction remaining as its basis.

Chinese Source

一者、害伴隨眠，謂於前五地。何以故？善男子！諸不俱生現行煩惱，是俱生煩惱現行助伴，彼於爾時永無復有，是故說名害伴隨眠。二者、羸劣隨眠，謂於第六、第七地中，微細現行，若修所伏，不現行故。三者、微細隨眠，謂於第八地已上。從此已去，一切煩惱不復現行，唯有所知障為依止故。

Ru-Yi Translation

First, the dormant tendency that harms its companion, belonging to the first five grounds. Why is this so? Good sons! Non-innate afflictions currently arising serve as companions to the current arising of innate affliction; at this stage they are permanently no more — hence it is called the dormant tendency that harms its companion. Second, the weakened dormant tendency, belonging to the sixth and seventh grounds, arising only subtly and currently, which, once subdued through cultivation, no longer arises at all. Third, the subtle dormant tendency, belonging to the eighth ground and above. From here onward, no affliction whatsoever arises any longer, only the cognitive obstruction remaining as its basis.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi cites the *Mahāyāna Compendium of the Forest of Meanings* (juan 1) to clarify this threefold classification. The dormant tendency that harms its companion covers the non-innate, sixth-consciousness-associated craving and related afflictions that accompany the innate view of self and what belongs to a self — companions in the sense that they arise alongside that innate view and are "harmed," permanently ceasing, once the fourth ground's cultivation of the four foundations of mindfulness (contemplating the body's impurity, and ultimately the selflessness of all dharmas) permanently subdues that innate view by the fifth ground. The weakened dormant tendency covers the subtler, sixth-consciousness-associated afflictions and cognitive obstruction still arising on the sixth and seventh grounds — subtler and harder to sever than the first kind, since severing the innate view of self does not automatically remove them too, hence "weakened" rather than eliminated outright; they arise currently only when the counteracting path is not actively engaged, and cease to arise the moment it is. The subtle dormant tendency covers what remains from the eighth ground onward: by this point the afflictive obstruction no longer arises in current activity at all, leaving only the seventh-consciousness-associated cognitive obstruction — the residual habitual trace remaining after the first two kinds are severed, the most subtle of the three, finished only at the eighth ground and beyond.

89. Section: Coarseness Like Skin, Flesh, and Marrow

World-Honored One! These dormant tendencies are revealed through the severing of how many kinds of coarseness? Good sons! Simply through two. Namely: severing the coarseness that is like skin reveals the first two of these; and severing the coarseness that is like flesh reveals the third. As for severing the coarseness that is like marrow, I say that permanent freedom from every dormant tendency whatsoever is found at the Buddha ground.

Chinese Source

世尊！此諸隨眠，幾種麤重斷所顯示？善男子！但由二種。謂由在皮麤重斷故，顯彼初二；復由在膚麤重斷故，顯彼第三。若在於骨麤重斷者，我說永離一切隨眠，位在佛地。

Ru-Yi Translation

World-Honored One! These dormant tendencies are revealed through the severing of how many kinds of coarseness? Good sons! Simply through two. Namely: severing the coarseness that is like skin reveals the first two of these; and severing the coarseness that is like flesh reveals the third. As for severing the coarseness that is like marrow, I say that permanent freedom from every dormant tendency whatsoever is found at the Buddha ground.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi explains that coarseness (the dormant seeds themselves, described by their function) and dormant tendency (the same seeds described by their own nature) name the same underlying reality from two angles, both residing as unripened potential within the ālaya or maturation consciousness. The bodhisattva-ground stage involves only two of the traditional three degrees of coarseness: severing what is "like skin" (the surface-level coarseness cut during the second incalculable eon) reveals both the dormant tendency that harms its companion and the weakened dormant tendency together; severing what is "like flesh" (cut from the eighth ground, the beginning of the third incalculable eon) reveals the subtle dormant tendency. Only at the Buddha ground is the third and final degree, coarseness "like marrow" — the deepest, most deeply embedded remainder — finally severed, bringing permanent freedom from every dormant tendency whatsoever, afflictive and cognitive obstruction both fully exhausted.

90. Section: Three Great Uncountable Eons — A Single Instant, and the Longest Measure of Time

World-Honored One! Across how many uncountable eons is such coarseness severed? Good sons! Across three great uncountable eons, or immeasurable eons — that is to say, years, months, half-months, day and night, an hour, half an hour, a moment, an instant, and a single instant of time — measured by any of these, the eons cannot be counted.

Chinese Source

世尊！經幾不可數劫，能斷如是麤重？善男子！經於三大不可數劫，或無量劫。所謂年、月、半月、晝夜、一時、半時、須臾、瞬息、剎那量劫不可數故。

Ru-Yi Translation

World-Honored One! Across how many uncountable eons is such coarseness severed? Good sons! Across three great uncountable eons, or immeasurable eons — that is to say, years, months, half-months, day and night, an hour, half an hour, a moment, an instant, and a single instant of time — measured by any of these, the eons cannot be counted.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi walks through the full traditional cascade of time-units this passage invokes, from the briefest to the longest: a single instant (kṣaṇa), roughly 0.013 seconds, the smallest indivisible unit of time recognized in the *Abhidharmakośa*; one hundred twenty instants make a tatkṣaṇa, about 1.6 seconds; sixty tatkṣaṇas make a lava; thirty lavas make a muhūrta, working out to roughly forty-eight minutes — the same span elsewhere called a moment (muhūrta); and thirty muhūrtas make a full day and night. From there, half-months, months, and years build up further, but even this entire ladder of units, run all the way to its top, cannot number the length of even a single great eon (kalpa) — itself defined, per the *Mahāvibhāṣā* (juan 135), as the full cycle of a world's formation, abiding, destruction, and emptiness, subdivided into twenty intermediate eons each for formation and its abiding, and twenty more each for destruction and its subsequent emptiness, eighty intermediate eons together comprising one great eon — and it is not one but three full incalculable eons of such great eons, an uncountable multitude beyond even this scale, that a bodhisattva must traverse to sever coarseness completely, from the plain pāramitā through the near-pāramitā to the great pāramitā and finally to buddhahood itself.

91. Section: The Bodhisattva's Own Affliction — Free of Defilement, Without Fault, of Immeasurable Merit

World-Honored One! As for the affliction that arises in bodhisattvas across these grounds, what characteristic, what fault, and what excellent quality should be known regarding it? Good sons! Its characteristic is being free of defilement. Why is this so? These bodhisattvas, on the first ground, have already, with certainty, skillfully penetrated the dharma-realm of all dharmas. For this reason, whenever a bodhisattva gives rise to affliction, it is only with full knowledge, never through ignorance — hence it is called the characteristic of being free of defilement. Because it is unable to produce any suffering within the bodhisattva's own person, it is without fault. Because a bodhisattva who gives rise to such affliction is thereby able to sever the very cause of suffering within the realm of sentient beings, it possesses immeasurable excellent qualities. How truly wondrous, World-Honored One! Unsurpassed enlightenment carries such great benefit and excellent quality that even the affliction a bodhisattva gives rise to surpasses the roots of goodness of every ordinary being, śrāvaka, and pratyekabuddha alike — how much more so its other immeasurable excellent qualities!

Chinese Source

世尊！是諸菩薩，於諸地中所生煩惱，當知何相？何失？何德？善男子！無染汙相。何以故？是諸菩薩於初地中，定於一切諸法法界已善通達。由此因緣，菩薩要知方起煩惱，非為不知，是故說名無染汙相。於自身中不能生苦，故無過失。菩薩生起如是煩惱，於有情界能斷苦因，是故彼有無量功德。甚奇世尊！無上菩提乃有如是大功德利，令諸菩薩生起煩惱，尚勝一切有情、聲聞、獨覺善根，何況其餘無量功德。

Ru-Yi Translation

World-Honored One! As for the affliction that arises in bodhisattvas across these grounds, what characteristic, what fault, and what excellent quality should be known regarding it? Good sons! Its characteristic is being free of defilement. Why is this so? These bodhisattvas, on the first ground, have already, with certainty, skillfully penetrated the dharma-realm of all dharmas. For this reason, whenever a bodhisattva gives rise to affliction, it is only with full knowledge, never through ignorance — hence it is called the characteristic of being free of defilement. Because it is unable to produce any suffering within the bodhisattva's own person, it is without fault. Because a bodhisattva who gives rise to such affliction is thereby able to sever the very cause of suffering within the realm of sentient beings, it possesses immeasurable excellent qualities. How truly wondrous, World-Honored One! Unsurpassed enlightenment carries such great benefit and excellent quality that even the affliction a bodhisattva gives rise to surpasses the roots of goodness of every ordinary being, śrāvaka, and pratyekabuddha alike — how much more so its other immeasurable excellent qualities!

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi connects this closing exchange to a familiar distinction from the bodhisattva precepts themselves: a transgression committed with defiled intent versus one committed without it, the latter belonging only to a noble bodhisattva already established on the grounds. A first-ground bodhisattva has already directly realized the dharma-realm underlying all dharmas, so that whenever affliction does arise, it arises with full clear knowledge of what it is doing and why — never through actual ignorance or genuine confusion — and it is precisely this knowing quality that keeps it free of defilement, distinguishing it categorically from an ordinary being's own blind affliction. Because a bodhisattva's own mind, grounded in non-discriminating wisdom, is never actually disturbed or made to suffer by such affliction, it carries no fault whatsoever for the bodhisattva personally; and because deliberately manifesting this affliction — as in the fifth ground's willingness to appear "impure" in service of guiding a being, or a compassionate display of anger meant to correct rather than harm — actually helps sever the very roots of suffering within the beings it is directed toward, it carries immeasurable merit rather than any actual harm. Affliction, for a bodhisattva already on the grounds, is itself bodhi in action rather than its opposite. Avalokiteśvara's closing exclamation marvels precisely at this: that unsurpassed enlightenment is so supremely excellent that even a bodhisattva's own afflictions, deliberately manifested this way, already surpass every root of goodness an ordinary being, śrāvaka, or pratyekabuddha could ever cultivate — let alone the bodhisattva's countless other, more obviously excellent qualities. With this exchange, the seventh of the ground-pāramitā chapter's eight major sections — the matters a bodhisattva must learn, unfolded across dozens of sub-topics from the six pāramitās' basic definition all the way to this closing account of a noble one's own affliction — comes, at long last, to its complete close.

92. Section: The Hidden Intent of "One Vehicle" — The Eighth and Final Section Opens

World-Honored One! As the Buddha has taught: the śrāvaka vehicle and the Mahāyāna are but a single vehicle. What is the hidden intent behind this?

Chinese Source

世尊！如佛所說：若聲聞乘、若復大乘唯是一乘。此何密意？

Ru-Yi Translation

World-Honored One! As the Buddha has taught: the śrāvaka vehicle and the Mahāyāna are but a single vehicle. What is the hidden intent behind this?

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

With the seventh section on the matters to be learned now closed in full, Avalokiteśvara opens the eighth and final section of the ground-pāramitā chapter: the hidden intent behind the Buddha's teaching that the śrāvaka vehicle and the Mahāyāna, elsewhere distinguished as separate vehicles for separate kinds of practitioners, are ultimately but a single vehicle. Master Cháng Bǎi frames this as the chapter's capstone question — after the exhaustive account of what the ten grounds contain, are named for, overcome, excel in, are reborn through, vow toward, and practice, the final piece is this reconciling teaching, addressing why the Buddha sometimes speaks of three vehicles and sometimes of only one, and what deeper unity underlies the apparent tension between them.

93. Section: The Śrāvaka Vehicle's Many Natures, and the Mahāyāna's Single Dharma-Realm

Good sons! Just as, within the śrāvaka vehicle, I have taught the various natures of dharmas — the five aggregates, or the six inner sense-fields, or the six outer sense-fields, and categories of this kind — within the Mahāyāna, I teach that these very same dharmas share a single dharma-realm and a single guiding principle; for this reason I do not teach any difference in nature among the vehicles.

Chinese Source

善男子！如我於彼聲聞乘中，宣說種種諸法自性，所謂五蘊、或內六處、或外六處，如是等類。於大乘中，即說彼法同一法界、同一理趣，故我不說乘差別性。

Ru-Yi Translation

Good sons! Just as, within the śrāvaka vehicle, I have taught the various natures of dharmas — the five aggregates, or the six inner sense-fields, or the six outer sense-fields, and categories of this kind — within the Mahāyāna, I teach that these very same dharmas share a single dharma-realm and a single guiding principle; for this reason I do not teach any difference in nature among the vehicles.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

The Buddha's answer identifies exactly where the teaching differs and exactly where it converges. To śrāvaka practitioners, the Buddha taught the various self-natures of dharmas directly — the five aggregates, the six inner and outer sense-fields, and so on — without yet unpacking the three natures or teaching that these very dharmas, verbally designated, are themselves empty. To Mahāyāna practitioners, the same dharmas are taught to share a single dharma-realm and a single guiding principle: whatever is verbally established is empty of substance, and this emptiness is the very suchness every vehicle alike must ultimately realize. Precisely because the emptiness realized is identical regardless of vehicle — a śrāvaka's realization of no-self is the same emptiness a Mahāyāna bodhisattva realizes — the Buddha does not, in this specific respect, teach any real difference in nature among the vehicles; it is only with regard to differing motivation, differing scope of practice, and differing accumulation of merit that the vehicles are properly distinguished at all.

94. Section: Exaggeration, Denial, and Needless Dispute — The Hidden Intent Revealed

Amid this, some, taking the words literally, wrongly discriminate their meaning — one kind exaggerating, another kind denying. Further, regarding the distinct principles of the various vehicles, holding that they mutually contradict one another, they thereby stir up dispute after dispute in turn. This is what is called the hidden intent here.

Chinese Source

於中或有如言於義妄起分別，一類增益、一類損減。又於諸乘差別道理，謂互相違，如是展轉遞興諍論。如是名為此中密意。

Ru-Yi Translation

Amid this, some, taking the words literally, wrongly discriminate their meaning — one kind exaggerating, another kind denying. Further, regarding the distinct principles of the various vehicles, holding that they mutually contradict one another, they thereby stir up dispute after dispute in turn. This is what is called the hidden intent here.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

The Buddha closes by naming the two failure modes this teaching is meant to forestall. Some hearers exaggerate — taking the śrāvaka teaching's five aggregates and sense-fields as substantially, permanently real (as certain Abhidharma schools held that dharma-substance persists across all three times), or, hearing that the three vehicles share one suchness, wrongly concluding the lesser vehicles and the Mahāyāna are therefore simply identical in every respect. Others deny — having studied the signless teaching of emptiness too aggressively, they collapse into nihilistic emptiness, denying even the dependent and perfected natures altogether, as though nothing whatsoever, not even conditioned arising, could be found anywhere. Both errors, and the disputes that inevitably follow once one side insists the vehicles are simply the same and another insists they are simply opposed, are exactly what a correct understanding of this teaching dissolves: the vehicles converge in the single emptiness ultimately realized, while still differing in their conventional scope of practice, motivation, and merit — a distinction, once properly grasped, that leaves no real ground for dispute at all. This, the Buddha concludes, is the entire hidden intent behind teaching sometimes three vehicles and sometimes only one — precisely the reconciliation the *Lotus Sūtra*'s own "opening the three to reveal the one" enacts at greater length. With this, the eighth and final section of the ground-pāramitā chapter's extended exposition — and with it, the entire "elaborate distinguishing" portion of the whole chapter — comes to its complete close.

95. Section: The Closing Verses — Reviewing All Eight Sections in Six Lines

At that time, the World-Honored One, wishing to restate this meaning once more, spoke these verses: What the grounds contain, their names, what they counteract, their excellence, their rebirth, their vows, and the matters to be learned — relying on what the Buddha teaches as the Mahāyāna, excellently cultivating these, one attains great awakening. Teaching the various natures of all dharmas, and teaching too that all share a single guiding principle — whether within the lesser vehicle or the higher vehicle, I therefore teach that the vehicles have no difference in nature. Taking the words literally, wrongly discriminating their meaning, some exaggerate and others deny; holding that these two mutually contradict each other, a foolish understanding gives rise to dispute.

Chinese Source

爾時世尊欲重宣此義而說頌曰：諸地攝想所對治，殊勝生願及諸學；由依佛說是大乘，於此善修成大覺。宣說諸法種種性，復說皆同一理趣；謂於下乘或上乘，故我說乘無異性。如言於義妄分別，或有增益或損減；謂此二種互相違，愚癡意解成乖諍。

Ru-Yi Translation

At that time, the World-Honored One, wishing to restate this meaning once more, spoke these verses: What the grounds contain, their names, what they counteract, their excellence, their rebirth, their vows, and the matters to be learned — relying on what the Buddha teaches as the Mahāyāna, excellently cultivating these, one attains great awakening. Teaching the various natures of all dharmas, and teaching too that all share a single guiding principle — whether within the lesser vehicle or the higher vehicle, I therefore teach that the vehicles have no difference in nature. Taking the words literally, wrongly discriminating their meaning, some exaggerate and others deny; holding that these two mutually contradict each other, a foolish understanding gives rise to dispute.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi reads the first couplet as a compact table of contents for the entire chapter just completed: "what the grounds contain" names the first section (the fourfold purity and elevenfold division), "their names" the second (why each ground bears the name it does), "what they counteract" the third (the twenty-two delusions and eleven kinds of coarseness), "their excellence" the fourth (the eight qualities growing more excellent ground by ground), "their rebirth" the fifth (the four reasons a bodhisattva's rebirth is supreme), "their vows" the sixth (the vast, wondrous, and supreme vow), and "the matters to be learned" the seventh (the six pāramitās unpacked across dozens of sub-topics) — every section of this long chapter relying on what the Buddha teaches as the Mahāyāna, and a bodhisattva who excellently cultivates all of it in turn thereby attains great awakening. The second couplet restates the eighth section's own resolution in miniature: the Buddha teaches the various distinct natures of dharmas to the lesser vehicle, and teaches that all dharmas share one guiding principle to every vehicle including the highest, so that no real difference in nature separates lower from higher vehicle where ultimate emptiness itself is concerned. The third couplet names the two errors this teaching guards against: exaggeration, clinging to verbally designated natures as substantially real, and denial, collapsing the dependent and perfected natures into nothingness — errors that, held by different parties convinced the other side is simply wrong, breed the very disputes a correct middle-way understanding dissolves. With this six-line summary, the Buddha's own verse restatement of the entire chapter distinguishing the ground-pāramitās comes to its close.

96. Section: Named the Ground-Pāramitā Teaching of Definitive Meaning — The Chapter Concludes

World-Honored One! Within this Dharma-door of Saṃdhinirmocana, what should this teaching be called? How should I uphold it? Good sons! This is called the teaching of the definitive meaning of the ground-pāramitās; you should uphold this teaching of the definitive meaning of the ground-pāramitās. When this teaching of the definitive meaning of the ground-pāramitās was spoken, within the great assembly, seventy-five thousand bodhisattvas all attained the bodhisattva's Mahāyāna samādhi of luminosity.

Chinese Source

世尊！於是解深密法門中，此名何教？我當云何奉持？善男子！此名諸地波羅蜜多了義之教，於此諸地波羅蜜多了義之教，汝當奉持。說此諸地波羅蜜多了義教時，於大會中，有七十五千菩薩，皆得菩薩大乘光明三摩地。

Ru-Yi Translation

World-Honored One! Within this Dharma-door of Saṃdhinirmocana, what should this teaching be called? How should I uphold it? Good sons! This is called the teaching of the definitive meaning of the ground-pāramitās; you should uphold this teaching of the definitive meaning of the ground-pāramitās. When this teaching of the definitive meaning of the ground-pāramitās was spoken, within the great assembly, seventy-five thousand bodhisattvas all attained the bodhisattva's Mahāyāna samādhi of luminosity.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Avalokiteśvara closes the chapter exactly as Maitreya closed the previous one: asking the Buddha to name the teaching just given, so it may be properly upheld. The Buddha names it the definitive-meaning teaching of the ground-pāramitās — the entire eight-section exposition of what the ten grounds and the Buddha ground contain, are named for, overcome, excel in, are reborn through, vow toward, practice, and ultimately unify as a single vehicle, now sealed as direct, ultimate teaching requiring no further unpacking. Seventy-five thousand bodhisattvas in the assembly attain the Mahāyāna's own samādhi of luminosity — a concentration whose brightness, Master Cháng Bǎi explains, comes from the non-discriminating wisdom cultivated throughout this teaching, dispelling the ignorance that would otherwise leave even a bodhisattva's own study of the Mahāyāna clouded. He notes that a reader who has simply followed this chapter's teaching without yet cultivating meditative concentration has not thereby attained this same samādhi outright — hearing-wisdom must still mature into reflection-wisdom, and reflection into the cultivation-wisdom that calm-abiding and insight alone can bring — but the same teaching, genuinely taken up, leads to the very same destination in time. With this, the Chapter Distinguishing the Ground-Pāramitās — the sixth of the seven citations this treatise's Chapter on Excellent Qualities draws from the Saṃdhinirmocana Sūtra — comes to its complete and final close.

97. Section: Mañjuśrī Asks — What Characteristics Does the Tathāgata's Dharma-Body Have?

Further, again in dependence on the vehicle provisionally established, distinguishing and explaining the Tathāgata's accomplished activity, it should be known exactly as the Saṃdhinirmocana Sūtra describes: the bodhisattva-mahāsattva Mañjuśrī said to the Buddha: World-Honored One! As the Buddha has taught concerning the Tathāgata's Dharma-body, what characteristics does the Tathāgata's Dharma-body have? The Buddha told the bodhisattva Mañjuśrī: Good sons! If one has thoroughly cultivated release with regard to the grounds and the pāramitās, and the transformation of basis has reached its full completion, this is called the characteristic of the Tathāgata's Dharma-body. It should be known that this characteristic is inconceivable for two reasons: because it is free of discursive elaboration, and because it involves no constructed activity. Yet sentient beings cling to discursive elaboration, and so perceive it as though it involved constructed activity.

Chinese Source

復次，即依乘假安立，分別如來成所作事，當知如解深密經中，曼殊室利菩薩摩訶薩請問佛言：世尊！如佛所說如來法身，如來法身有何等相？佛告曼殊室利菩薩：善男子！若於諸地波羅蜜多善修出離，轉依成滿，是名如來法身之相。當知此相，二因緣故不可思議，無戲論故，無所為故。而諸眾生計著戲論，有所為故。

Ru-Yi Translation

Further, again in dependence on the vehicle provisionally established, distinguishing and explaining the Tathāgata's accomplished activity, it should be known exactly as the Saṃdhinirmocana Sūtra describes: the bodhisattva-mahāsattva Mañjuśrī said to the Buddha: World-Honored One! As the Buddha has taught concerning the Tathāgata's Dharma-body, what characteristics does the Tathāgata's Dharma-body have? The Buddha told the bodhisattva Mañjuśrī: Good sons! If one has thoroughly cultivated release with regard to the grounds and the pāramitās, and the transformation of basis has reached its full completion, this is called the characteristic of the Tathāgata's Dharma-body. It should be known that this characteristic is inconceivable for two reasons: because it is free of discursive elaboration, and because it involves no constructed activity. Yet sentient beings cling to discursive elaboration, and so perceive it as though it involved constructed activity.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

A new citation opens: the Chapter on Excellent Qualities' seventh and final citation from the Saṃdhinirmocana Sūtra, the Chapter Distinguishing Accomplished Activities — what a buddha's own attainment actually consists in and does, following naturally after the ground-pāramitā chapter's account of the cause (a bodhisattva's practice across the ten grounds). Master Cháng Bǎi frames the Saṃdhinirmocana's overall structure one final time: object, practice, and fruit — ultimate truth, mind-consciousness, the three natures, and the three kinds of lacking a nature comprising the object; the yoga chapter and the ground-pāramitā chapter comprising practice, for bodhisattvas before and upon the grounds respectively; and this final chapter comprising the fruit, a buddha's own accomplished activity. The new questioner is Mañjuśrī — glossed through three traditional etymologies of "Tathāgata" itself: doctrinally, tathāgata simply is suchness, coming as "thus," in the sense the *Diamond Sūtra* gives, "the Tathāgata comes from nowhere and goes nowhere"; through practice, "thus" names the six pāramitās as the very path by which one arrives at buddhahood; and combining both, arriving at reality itself by means of the six pāramitās and the eleven kinds of emptiness, practice and principle together — while Mañjuśrī's own name, "wondrous auspiciousness," is glossed through a mind so utterly tranquil that it benefits friend and foe with perfect equality, untroubled and untroubling both, precisely the auspiciousness every being in the world instinctively recognizes and reveres. Mañjuśrī's opening question asks directly after the Dharma-body's own characteristics, and the Buddha's answer identifies it precisely: the completed transformation of basis, once release from the twenty-two delusions and eleven kinds of coarseness across the ten grounds and pāramitās has been thoroughly cultivated to its very completion — the maturation consciousness fully transformed into the stainless consciousness. This characteristic is inconceivable for two reasons: free of all discursive elaboration (beyond the reach of verbal designation and conceptual proliferation alike) and involving no constructed activity (since a mind perfectly matched to suchness itself no longer "does" anything in the conditioned sense) — though ordinary beings, still clinging to discursive elaboration out of habit, inevitably misperceive even this activity-free reality as though it involved deliberate construction, precisely the confusion the coming sections will work to dispel.

98. Section: Not the Dharma-Body but the Body of Liberation — What a Śrāvaka or Pratyekabuddha Attains

World-Honored One! Is the transformation of basis attained by śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas called the Dharma-body? Good sons! It is not called the Dharma-body. World-Honored One! What, then, should it be called? Good sons! It is called the body of liberation.

Chinese Source

世尊！聲聞、獨覺所得轉依，名法身不？善男子！不名法身。世尊！當名何身？善男子！名解脫身。

Ru-Yi Translation

World-Honored One! Is the transformation of basis attained by śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas called the Dharma-body? Good sons! It is not called the Dharma-body. World-Honored One! What, then, should it be called? Good sons! It is called the body of liberation.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Mañjuśrī now asks the natural follow-up: śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas also realize suchness and undergo a genuine transformation of basis upon attaining arhatship or the pratyekabuddha's own fruit — does their attainment likewise deserve the name Dharma-body? The Buddha answers firmly that it does not: an arhat or pratyekabuddha's transformation only renames the ālaya-consciousness as the maturation consciousness, without transforming its very substance into the stainless consciousness a buddha alone achieves, since only the afflictive obstruction's own seeds are severed, the cognitive obstruction's own seeds left untouched. What they attain instead is properly called the body of liberation — the accomplishment of freedom from the afflictive entanglements of craving and view within the three realms, genuine liberation in its own right, but categorically short of the full Dharma-body only a buddha's complete severing of both obstructions together can bring about.

99. Section: Equal in Liberation, Different in the Dharma-Body

By the body of liberation, it is said that all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas are equal, entirely equal, to all Tathāgatas. By the Dharma-body, it is said that there is a difference. Because the Tathāgata's Dharma-body involves this difference, its immeasurable, most excellent qualities are such that no calculation or simile can ever reach them.

Chinese Source

由解脫身故，說一切聲聞、獨覺，與諸如來平等平等。由法身故，說有差別。如來法身有差別故，無量功德最勝差別，算數譬喻所不能及。

Ru-Yi Translation

By the body of liberation, it is said that all śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas are equal, entirely equal, to all Tathāgatas. By the Dharma-body, it is said that there is a difference. Because the Tathāgata's Dharma-body involves this difference, its immeasurable, most excellent qualities are such that no calculation or simile can ever reach them.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

The Buddha resolves the apparent tension directly: measured by the body of liberation alone — freedom from the afflictive entanglements of craving and view within the three realms — śrāvakas, pratyekabuddhas, and buddhas are genuinely, entirely equal, since every one of them has severed the same afflictive obstruction completely. But measured by the Dharma-body specifically, only a buddha's attainment qualifies, since only a buddha has also severed the cognitive obstruction in its entirety, down to its subtlest habitual trace, accumulating immeasurable excellent qualities across three full incalculable eons of the pāramitās that no number or comparison could ever capture. With this, the second of ten sections making up the Chapter Distinguishing Accomplished Activities' own detailed exposition — distinguishing śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas from buddhas — closes in full.

100. Section: The Transformation-Body's Activity — Arising Like the World Itself, Yet the Dharma-Body Never Arises

World-Honored One! How should I understand the characteristic of the Tathāgata's arising? Good sons! Every Tathāgata's transformation-body carries out its activity just as the world gives rise to every kind of being; the Tathāgata abides with the characteristic of being adorned by a multitude of excellent qualities. It should be known that the characteristic of the transformation-body involves arising, while the characteristic of the Dharma-body involves no arising at all.

Chinese Source

世尊！我當云何應知如來生起之相？善男子！一切如來化身作業，如世界起一切種類，如來功德眾所莊嚴住持為相。當知化身相有生起，法身之相無有生起。

Ru-Yi Translation

World-Honored One! How should I understand the characteristic of the Tathāgata's arising? Good sons! Every Tathāgata's transformation-body carries out its activity just as the world gives rise to every kind of being; the Tathāgata abides with the characteristic of being adorned by a multitude of excellent qualities. It should be known that the characteristic of the transformation-body involves arising, while the characteristic of the Dharma-body involves no arising at all.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Mañjuśrī opens the third section, on the characteristic of the Tathāgata's own arising — meaning specifically the transformation-body ordinary beings actually perceive, since the reward-body is seen only by noble bodhisattvas and the Dharma-body itself has no perceptible form at all. The Buddha answers that the transformation-body's activity mirrors the full range of activity found throughout the world itself, sustained by a buddha's own accumulated excellent qualities. Citing the *Buddhabhūmi-śāstra* (juan 6) at length, Master Cháng Bǎi details the threefold structure this activity takes — bodily, verbal, and mental transformation, each further divided into three or four kinds — arising from the duty-fulfilling wisdom the five sense-consciousnesses are transformed into at buddhahood. Bodily transformation appears as manifesting supernormal power (to humble the arrogance of the skilled and draw beings toward the teaching), manifesting rebirth (taking a form suited to whatever world he appears in, matching its own social station), and manifesting the fruit of karma (appearing to experience illness, injury, or slander from past causes, demonstrating that karma is never voided even for a buddha, so that beings take care with their own conduct). Verbal transformation appears as congratulatory speech (encouragement offered when a practitioner makes genuine progress), skillful speech (practical guidance suited to what a given audience actually needs, as when lay disciples are first taught giving, precepts, and heavenly rebirth before being shown desire's own dangers), and disputational speech (resolving doubts and refuting wrong views, as in the Buddha's own debate with the wandering ascetic Dīrghanakha). Mental transformation appears as discerning what should and should not be done for the sake of the eighty-four thousand afflictive tendencies among beings, as deliberating on beings' own progress or regression in practice, as initiating the specific antidote a given affliction calls for, and as receiving and correctly answering whatever a questioner asks, whether definite, indefinite, best met with a counter-question, or best left unanswered. Crucially, the Buddha closes by drawing the sharpest distinction of all: this entire, vast field of activity belongs only to the transformation-body, which does arise and pass in time; the Dharma-body itself, free of discursive elaboration and constructed activity as already established, has no arising whatsoever — the two must never be confused.

101. Section: The Eight Aspects of a Buddha's Career — Descending, Entering the Womb, Birth, and Enlightenment

World-Honored One! How should the skillful means by which the transformation-body is manifested be understood? Good sons! Throughout every trichiliocosm buddha-land alike, manifesting in a household universally acknowledged as a sovereign royal house, or in a household universally acknowledged as a supreme field of merit, the Tathāgata simultaneously manifests, in sequence: entering the womb, being born, growing up and enjoying the pleasures of desire, going forth into homelessness, demonstrating ascetic practice, and, having relinquished ascetic practice, attaining perfect, complete enlightenment. This is called the skillful means by which the Tathāgata manifests the transformation-body.

Chinese Source

世尊！云何應知示現化身方便善巧？善男子！遍於一切三千大千佛國土中，或眾推許增上王家，或眾推許大福田家，同時入胎、誕生、長大受欲、出家、示行苦行、捨苦行已成等正覺次第示現，是名如來示現化身方便善巧。

Ru-Yi Translation

World-Honored One! How should the skillful means by which the transformation-body is manifested be understood? Good sons! Throughout every trichiliocosm buddha-land alike, manifesting in a household universally acknowledged as a sovereign royal house, or in a household universally acknowledged as a supreme field of merit, the Tathāgata simultaneously manifests, in sequence: entering the womb, being born, growing up and enjoying the pleasures of desire, going forth into homelessness, demonstrating ascetic practice, and, having relinquished ascetic practice, attaining perfect, complete enlightenment. This is called the skillful means by which the Tathāgata manifests the transformation-body.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi unpacks this sequence into the traditional eight aspects of a buddha's earthly career: descent from Tuṣita heaven (where a future buddha, as a bodhisattva of one final rebirth, waits until beings' own capacity is ripe — the *Mahāvibhāṣā*'s own account of why specifically Tuṣita, the fourth of the six heavens, gives both a practical reason, its lifespan matching the interval before conditions mature, and a symbolic one, its position at the very middle between the four heavenly kings and the great Brahmā heaven); entering the womb (traditionally as a white elephant, chosen for its great size, its four legs symbolizing wisdom, ultimate truth, the canon, and tranquility, and its six tusks symbolizing six kinds of unsurpassed excellence, the bodhisattva said to abide within the womb itself as though enthroned in a vast palace, teaching the Dharma to visiting gods day and night rather than suffering as an ordinary embryo does); birth itself (emerging from the mother's right side, walking seven steps at once — the *Mahāvibhāṣā* glossing this as offering the seven treasures that spring from the earth with each step, or fulfilling the earth-deity's own wish — and displaying, even as a child, mastery of the five fields of learning no teacher could instruct him in further); growing up and enjoying the pleasures of desire (his father, warned by fortune-tellers that the prince would become either a universal monarch or a buddha, arranging marriage and every seasonal pleasure to keep him from renunciation); going forth into homelessness (troubled after witnessing an old man, a sick man, a corpse, and a renunciant on excursions beyond the palace walls, and stealing away by night); demonstrating ascetic practice (six years of severe austerity, a single grain of rice and sesame a day, undertaken specifically to overcome the ascetic traditions of the time on their own terms); and, having relinquished that austerity — accepting a bowl of milk-rice from a herding girl to demonstrate the middle way between indulgence and self-mortification — defeating Māra's daughters sent to seduce him, and attaining, in the course of a single night, first the recollection of countless past lives, then the divine eye seeing all beings' rebirths, and finally, as dawn broke, the complete exhaustion of every contaminant, becoming the fully awakened Buddha. Master Cháng Bǎi notes that this same sequence, though sometimes counted as eleven or thirteen aspects in other sources, occurs simultaneously across every world-system a single buddha's activity extends to — this fascicle's own root text specifying only through enlightenment itself, with the turning of the Dharma-wheel and final nirvāṇa (invited by Brahmā's own request, since the Buddha initially doubted whether the teaching could be understood at all, and eventually undertaken, per the *Avataṃsaka*'s own tenfold rationale, to demonstrate impermanence, dispel attachment to the physical body, and turn beings toward diligence rather than complacent reliance on his continued presence) rounding out the traditional eight when the fuller citations elsewhere in the treatise are consulted. He closes by noting how this entire pattern of realization — recollecting past lives at the first watch, seeing all beings' rebirths at the middle watch, and severing every contaminant at dawn — mirrors what calm-abiding and insight, cultivated to their very completion, actually accomplish: a direct, non-discursive seeing into the nature of saṃsāra that discursive philosophy alone could never reach.

102. Section: Three Kinds of Speech — Discourses, Discipline, and the Fundamental Matrices

World-Honored One! In general, how many kinds of distinct speech does every Tathāgata's own body sustain, by which beings not yet matured are brought to maturity, and beings already matured, taking this very speech as their object, swiftly attain liberation? Good sons! The Tathāgata's speech is, in brief, of three kinds: first, the discourses; second, the discipline; and third, the fundamental matrices. World-Honored One! What are the discourses? What is the discipline? And what are the fundamental matrices?

Chinese Source

世尊！凡有幾種一切如來身所住持言音差別，由此言音，所化有情未成熟者，令其成熟；已成熟者，緣此為境速得解脫？善男子！如來言音略有三種。一者、契經，二者、調伏，三者、本母。世尊！云何契經？云何調伏？云何本母？

Ru-Yi Translation

World-Honored One! In general, how many kinds of distinct speech does every Tathāgata's own body sustain, by which beings not yet matured are brought to maturity, and beings already matured, taking this very speech as their object, swiftly attain liberation? Good sons! The Tathāgata's speech is, in brief, of three kinds: first, the discourses; second, the discipline; and third, the fundamental matrices. World-Honored One! What are the discourses? What is the discipline? And what are the fundamental matrices?

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Mañjuśrī opens the fourth section — the distinct kinds of speech by which a buddha's transformation-body actually teaches — and the Buddha answers with the traditional threefold division underlying the entire canon: the discourses (sūtra, glossed as what connects with the truth above and with beings' own capacity below, gathering the teaching's full meaning the way a garland gathers flowers, and sorting right from wrong the way a carpenter's plumb-line sorts crooked wood from straight), the discipline (vinaya, governing and restraining bodily and verbal conduct, cutting off wrongdoing), and the fundamental matrices (mātṛkā, glossed as the "mother" that gives rise to further understanding, since treatises gather and discuss the deeper meaning implicit within the discourses themselves). Mañjuśrī immediately presses further, asking the Buddha to define each of these three in turn — the question the coming section will answer point by point.

103. Section: The Discourses Defined — Four Matters, from Hearing to Enlightenment

Good sons! Wherever I gather matters together to reveal the various dharmas, this is called a discourse — relying on four matters, or on nine matters, or, in fuller detail, on twenty-nine matters. What are the four matters? First, the matter of hearing; second, the matter of taking refuge; third, the matter of training; and fourth, the matter of enlightenment.

Chinese Source

善男子！若於是處，我依攝事顯示諸法，是名契經。謂依四事，或依九事，或復依於二十九事。云何四事？一者、聽聞事，二者、歸趣事，三者、修學事，四者、菩提事。

Ru-Yi Translation

Good sons! Wherever I gather matters together to reveal the various dharmas, this is called a discourse — relying on four matters, or on nine matters, or, in fuller detail, on twenty-nine matters. What are the four matters? First, the matter of hearing; second, the matter of taking refuge; third, the matter of training; and fourth, the matter of enlightenment.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

The Buddha begins defining "discourse" (sūtra) by three frameworks of increasing detail. The simplest, four matters, covers the whole of a discourse's teaching content: the matter of hearing (how to properly listen to and reflect on the Dharma, with an undefiled, unscattered mind); the matter of taking refuge (going for refuge to the Three Jewels, and the five precepts and ten wholesome courses of action that follow from it); the matter of training (cultivating discipline, concentration, and wisdom); and the matter of enlightenment (the thirty-seven factors of awakening — the foundations of mindfulness, right efforts, faculties, powers, factors of enlightenment, and the eightfold path — together with calm-abiding and insight themselves). Every discourse the Buddha ever taught, however varied its surface content, ultimately addresses one or more of these four underlying matters.

104. Section: Nine Matters — Beings, Their Sustenance, Arising, and the Eightfold Assembly

What are the nine matters? First, the matter of designating sentient beings; second, the matter of what they make use of; third, the matter of their arising; fourth, the matter of their abiding once arisen; fifth, the matter of their defilement and purity; sixth, the matter of their distinctions; seventh, the matter of the one who teaches; eighth, the matter of what is taught; and ninth, the matter of the assemblies themselves.

Chinese Source

云何九事？一者、施設有情事，二者、彼所受用事，三者、彼生起事，四者、彼生已住事，五者、彼染淨事，六者、彼差別事，七者、能宣說事，八者、所宣說事，九者、諸眾會事。

Ru-Yi Translation

What are the nine matters? First, the matter of designating sentient beings; second, the matter of what they make use of; third, the matter of their arising; fourth, the matter of their abiding once arisen; fifth, the matter of their defilement and purity; sixth, the matter of their distinctions; seventh, the matter of the one who teaches; eighth, the matter of what is taught; and ninth, the matter of the assemblies themselves.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi glosses each of the nine, citing the Ground of Mind (juan 3): designating beings means teaching through the five aggregates a being is made up of; what they make use of means the twelve sense-fields, the six faculties engaging six objects from which feeling, and defilement or purity, arise; their arising means the twelve links of dependent arising, whether already occurring or presently occurring; their abiding once arisen means the four foods (morsel, contact, volition, and consciousness) that sustain a being already born; their defilement and purity means the four noble truths themselves; their distinctions means the boundless variety of realms — Master Cháng Bǎi citing the Bodhisattva-bhūmi's own fivefold "immeasurables" a bodhisattva must master (the immeasurable variety of beings, of world-systems, of dharmas, of beings to be tamed, and of means for taming them) as illustrative of just how vast this category is; the one who teaches means both the Buddha himself and his noble disciples, whose own teaching the Buddha endorses as equally authoritative; what is taught means the very factors of awakening already named among the four matters; and the assemblies themselves means the traditional eightfold audience — the ruling class, the priestly class, the householder class, renunciants, and the four heavenly kings, the Trāyastriṃśa gods, the Yāma gods, and the Brahmā gods — every distinct kind of listener a discourse might actually be addressed to.

105. Section: Twenty-Nine Matters — From Conditioned Formations to Not Regressing from Right View

What is meant by the twenty-nine matters? Relying on the defiled class, there is: the matter of gathering all conditioned formations; the matter of their sequential turning; the matter, within this very gathering, of the cause of continued flow into future lives once the notion of a person has been formed; and the matter of the cause of continued flow into future lives once the notion of a dharma has been formed. Relying on the pure class: the matter of fixing mindfulness upon an object; the matter, within this, of diligent exertion; the matter of the mind's abiding; the matter of blissful abiding in this very life; the matter of the skillful means for transcending every condition of suffering; the matter of thoroughly knowing this — itself threefold, as the basis for thoroughly knowing what rests on distorted view, the basis for thoroughly knowing the deviant conduct found among beings apart from the notion of a being, and the basis for thoroughly knowing freedom, within oneself, from overestimation; the matter of the basis for cultivation; the matter of direct realization; the matter of cultivation itself; the matter of making that firm; the matter of its aspect; the matter of its object; the matter of the skillful discernment of what has and has not yet been severed; the matter of distraction; the matter of freedom from distraction; the matter of the basis for freedom from distraction; the matter of not abandoning the diligent, laborious effort of cultivation; the matter of the benefit of cultivation; the matter of its firmness; the matter of gathering noble conduct; the matter of gathering the attendants of noble conduct; the matter of penetrating reality; the matter of realizing nirvāṇa; the matter, within the well-taught Dharma and discipline, of mundane right view surpassing the very summit of right view attained by every non-Buddhist teaching; and the matter of not regressing from this very attainment.

Chinese Source

云何名為二十九事？謂依雜染品，有攝諸行事；彼次第隨轉事；即於是中，作補特伽羅想已，於當來世流轉因事；作法想已，於當來世流轉因事。依清淨品，有繫念於所緣事；即於是中，勤精進事；心安住事；現法樂住事；超一切苦緣方便事；彼遍知事，此復三種，顛倒遍知所依處故，依有情想外有情中邪行遍知所依處故，內離增上慢遍知所依處故；修依處事；作證事；修習事；令彼堅固事；彼行相事；彼所緣事；已斷、未斷觀察善巧事；彼散亂事；彼不散亂事；不散亂依處事；不棄修習劬勞加行事；修習勝利事；彼堅牢事；攝聖行事；攝聖行眷屬事；通達真實事；證得涅槃事；於善說法毗奈耶中，世間正見超昇一切外道所得正見頂事；及即於此不修退事。

Ru-Yi Translation

What is meant by the twenty-nine matters? Relying on the defiled class, there is: the matter of gathering all conditioned formations; the matter of their sequential turning; the matter, within this very gathering, of the cause of continued flow into future lives once the notion of a person has been formed; and the matter of the cause of continued flow into future lives once the notion of a dharma has been formed. Relying on the pure class: the matter of fixing mindfulness upon an object; the matter, within this, of diligent exertion; the matter of the mind's abiding; the matter of blissful abiding in this very life; the matter of the skillful means for transcending every condition of suffering; the matter of thoroughly knowing this — itself threefold, as the basis for thoroughly knowing what rests on distorted view, the basis for thoroughly knowing the deviant conduct found among beings apart from the notion of a being, and the basis for thoroughly knowing freedom, within oneself, from overestimation; the matter of the basis for cultivation; the matter of direct realization; the matter of cultivation itself; the matter of making that firm; the matter of its aspect; the matter of its object; the matter of the skillful discernment of what has and has not yet been severed; the matter of distraction; the matter of freedom from distraction; the matter of the basis for freedom from distraction; the matter of not abandoning the diligent, laborious effort of cultivation; the matter of the benefit of cultivation; the matter of its firmness; the matter of gathering noble conduct; the matter of gathering the attendants of noble conduct; the matter of penetrating reality; the matter of realizing nirvāṇa; the matter, within the well-taught Dharma and discipline, of mundane right view surpassing the very summit of right view attained by every non-Buddhist teaching; and the matter of not regressing from this very attainment.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi maps this longest and most exhaustive of the three frameworks directly onto the five stages of the path, four matters belonging to the defiled class and twenty-five to the pure. The four defiled matters cover an ordinary being's condition entire: the five aggregates gathered together, their moment-to-moment turning through the twelve links of dependent arising, and the twin roots of continued rebirth — clinging to a self within these aggregates, and clinging to the aggregates themselves as substantially real dharmas. The twenty-five pure matters then trace the entire path from provisions to final arhatship. Four belong to still-mundane practice: fixing mindfulness on what has been heard (hearing-wisdom), diligent reflection upon it (reflection-wisdom), the mind's stable abiding (approaching absorption), and blissful abiding in this very life (the root dhyānas themselves, still without uncontaminated wisdom). Five belong to the stage that inclines toward liberation and the stage of decisive distinction together: the four noble truths as the very means of transcending suffering, and, within thoroughly knowing suffering specifically, the threefold task of overcoming the distorted view still active in the desire realm, the subtle deviant conduct and wrong view that meditative attainment in the form realm can still produce, and the overestimation of one's own attainment that meditative absorption in the formless realm invites — followed by the basis for cultivating the path (severing the causes of suffering), direct realization (of cessation), and cultivation itself (the thirty-seven factors of awakening). Four belong to the path of seeing, the attainment of stream-entry: irreversibility once the path is realized, the subsequently attained wisdom that follows root wisdom, the specific object of that subsequently attained wisdom (whether described as three or sixteen mind-moments), and the skillful discernment distinguishing which afflictions have already been severed by seeing from which still remain to be severed by cultivation. Six belong to the path of cultivation: the subtle distraction still remaining after seeing, freedom from that distraction, the very basis of that freedom (concentration itself), the encouragement never to abandon diligent effort merely because stream-entry has already been reached, the benefit that further cultivation still brings, and the diamond-like concentration that finally makes all of this unshakeably firm. Four belong to the stage beyond further training, arhatship itself: the wisdom of exhaustion and non-arising directly realized, the same wisdom's own attendant qualities, penetrating the true nature of reality before entering final nirvāṇa, and the actual realization of nirvāṇa itself. And the last two matters offer a comparative measure: even the merely mundane right view a practitioner gains simply by studying the well-taught Dharma and discipline already surpasses the very highest view any non-Buddhist teaching can produce, and abandoning cultivation even at this preliminary stage still counts as a genuine regression — not because any fault is found in the teaching itself, but simply because its benefit is forfeited the moment practice stops.

106. Section: Regression Means Ceasing to Practice, Not Finding Fault in the Teaching

Within the well-taught Dharma and discipline, it is called regression because of failing to cultivate it, not because some fault has been found in it that is called regression.

Chinese Source

於善說法毗奈耶中，不修習故說名為退，非見過失故名為退。

Ru-Yi Translation

Within the well-taught Dharma and discipline, it is called regression because of failing to cultivate it, not because some fault has been found in it that is called regression.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi clarifies the last of the twenty-nine matters: a practitioner is said to "regress" here not because the Dharma has revealed some hidden defect, causing him to abandon it in disillusionment, but simply because he has stopped cultivating a genuinely sound teaching — even continuing to accumulate ordinary mundane merit through hearing and reflection, short of actual absorption, already counts as a kind of regression relative to what full, uninterrupted cultivation could have achieved. This distinction — never blaming the teaching itself, only ever the practitioner's own lapse — closes the discourses' twenty-nine-matter framework and, with it, the full definition of what a "discourse" (sūtra) actually is.

107. Section: The Discipline Defined — Individual Liberation for Śrāvakas and Bodhisattvas Alike

Mañjuśrī! Wherever I, relying on śrāvakas and bodhisattvas alike, reveal the individual liberation and the dharmas associated with individual liberation, this is called the discipline.

Chinese Source

曼殊室利！若於是處，我依聲聞及諸菩薩，顯示別解脫及別解脫相應之法，是名調伏。

Ru-Yi Translation

Mañjuśrī! Wherever I, relying on śrāvakas and bodhisattvas alike, reveal the individual liberation and the dharmas associated with individual liberation, this is called the discipline.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Having finished defining the discourses, the Buddha turns to the second of the three, the discipline (vinaya). Master Cháng Bǎi notes the text names only śrāvakas and bodhisattvas, not pratyekabuddhas, since a pratyekabuddha practices alone and requires no formal code of restraint the way a monastic community does. "Individual liberation" (prātimokṣa) names the code of restraint itself — a śrāvaka's own precepts, or, for a bodhisattva, the restraint-discipline that transforms those same precepts once the bodhi-mind is generated — each individual precept freeing the one who upholds it from its own corresponding fault, hence "individual" liberation; "the dharmas associated with individual liberation" then names everything that supports and accompanies that restraint — for a bodhisattva, the further disciplines of gathering wholesome dharmas and of benefiting sentient beings already introduced earlier in this fascicle's account of the six pāramitās' threefold discipline. Together, restraint and its supporting practices comprise everything the term "discipline" covers in this threefold division of the Buddha's own speech.

108. Section: Seven Marks of the Discipline — From Receiving the Precepts to Relinquishing Them

World-Honored One! By how many marks is a bodhisattva's individual liberation encompassed? Good sons! You should know that there are seven marks: first, teaching the matter of the proper procedure for receiving the precepts; second, teaching the matter of what accords with defeat; third, teaching the matter of what accords with transgression; fourth, teaching the inherent nature of transgression; fifth, teaching the inherent nature of non-transgression; sixth, teaching how to be released from a transgression; and seventh, teaching the relinquishing of the code of restraint.

Chinese Source

世尊！菩薩別解脫幾相所攝？善男子！當知七相。一者、宣說受軌則事故；二者、宣說隨順他勝事故；三者、宣說隨順毀犯事故；四者、宣說有犯自性故；五者、宣說無犯自性故；六者、宣說出所犯故；七者、宣說捨律儀故。

Ru-Yi Translation

World-Honored One! By how many marks is a bodhisattva's individual liberation encompassed? Good sons! You should know that there are seven marks: first, teaching the matter of the proper procedure for receiving the precepts; second, teaching the matter of what accords with defeat; third, teaching the matter of what accords with transgression; fourth, teaching the inherent nature of transgression; fifth, teaching the inherent nature of non-transgression; sixth, teaching how to be released from a transgression; and seventh, teaching the relinquishing of the code of restraint.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Mañjuśrī asks how many marks fully account for a bodhisattva's own individual liberation, and the Buddha answers with seven. The proper procedure for receiving the precepts covers the formal ceremony and ongoing conduct of one who upholds them, abiding firmly in the training without any lapse of body or speech. What accords with defeat (pārājika, "the one overcome") covers the root transgressions committed when affliction proves stronger than restraint, repeated without any shame whatsoever. What accords with transgression more broadly covers the conditions under which lesser violations occur, along with their relative gravity.
