The inherent nature of transgression names actual instances of wrongdoing an ordained community's own history illustrates; the inherent nature of non-transgression names the opposite, noble ones who guard the precepts so purely that not even the subtlest infraction ever occurs, some willing to die rather than break them. Being released from a transgression covers the various methods of confession and repentance available once a fault has occurred — through visualization, formal ritual, or realization of non-arising. And relinquishing the code of restraint names the specific circumstances under which the precepts are formally given up: deliberately renouncing them before a discerning witness, committing one of the four root transgressions, an irreversible physical change removing the very basis for monastic status, generating karma so severe that all wholesome roots are cut off, or simple death, which naturally ends monastic vows bound to a single lifetime — for a bodhisattva, losing the bodhi-mind itself likewise loses the bodhisattva precepts along with it. With this sevenfold account complete, the definition of "discipline," the second of the three kinds of speech, closes in full.

109. Section: The Fundamental Matrices Defined — Eleven Marks, Beginning with the Conventional

Mañjuśrī! Wherever I, through eleven marks, decisively distinguish and reveal the various dharmas, this is called the fundamental matrices. What are these eleven marks? First, the mark of the conventional; second, the mark of the ultimate; third, the mark of the object of the factors of awakening; fourth, the mark of practice; fifth, the mark of essential nature; sixth, the mark of its fruit; seventh, the mark of receiving and expounding it; eighth, the mark of what obstructs it; ninth, the mark of what accords with it; tenth, the mark of its faults; and eleventh, the mark of its benefits. The mark of the conventional should be known to be threefold: first, teaching persons; second, teaching the imagined nature; and third, teaching the function and activity of dharmas.

Chinese Source

曼殊室利！若於是處，我以十一種相，決了分別顯示諸法，是名本母。何等名為十一種相？一者、世俗相，二者、勝義相，三者、菩提分法所緣相，四者、行相，五者、自性相，六者、彼果相，七者、彼領受開示相，八者、彼障礙法相，九者、彼隨順法相，十者、彼過患相，十一者、彼勝利相。世俗相者，當知三種。一者、宣說補特伽羅故；二者、宣說遍計所執自性故；三者、宣說諸法作用事業故。

Ru-Yi Translation

Mañjuśrī! Wherever I, through eleven marks, decisively distinguish and reveal the various dharmas, this is called the fundamental matrices. What are these eleven marks? First, the mark of the conventional; second, the mark of the ultimate; third, the mark of the object of the factors of awakening; fourth, the mark of practice; fifth, the mark of essential nature; sixth, the mark of its fruit; seventh, the mark of receiving and expounding it; eighth, the mark of what obstructs it; ninth, the mark of what accords with it; tenth, the mark of its faults; and eleventh, the mark of its benefits. The mark of the conventional should be known to be threefold: first, teaching persons; second, teaching the imagined nature; and third, teaching the function and activity of dharmas.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

The Buddha turns to the third and final kind of speech, the fundamental matrices (mātṛkā), meaning the treatises — organized here through eleven marks the coming sections unpack one by one. The first, the conventional, itself divides into three: teaching persons (beings who die in one place and are reborn in another, spoken of conventionally even though, in ultimate truth, no substantial person is ever found); teaching the imagined nature (the endless conceptual distinctions — this versus that, permanent versus impermanent — that ordinary language constantly draws, themselves empty when examined against ultimate truth); and teaching the function and activity of dharmas (this cause producing that effect, this faculty perceiving that object, all the causal relationships conventional discourse depends on, though in ultimate truth even conditioned arising is, in the end, illusion-like and without fixed substance). Master Cháng Bǎi notes the first two belong to the conventional truth of imagined self and dharmas, while the third belongs to the conventional truth of dependent arising specifically — three distinct ways ordinary language operates, all of them provisionally necessary even though none of them survives scrutiny at the level of ultimate truth.

110. Section: The Ultimate, and the Object of the Factors of Awakening

The mark of the ultimate should be known as the teaching of the sevenfold suchness. The mark of the object of the factors of awakening should be known as the teaching of every kind of knowable matter without exception.

Chinese Source

勝義相者，當知宣說七種真如故。菩提分法所緣相者，當知宣說遍一切種所知事故。

Ru-Yi Translation

The mark of the ultimate should be known as the teaching of the sevenfold suchness. The mark of the object of the factors of awakening should be known as the teaching of every kind of knowable matter without exception.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

The second mark, the ultimate, is once again the sevenfold suchness already treated at length in this fascicle's own citation of the yoga chapter — the suchness of flow, characteristics, consciousness-only, and the four truths under their alternate names — the single teaching every treatise's discussion of ultimate reality ultimately returns to. The third mark, the object of the factors of awakening, covers every kind of object a bodhisattva's practice actually engages: the five purifying-conduct objects (impurity, loving-kindness, dependent arising, the elements' distinctions, and mindfulness of breathing), the five skillful-means objects (skill in the aggregates, elements, sense-fields, dependent arising, and the possible and impossible), and the objects for purifying affliction through either the mundane path (contemplating impurity) or the supramundane path (the four noble truths) — the entire range of objects the thirty-seven factors of awakening are actually cultivated upon, generating the wisdom that discerns every one of their distinct aspects.

111. Section: The Eightfold Contemplation Begins — Reality Itself Is the Suchness of All Dharmas

The mark of practice should be known as the teaching of the eightfold contemplation. What is meant by the eightfold contemplation? First, reality itself; second, establishment; third, fault; fourth, excellent quality; fifth, guiding principle; sixth, the flow of causation; seventh, reasoning; and eighth, the general and the particular. Reality itself is the suchness of all dharmas.

Chinese Source

行相者，當知宣說八行觀故。云何名為八行觀耶？一者、諦實故；二者、安立故；三者、過失故；四者、功德故；五者、理趣故；六者、流轉故；七者、道理故；八者、總別故。諦實者，謂諸法真如。

Ru-Yi Translation

The mark of practice should be known as the teaching of the eightfold contemplation. What is meant by the eightfold contemplation? First, reality itself; second, establishment; third, fault; fourth, excellent quality; fifth, guiding principle; sixth, the flow of causation; seventh, reasoning; and eighth, the general and the particular. Reality itself is the suchness of all dharmas.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

The fourth mark, practice, unpacks into eight modes of contemplation every treatise's own reasoning follows. The first, reality itself, is explicitly not the established conventional truth but the unestablished, wordless suchness realized directly — citing the Decisive-Discussion chapter's own definition of the perfected nature (juan 73): the suchness of all dharmas, the very domain a noble one's wisdom operates within and takes as its object, whose realization brings liberation, freedom from the bondage of both characteristics and coarseness, and the arising of every excellent quality. Master Cháng Bǎi glosses "suchness" itself as what pervades every dharma without exception, unchanging and universal — this is thus, that is thus, everywhere and always thus — the single underlying reality every treatise's argument, however varied its surface content, ultimately points back toward.

112. Section: Establishment — Persons, the Three Natures, and the Four Kinds of Answer

Establishment means: establishing persons; or establishing the imagined nature of dharmas; or establishing categorical answer, analytical answer, counter-question, and setting aside; or establishing the distinction between the hidden and the revealed manner of teaching.

Chinese Source

安立者，謂或安立補特伽羅，或復安立諸法遍計所執自性，或復安立一向、分別、反問、置記，或復安立隱密、顯了記別差別。

Ru-Yi Translation

Establishment means: establishing persons; or establishing the imagined nature of dharmas; or establishing categorical answer, analytical answer, counter-question, and setting aside; or establishing the distinction between the hidden and the revealed manner of teaching.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

The second contemplation, establishment, covers everything a treatise provisionally sets up in order to teach. Establishing persons covers the many ways beings are classified — the sixty-two kinds already introduced in this treatise's opening ground, the three realms and five destinies, the thirteen kinds of person distinguished by their relation to the four truths, or the threefold grading of inferior, middling, and superior practitioner by their conduct. Establishing the imagined nature covers the full threefold framework itself — the imagined, dependent, and perfected natures together, dependent arising and its provisional reality distinguished from what is merely imagined and from what is truly perfected. Establishing the four kinds of answer covers the traditional fourfold classification of how a question should be met: a categorical answer, given directly when the question itself accords with reality (all conditioned things are impermanent, all contaminated things are suffering); an analytical answer, given when the matter is too varied to state in one stroke (the ripening of karma, which differs case by case depending on how mixed wholesome and unwholesome actions actually are); a counter-question, given when the question itself is unclear or asked in bad faith, clarifying exactly what is being asked before answering it (asked whether a human is superior or inferior, one first asks: compared to what?); and setting aside, given when a question rests on a false premise no direct answer could ever resolve, such as the fourteen unanswerable questions about the world's eternity or the Tathāgata's existence after death, or whether the aggregates and the person are one or different — questions best met with silence rather than an answer that would only reinforce the false premise. Establishing hidden and revealed teaching, finally, covers the treatises' own account of provisional versus definitive teaching — the śrāvaka teaching, or the second turning's emptiness teaching, read as hidden in intent, and the third turning's consciousness-only teaching read as fully revealed — together with the Buddha's own prophecies of a disciple's future buddhahood, specifying the eon, the buddha-land, the name, and the lifespan involved. Together these four kinds of establishment cover the full range of provisional structure a treatise's reasoning relies on before arriving at reality itself.

113. Section: Fault and Excellent Quality — Boundless Faults in Every Defiled Dharma, Boundless Benefits in Every Pure One

Fault means: I teach that every defiled dharma carries boundless distinct faults and calamities, through countless gateways. Excellent quality means: I teach that every pure dharma carries boundless distinct benefits, through countless gateways.

Chinese Source

過失者，謂我宣說諸雜染法，有無量門差別過患。
功德者，謂我宣說諸清淨法，有無量門差別勝利。

Ru-Yi Translation

Fault means: I teach that every defiled dharma carries boundless distinct faults and calamities, through countless gateways.
Excellent quality means: I teach that every pure dharma carries boundless distinct benefits, through countless gateways.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

The third contemplation, fault, covers every treatise passage cataloguing the boundless faults of affliction, karma, and rebirth — the Ground of Applied-and-Sustained Thought (juan 8) alone lists them by the score: affliction first disturbs the mind, then distorts its object, entrenches the latent tendencies, and perpetuates their continuous flow; it brings harm to oneself, to others, and to both; it generates present, future, and mixed offense; it burdens this life and the next with suffering; it draws one ever further from nirvāṇa's peace; it causes wealth to waste away, robs one of composure before an assembly, spreads an ill reputation in every direction, brings deep regret at the moment of death, and casts one down into the hells after the body perishes — a survey of harm without end. The fourth, excellent quality, mirrors it exactly: the boundless benefits of the four fruits, the thirty-seven factors of awakening, the paths of seeing and cultivation, and — as the Bodhisattva Ground (juan 42) specifies for a bodhisattva who perfects the pāramitā of pure precepts — five particular benefits: the constant protection of the buddhas of the ten directions; great joy at the moment of death; being born again and again among fellow holders of equal or greater precepts as one's companions and teachers; the accomplishment of a boundless treasury of merit sufficient to perfect the pāramitā of precepts; and the constant, natural possession of pure precepts in this life and the next. Every pure dharma's excellence differs by stage — a first fruit's purity is partial, a buddha's alone complete, and each of the ten grounds surpasses the one before it — which is why the text calls these benefits not merely many but "distinct," graded rather than uniform.

114. Section: Guiding Principle — The Six Gateways That Unlock the Buddha's Teaching

Guiding principle should be known as sixfold. First, the guiding principle of true meaning; second, the guiding principle of attainment; third, the guiding principle of instruction; fourth, the guiding principle of transcending the two extremes; fifth, the guiding principle of the inconceivable; and sixth, the guiding principle of intent.

Chinese Source

理趣者，當知六種。一者、真義理趣，二者、證得理趣，三者、教導理趣，四者、遠離二邊理趣，五者、不可思議理趣，六者、意趣理趣。

Ru-Yi Translation

Guiding principle should be known as sixfold. First, the guiding principle of true meaning; second, the guiding principle of attainment; third, the guiding principle of instruction; fourth, the guiding principle of transcending the two extremes; fifth, the guiding principle of the inconceivable; and sixth, the guiding principle of intent.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

The fifth contemplation, guiding principle, matches the six gateways the Decisive-Discussion chapter (juan 64) offers as the master key to the entire canon — Master Yìn Shùn's own method of "studying the Dharma by means of the Dharma." True meaning unfolds into six kinds of reality: what the world simply agrees is real (this is a table); what reason establishes as real (the twelvefold sense-bases, good-cause-good-fruit); what a śrāvaka's afflictive-obstruction-free wisdom discloses (the four truths); what a bodhisattva's cognitive-obstruction-free wisdom discloses (the emptiness of dharmas); reality as conventionally formulated in words (the four truths as taught); and reality beyond all formulation, where words and thought both give out (suchness itself). Attainment covers four gateways — the ripening of ordinary karma, the śrāvaka path's five attainments, the pratyekabuddha's three, and the bodhisattva path's eight, from the arising of the resolve through the attainment unique to a buddha. Instruction sorts every teaching by which basket transmits it — discourse and discipline together, treatise-matrices alone, or a blend of both, unfolding further into ten or twelve traditional teaching-types (provisional versus definitive, conventional versus ultimate, hidden versus revealed, and so on). Transcending the two extremes names the three classic pairs a teaching steers between — increase and decrease, permanence and annihilation, pleasure and pain — the gateway by which true meaning itself becomes accessible. The inconceivable names six domains thought should not attempt to grasp — the self, sentient beings, the world, karmic ripening, a meditator's own meditative object, and a buddha's domain — attempting to reason them out only disturbs the mind and yields no merit; some texts add three more (the fourteen unanswerable questions, what is not true dharma, and the full sweep of affliction) for nine. Intent names sixteen distinct rhetorical purposes behind a given saying — to demonstrate, to draw someone away from error, to exhort, to praise, to rejoice, to lead one in, to cut off doubt, to bring to maturity, and more — the same statement serving entirely different ends depending on which intent moves it. Cháng Bǎi notes the six interlock: the first three gateways are each clarified by one of the last three — true meaning by transcending the two extremes, attainment by the inconceivable (realized only by direct proof, never by argument), and instruction by intent.

115. Section: The Flow of Causation — The Three Times, the Three Conditioned Marks, and the Four Conditions

The flow of causation refers to the three times, the three conditioned marks, and the four kinds of condition.

Chinese Source

流轉者，所謂三世三有為相及四種緣。

Ru-Yi Translation

The flow of causation refers to the three times, the three conditioned marks, and the four kinds of condition.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

The sixth contemplation, the flow of causation, gathers the treatise's own account of how time, arising, and causal support together explain saṃsāra's turning. The three times — past, future, present — are established, per the Ground of Mind (juan 3), by seed and by fruit together: a seed already ripened into manifest activity, whether that activity has already passed or is currently occurring, counts as past or present; a seed not yet ripened counts as future. The three conditioned marks resolve the classical fourfold scheme (arising, aging, abiding, and impermanence) into three, since abiding and change-through-abiding are both only ever found in the present moment: arising is what the future contributes (a dharma, previously nonexistent, newly comes to be); cessation is what the past contributes (a dharma, once arisen, has already fallen away); and abiding-and-change is what the present alone contributes, since only now can a thing be said to persist at all, and only now can its moment-to-moment alteration be observed. The Compiled Treatise (juan 88) further distinguishes two scales at which this turning is tracked — across an entire lifetime (birth to death) and moment to moment (each instant newly arising, not persisting past its own instant) — with "change" itself splitting into a gentler, resembling continuity and a sharper, unresembling one. The four conditions — the causal condition (the seed itself), the condition of immediate succession (one moment of mind vacating its place for the next), the objective-support condition (whatever a mind or mental factor takes as its object), and the dominant condition (every other supporting circumstance — a sense-faculty, an accompanying mental factor, or simply not obstructing) — together account for how any mental or physical event actually comes to arise: a mental event needs all four, a physical event needs only the causal and dominant conditions. The causal condition alone is described as "both cause and condition"; the other three are conditions but not, strictly, causes.

116. Section: Reasoning — Four Kinds, and the First Two: Correlation and Function

Reasoning should be known as fourfold. First, the reasoning of correlation; second, the reasoning of function; third, the reasoning of proof; and fourth, the reasoning of what is simply so. The reasoning of correlation is: whatever cause or condition gives rise to formations, and whatever gives rise to their corresponding designation — this is called the reasoning of correlation. The reasoning of function is: whatever cause or condition enables dharmas to be attained, or accomplished, or brought to completion, or, once arisen, to perform their function — this is called the reasoning of function.

Chinese Source

道理者，當知四種。一者、觀待道理，二者、作用道理，三者、證成道理，四者、法爾道理。
觀待道理者，謂若因、若緣能生諸行，及起隨說，如是名為觀待道理。
作用道理者，謂若因、若緣能得諸法，或能成辦，或復生已作諸業用，如是名為作用道理。

Ru-Yi Translation

Reasoning should be known as fourfold. First, the reasoning of correlation; second, the reasoning of function; third, the reasoning of proof; and fourth, the reasoning of what is simply so.
The reasoning of correlation is: whatever cause or condition gives rise to formations, and whatever gives rise to their corresponding designation — this is called the reasoning of correlation.
The reasoning of function is: whatever cause or condition enables dharmas to be attained, or accomplished, or brought to completion, or, once arisen, to perform their function — this is called the reasoning of function.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

The seventh contemplation, reasoning, is the treatise's own fourfold logic already met in the Śrāvaka Ground (juan 25), used there to guide insight practice and now generalized to the whole canon. Correlation covers two senses of "depending on": arising-correlation, where every conditioned aggregate depends on its causes and conditions to arise at all (a mental event needing all four conditions, a physical dharma needing only the causal and dominant conditions); and designation-correlation, where a thing, once arisen, depends on name, phrase, and syllable to be spoken of, thought about, or seen at all — perception generates conception, conception generates speech, and speech generates the reports of what is seen, heard, sensed, or known. Function then covers what a dharma, once it has arisen through those same causes and conditions, actually does — the eye sees form, the ear hears sound, and so through all six faculties and their objects, each with its own irreducible function, exactly as the Śrāvaka Ground lays out. The treatise elsewhere (juan 5) asks the same question four ways of any arisen, attained, established, accomplished, or functioning dharma: what precedes it, what constitutes it, what conjoins with it, and what results — a single analytic template applied uniformly across every kind of arising, whether of a physical dharma, a liberation, a debate's conclusion, a craft's product, or a rebirth sustained by food.

117. Section: Reasoning of Proof — Purity's Five Marks Named, Beginning with What Is Directly Seen

The reasoning of proof is: whatever cause or condition brings about the establishment of a thesis, a statement, or a designated meaning, and produces genuine awakening to it — this is called the reasoning of proof. This reasoning is, in brief, of two kinds: pure and impure. Five marks constitute purity; seven marks constitute impurity. What are the five marks constituting purity? First, the mark of what is directly seen; second, the mark of what rests on what is directly seen; third, the mark drawn from an analogy of the same kind; fourth, the mark of perfected accomplishment; and fifth, the mark of well-purified verbal teaching. The mark of what is directly seen is: that every formation is impermanent, that every formation is suffering, and that every dharma is without self — these are apprehended by the world's own direct perception. Things of this kind are called the mark of what is directly seen.

Chinese Source

證成道理者，謂若因、若緣，能令所立、所說、所標義得成立，令正覺悟，如是名為證成道理。又此道理略有二種。一者、清淨，二者、不清淨。由五種相名為清淨，由七種相名不清淨。云何由五種相名為清淨？一者、現見所得相，二者、依止現見所得相，三者、自類譬喻所引相，四者、圓成實相，五者、善清淨言教相。現見所得相者，謂一切行皆無常性，一切行皆是苦性，一切法皆無我性，此為世間現量所得。如是等類，是名現見所得相。

Ru-Yi Translation

The reasoning of proof is: whatever cause or condition brings about the establishment of a thesis, a statement, or a designated meaning, and produces genuine awakening to it — this is called the reasoning of proof. This reasoning is, in brief, of two kinds: pure and impure. Five marks constitute purity; seven marks constitute impurity. What are the five marks constituting purity? First, the mark of what is directly seen; second, the mark of what rests on what is directly seen; third, the mark drawn from an analogy of the same kind; fourth, the mark of perfected accomplishment; and fifth, the mark of well-purified verbal teaching. The mark of what is directly seen is: that every formation is impermanent, that every formation is suffering, and that every dharma is without self — these are apprehended by the world's own direct perception. Things of this kind are called the mark of what is directly seen.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

The third of the four reasonings, proof, is this treatise's own logic (因明, hetuvidya) — how a thesis gets established well enough to genuinely convince another mind, corresponding to what secular learning calls logic. It rests on dependent arising itself: because things are bound together by cause and condition, a thesis can be proven through its reason and example; were nothing so bound, no reason could ever prove anything. Five marks make a proof pure. The first, what is directly seen, corresponds to direct perception (pratyakṣa) — the coarse impermanence, suffering, and selflessness anyone can observe simply by watching birth, aging, sickness, and death unfold, with no inference required at all.

118. Section: The Mark of What Rests on Direct Seeing — Momentariness, Future Lives, and Karma That Is Never Lost

The mark of what rests on what is directly seen is: that every formation possesses the nature of momentariness, that there exists a nature belonging to other lives, and that pure and impure karma possesses a nature that is never lost or destroyed. This is because the coarse impermanence that depends on it is itself directly apprehended; because the manifold distinctions among sentient beings, resting on their manifold karma, are directly apprehended; and because the pleasure and pain of sentient beings, resting on pure and impure karma, are directly apprehended. Owing to these causes and conditions, inference can be drawn concerning what is not directly seen. Things of this kind are called the mark of what rests on what is directly seen.

Chinese Source

依止現見所得相者，謂一切行皆剎那性，他世有性，淨不淨業無失壞性。由彼能依麤無常性現可得故；由諸有情種種差別，依種種業現可得故；由諸有情若樂、若苦，淨不淨業以為依止現可得故。由此因緣，於不現見可為比度。如是等類，是名依止現見所得相。

Ru-Yi Translation

The mark of what rests on what is directly seen is: that every formation possesses the nature of momentariness, that there exists a nature belonging to other lives, and that pure and impure karma possesses a nature that is never lost or destroyed. This is because the coarse impermanence that depends on it is itself directly apprehended; because the manifold distinctions among sentient beings, resting on their manifold karma, are directly apprehended; and because the pleasure and pain of sentient beings, resting on pure and impure karma, are directly apprehended. Owing to these causes and conditions, inference can be drawn concerning what is not directly seen. Things of this kind are called the mark of what rests on what is directly seen.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

The second mark corresponds to inference (anumāna) — reasoning from what direct perception already establishes to a subtler truth direct perception alone cannot reach. Three inferences run in parallel, one per each of the three times: the coarse impermanence everyone directly observes (present) implies a subtler, moment-to-moment impermanence beneath it, since coarse change could never arise without finer change constantly underway; the visible diversity of karmic fortune among beings (their present differences) implies that a future life exists to receive karma not yet ripened, since present causes plainly reach beyond the present; and the pleasure and pain beings presently experience, tracing back to virtuous or unwholesome conduct, implies that past karma is never simply lost, since conditions once gathered and dispersed still leave their seed intact in the storehouse consciousness, ready to ripen "though a hundred eons pass." Each case takes something coarse and directly seen as the dependent mark, and infers from it something finer and not directly seen as the mark it rests upon.

119. Section: Analogy, Perfected Accomplishment, and Well-Purified Teaching — The Remaining Three Clean Marks

The mark drawn from an analogy of the same kind is: drawing, from among the inward and outward masses of formations, on birth-and-death as commonly known and apprehended by the world as an analogy; drawing on the various marks of suffering in birth and so forth as commonly known and apprehended by the world as an analogy; drawing on the mark of having-no-mastery as commonly known and apprehended by the world as an analogy; and further, with respect to what is outward, drawing on decline and flourishing as commonly known and apprehended by the world as an analogy. Things of this kind should be known as the mark drawn from an analogy of the same kind. The mark of perfected accomplishment is: when the mark of what is directly seen, the mark of what rests on what is directly seen, and the mark drawn from an analogy of the same kind, taken together, are able to establish decisively what is to be proven — this should be known as the mark of perfected accomplishment. The mark of well-purified verbal teaching is: what is proclaimed by an omniscient one, such as the saying that nirvāṇa is ultimate tranquility. Things of this kind should be known as the mark of well-purified verbal teaching.

Chinese Source

自類譬喻所引相者，謂於內外諸行聚中，引諸世間共所了知所得生死以為譬喻，引諸世間共所了知所得生等種種苦相以為譬喻，引諸世間共所了知所得不自在相以為譬喻；又復於外，引諸世間共所了知所得衰盛以為譬喻。如是等類，當知是名自類譬喻所引相。圓成實相者，謂即如是現見所得相，若依止現見所得相，若自類譬喻所得相，於所成立決定能成，當知是名圓成實相。善清淨言教相者，謂一切智者之所宣說，如言涅槃究竟寂靜。如是等類，當知是名善清淨言教相。

Ru-Yi Translation

The mark drawn from an analogy of the same kind is: drawing, from among the inward and outward masses of formations, on birth-and-death as commonly known and apprehended by the world as an analogy; drawing on the various marks of suffering in birth and so forth as commonly known and apprehended by the world as an analogy; drawing on the mark of having-no-mastery as commonly known and apprehended by the world as an analogy; and further, with respect to what is outward, drawing on decline and flourishing as commonly known and apprehended by the world as an analogy. Things of this kind should be known as the mark drawn from an analogy of the same kind.
The mark of perfected accomplishment is: when the mark of what is directly seen, the mark of what rests on what is directly seen, and the mark drawn from an analogy of the same kind, taken together, are able to establish decisively what is to be proven — this should be known as the mark of perfected accomplishment.
The mark of well-purified verbal teaching is: what is proclaimed by an omniscient one, such as the saying that nirvāṇa is ultimate tranquility. Things of this kind should be known as the mark of well-purified verbal teaching.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

The third mark corresponds to the analogy of shared kind (a homologous example): four analogies, each drawn from what everyone already agrees on, back up the same three theses — birth-and-death itself proves that all formations are impermanent (whatever arises must perish); the many faces of suffering in birth, aging, sickness, and death prove that all formations are suffering; the plain fact of having no mastery over any of it proves that all dharmas are without self; and, turning outward, the rise and fall of mountains and rivers proves the same impermanence for the insentient world. The fourth, perfected accomplishment, is simply what results once the first three marks converge without any of the thirty-three classical flaws a thesis, reason, or example can suffer from — nine flaws in the thesis, fourteen in the reason, ten in the example — so that reason and example together truly establish what was claimed. The fifth, well-purified verbal teaching, corresponds to scriptural authority (āgama): only an omniscient one's word — a buddha's, whose knowledge of every realm, every phenomenon, every category, and every era is unobstructed — counts as this kind of proof, exemplified by teachings such as "nirvāṇa is ultimate tranquility."

120. Section: The Marks of an Omniscient One — Renown, the Thirty-Two Marks, the Ten Powers, the Four Fearlessnesses, and the Noble Path Made Manifest

World-Honored One! The marks of an omniscient one — how many kinds should be known? Good son! In brief, there are five. First, if one appears in the world, the report of omniscience is heard everywhere without exception; second, one is endowed with the thirty-two marks of a great being; third, one is endowed with the ten powers, able to cut off every doubt of every sentient being; fourth, one is endowed with the four fearlessnesses, proclaiming the true Dharma, never overcome by any other doctrine, yet able to overcome every wrong doctrine; and fifth, within the well-taught Dharma and discipline, the eightfold noble path and the four kinds of ascetic are all made manifestly attainable. Because of birth, because of marks, because of the cutting off of the net of doubt, because of not being overcome by others while overcoming others, and because the noble path and its ascetics are made manifestly attainable — these five, it should be known, are called the marks of omniscience. Good son! This is how the reasoning of proof, through direct perception, through inference, through scriptural authority, through analogy, and through perfected accomplishment — through these five marks — constitutes purity.

Chinese Source

世尊！一切智者相，當知有幾種？善男子！略有五種。一者、若有出現世間，一切智聲無不普聞；二者、成就三十二種大丈夫相；三者、具足十力，能斷一切眾生一切疑惑；四者、具足四無所畏，宣說正法，不為一切他論所伏，而能摧伏一切邪論；五者、於善說法毗奈耶中，八支聖道、四沙門等皆現可得。如是生故、相故、斷疑網故、非他所伏能伏他故、聖道沙門現可得故，如是五種，當知名為一切智相。善男子！如是證成道理，由現量故、由比量故、由聖教量故、由譬喻故、由圓成實故，由五種相名為清淨。

Ru-Yi Translation

World-Honored One! The marks of an omniscient one — how many kinds should be known? Good son! In brief, there are five. First, if one appears in the world, the report of omniscience is heard everywhere without exception; second, one is endowed with the thirty-two marks of a great being; third, one is endowed with the ten powers, able to cut off every doubt of every sentient being; fourth, one is endowed with the four fearlessnesses, proclaiming the true Dharma, never overcome by any other doctrine, yet able to overcome every wrong doctrine; and fifth, within the well-taught Dharma and discipline, the eightfold noble path and the four kinds of ascetic are all made manifestly attainable.
Because of birth, because of marks, because of the cutting off of the net of doubt, because of not being overcome by others while overcoming others, and because the noble path and its ascetics are made manifestly attainable — these five, it should be known, are called the marks of omniscience.
Good son! This is how the reasoning of proof, through direct perception, through inference, through scriptural authority, through analogy, and through perfected accomplishment — through these five marks — constitutes purity.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Master Cháng Bǎi treats "well-purified verbal teaching" as calling for its own extended account of exactly whose word counts as this fifth, scriptural mark — Mañjuśrī's question opens a full survey of the marks of omniscience itself, i.e., of the Buddha. The ten powers (Bodhisattva Ground, juan 49), each said to exceed anything a śrāvaka or pratyekabuddha possesses: the power of knowing right from wrong causation; the power of knowing each being's own karma across all three times; the power of knowing every meditative absorption and its degree of purity; the power of knowing the relative sharpness of each being's faculties; the power of knowing the varied inclinations behind each being's aspiration; the power of knowing every latent affliction dormant in each being; the power of knowing every path any kind of conduct leads to, worldly or transcendent; the power of recollecting past lives without limit; the power of the heavenly eye, seeing where beings will be reborn; and the power of knowing whose contaminants are exhausted and whose are not — together forming the total scope of a buddha's causal, contemplative, and liberating knowledge. The four fearlessnesses (Bodhisattva Ground, juan 50) are the four things a buddha can proclaim of himself before any assembly without a trace of hesitation: perfect awakening (having forever ended the cognitive obstruction, uniquely among all beings); the exhaustion of every contaminant (having forever ended the afflictive obstruction, here shared with liberated śrāvakas); teaching the path out of suffering to those who seek it; and naming, without fear of refutation, the very obstacles that block that path. The first two proclaim self-benefit accomplished; the last two proclaim benefit for others accomplished — together the full, fearless confidence of one whose realization no opposing argument can actually touch, whatever hostility it provokes. The closing mark, the eightfold noble path and four kinds of ascetic "made manifestly attainable," points to the treatise's own fourfold classification of what a monastic can be — one who has attained the path, one who teaches it, one who simply lives it through sustained practice, or one who has broken it — none of which, the text notes, exist as such outside a teaching where the eightfold path itself has actually been established.

121. Section: Impurity's Seven Marks Named — And the Two Hardest to Grasp, Where the Reason Proves Too Much or Too Little

What are the seven marks constituting impurity? First, the mark obtainable in a remaining portion of the same kind; second, the mark obtainable in a remaining portion of a different kind; third, the mark obtainable in the whole of the same kind; fourth, the mark obtainable in the whole of a different kind; fifth, the mark drawn from an analogy of a different kind; sixth, the mark of what is not perfected accomplishment; and seventh, the mark of what is not well-purified verbal teaching. If a given dharma has the nature of being cognized by mental consciousness with respect to all dharmas whatsoever, this is called the mark obtainable in the whole of the same kind. If a given dharma's characteristic, nature, function, dharma-quality, and cause-and-result all differ, and, following each of these several points of difference, prove decisively and thoroughly divergent in every respect, this is called the mark obtainable in the whole of a different kind.

Chinese Source

云何由七種相名不清淨？一者、此餘同類可得相，二者、此餘異類可得相，三者、一切同類可得相，四者、一切異類可得相，五者、異類譬喻所引相，六者、非圓成實相，七者、非善清淨言教相。若一切法意識所識性，是名一切同類可得相。若一切法相、性、業、法、因果異相，由隨如是一一異相，決定展轉各各異相，是名一切異類可得相。

Ru-Yi Translation

What are the seven marks constituting impurity? First, the mark obtainable in a remaining portion of the same kind; second, the mark obtainable in a remaining portion of a different kind; third, the mark obtainable in the whole of the same kind; fourth, the mark obtainable in the whole of a different kind; fifth, the mark drawn from an analogy of a different kind; sixth, the mark of what is not perfected accomplishment; and seventh, the mark of what is not well-purified verbal teaching.
If a given dharma has the nature of being cognized by mental consciousness with respect to all dharmas whatsoever, this is called the mark obtainable in the whole of the same kind.
If a given dharma's characteristic, nature, function, dharma-quality, and cause-and-result all differ, and, following each of these several points of difference, prove decisively and thoroughly divergent in every respect, this is called the mark obtainable in the whole of a different kind.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

Where the five clean marks establish a thesis cleanly, these seven mirror them into flaws — corresponding, in the later terminology of Buddhist logic, to the nine kinds of inconclusive or contrary reason (九句因) classified around whether a proposed reason is present in the same-category examples, the different-category examples, both, or neither. The first two marks concern a reason present only in part of the matching category ("this remaining portion") while also reaching into the opposing category — too entangled to decide anything either way. The third and fourth marks, illustrated here, concern a reason that reaches everywhere or nowhere at once: proposing "sound is permanent, because it is cognized by mental consciousness" fails because literally everything — the permanent space taken as example and the impermanent vase taken as counter-example alike — is cognized by mental consciousness, so the reason proves both sides equally and therefore neither (an example of what logicians call the "common" inconclusive reason); proposing "sound is permanent, because it is audible" fails the opposite way, since neither the example of space nor of a vase is itself "audible" in the relevant sense, so the reason touches no example on any side and again proves nothing (the "uncommon" inconclusive reason). The fifth mark reverses the third clean mark, drawing its example only from what actually opposes the thesis; the sixth and seventh simply name the failure of the fourth and fifth clean marks — no genuine perfected accomplishment, no genuine scriptural backing.

122. Section: Where Impurity Bites, and What Is Simply So — Closing the Eightfold Contemplation Entirely

Good son! If, within the mark obtainable in a remaining portion of the same kind, and within its analogy, there is found the mark of the whole of a different kind, then, owing to this cause and condition, what is to be proven is not decisively established — this too is called the mark of what is not perfected accomplishment. And if, within the mark obtainable in a remaining portion of a different kind, and within its analogy, there is found the mark of the whole of the same kind, then, owing to this cause and condition, what is to be proven is not decided — this too is called the mark of what is not perfected accomplishment. Because it is not perfected accomplishment, reasoning is not well examined as pure; because it is impure, it should not be cultivated. Whether it is the mark drawn from an analogy of a different kind, or the mark of what is not well-purified verbal teaching, both should be known as impure in their very nature. The reasoning of what is simply so is: that whether the Tathāgata appears in the world or does not appear in the world, the nature of dharmas abides constant, established as the abiding of dharmas and the realm of dharmas — this is called the reasoning of what is simply so. The general and the particular is: that a single statement is first made in general, and afterward, through successive statements one by one, its particulars are fully distinguished and made manifest.

Chinese Source

善男子！若於此餘同類可得相及譬喻中，有一切異類相者，由此因緣，於所成立非決定故，是名非圓成實相。又於此餘異類可得相及譬喻中，有一切同類相者，由此因緣，於所成立不決定故，亦名非圓成實相。非圓成實故，非善觀察清淨道理；不清淨故，不應修習。若異類譬喻所引相、若非善清淨言教相，當知體性皆不清淨。法爾道理者，謂如來出世，若不出世，法性安住法住法界，是名法爾道理。總別者，謂先總說一句法已，後後諸句差別分別究竟顯了。

Ru-Yi Translation

Good son! If, within the mark obtainable in a remaining portion of the same kind, and within its analogy, there is found the mark of the whole of a different kind, then, owing to this cause and condition, what is to be proven is not decisively established — this too is called the mark of what is not perfected accomplishment. And if, within the mark obtainable in a remaining portion of a different kind, and within its analogy, there is found the mark of the whole of the same kind, then, owing to this cause and condition, what is to be proven is not decided — this too is called the mark of what is not perfected accomplishment. Because it is not perfected accomplishment, reasoning is not well examined as pure; because it is impure, it should not be cultivated.
Whether it is the mark drawn from an analogy of a different kind, or the mark of what is not well-purified verbal teaching, both should be known as impure in their very nature.
The reasoning of what is simply so is: that whether the Tathāgata appears in the world or does not appear in the world, the nature of dharmas abides constant, established as the abiding of dharmas and the realm of dharmas — this is called the reasoning of what is simply so.
The general and the particular is: that a single statement is first made in general, and afterward, through successive statements one by one, its particulars are fully distinguished and made manifest.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

The closing verdict simply confirms what the seven marks already implied: any thesis resting on an entangled, over-reaching, or under-reaching reason cannot be called pure reasoning and should not be relied upon in practice. The fourth reasoning, what is simply so (法爾道理), needs no proof at all — it names the bedrock beneath every other reasoning: that all formations are impermanent, that all dharmas are without self, is not a truth the Buddha invented but one that "abides constant" whether or not any buddha ever appears to teach it. Cháng Bǎi glosses the three terms as three angles on one fact — nature (法性) is how things simply and originally are, apart from any description; abiding (法住) is that same nature as fixed in unmistaken words; and the realm of dharmas (法界) is that same nature understood as the cause from which a thing's every observed feature follows — three names, one underlying suchness, distinguished only by whether or how language is being used to point at it. The eighth and final contemplation, the general and the particular, is simply this treatise's own recurring compositional method — state a matter briefly first, then unfold its particulars one clause at a time — exemplified by nothing more exotic than the very passage the reader has just finished, where "practice" was first named in one line and then unpacked into all eight of these contemplations in turn. With this, the eightfold contemplation (八行觀) — reality itself, establishment, fault, excellent quality, guiding principle, the flow of causation, reasoning, and the general-and-particular — is now complete in full: the fourth of the treatise's eleven marks of the fundamental matrices (本母), the largest and most technical of the eleven, finally closes here.

123. Section: The Remaining Seven Marks — Own-Nature, Fruit, Reception, Obstruction, Accordance, Fault, and Benefit — Closing the Eleven Marks of the Fundamental Matrices Entirely

The mark of own-nature is: what I teach as possessing activity and possessing an object, all that is capable of grasping the factors of awakening, namely the foundations of mindfulness and so forth — this is called the mark of its own-nature. The mark of its fruit is: the cutting off of afflictions, whether worldly or transcendent, and the worldly and transcendent fruits and virtues thereby brought forth — this is called the mark of attaining its fruit. The mark of its reception and disclosure is: receiving that very fruit through liberating wisdom, and broadly proclaiming and disclosing it to others — this is called the mark of its reception and disclosure. The mark of its obstructing dharmas is: whatever defiled dharmas are capable of obstructing the cultivation of the factors of awakening — this is called the mark of its obstructing dharmas. The mark of its accordant dharmas is: whatever dharmas are of great service to that same cultivation — this is called the mark of its accordant dharmas. The mark of its fault is: the very faults belonging to those obstructing dharmas — this is called the mark of its fault. The mark of its benefit is: the very virtues belonging to those accordant dharmas — this is called the mark of its benefit.

Chinese Source

自性相者，謂我所說有行、有緣所有能取菩提分法，謂念住等，如是名為彼自性相。彼果相者，謂若世間、若出世間諸煩惱斷，及所引發世出世間諸果功德，如是名為得彼果相。彼領受開示相者，謂即於彼以解脫智而領受之，及廣為他宣說開示，如是名為彼領受開示相。彼障礙法相者，謂即於修菩提分法，能隨障礙諸染汙法，是名彼障礙法相。彼隨順法相者，謂即於彼多所作法，是名彼隨順法相。彼過患相者，當知即彼諸障礙法所有過失，是名彼過患相。彼勝利相者，當知即彼諸隨順法所有功德，是名彼勝利相。

Ru-Yi Translation

The mark of own-nature is: what I teach as possessing activity and possessing an object, all that is capable of grasping the factors of awakening, namely the foundations of mindfulness and so forth — this is called the mark of its own-nature.
The mark of its fruit is: the cutting off of afflictions, whether worldly or transcendent, and the worldly and transcendent fruits and virtues thereby brought forth — this is called the mark of attaining its fruit.
The mark of its reception and disclosure is: receiving that very fruit through liberating wisdom, and broadly proclaiming and disclosing it to others — this is called the mark of its reception and disclosure.
The mark of its obstructing dharmas is: whatever defiled dharmas are capable of obstructing the cultivation of the factors of awakening — this is called the mark of its obstructing dharmas.
The mark of its accordant dharmas is: whatever dharmas are of great service to that same cultivation — this is called the mark of its accordant dharmas.
The mark of its fault is: the very faults belonging to those obstructing dharmas — this is called the mark of its fault.
The mark of its benefit is: the very virtues belonging to those accordant dharmas — this is called the mark of its benefit.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

The seven remaining marks move quickly now that the eightfold contemplation has already done the heavy lifting. Own-nature names what the thirty-seven factors of awakening actually are in themselves — the four foundations of mindfulness and the rest, each an active, object-taking mental factor capable of apprehending awakening. Fruit is what cultivating them yields — the severing of affliction, worldly or transcendent, and every fruit and virtue that follows. Reception and disclosure distinguishes two moments in a teacher's realization: receiving the fruit directly through non-conceptual liberating wisdom, then turning around and proclaiming it to others through the subsequently attained wisdom that can use words. Obstructing dharmas names whatever defiled state — the five hindrances, the eight kinds of discursive thought, and the like — blocks cultivation of the awakening factors; accordant dharmas names whatever helps it along, such as the two provisions of merit and wisdom or skillful practice of calm and insight; and the final pair, fault and benefit, simply names the harm the first brings (failing to sever affliction in this life, generating karma that leads to the lower realms) and the good the second brings (the manifold virtues born of every kind of purification, worldly and transcendent). With this, all eleven marks of the fundamental matrices (本母) — the conventional, the ultimate, the object of the factors of awakening, practice (the eightfold contemplation), own-nature, fruit, reception and disclosure, obstructing dharmas, accordant dharmas, fault, and benefit — are now complete in full. This closes the fundamental matrices entirely, and with it the first sub-topic of the chapter's third great topic, the varieties of speech, having now fully treated all three kinds — discourse, discipline, and the fundamental matrices — that make up the Tathāgata's teaching.

124. Section: The Dhāraṇī-Meaning Not Shared with Heretics — No Real Function, No Person, and Why Defilement Never Truly Precedes Purity

Mañjuśrī the bodhisattva said further to the Buddha: I beseech the World-Honored One to briefly explain, for the sake of the bodhisattvas, the dhāraṇī-meaning of discourse, discipline, and the fundamental matrices that is not shared with the heretics, so that, through this dhāraṇī-meaning not shared with them, the bodhisattvas may enter into the profound hidden intent of all the dharmas the Tathāgata has taught. The Buddha said to Mañjuśrī the bodhisattva: Good son, listen closely now — I shall briefly explain for you the dhāraṇī-meaning not shared with others, so that the bodhisattvas may skillfully awaken to the words of hidden intent that I speak. Good son! Whether it is a defiled dharma or a pure dharma, I say that all of them are entirely without real function, and likewise there is no person whatsoever — because, in every respect, they are free of any thing being truly done. It is not that a defiled dharma is first defiled and afterward becomes pure, nor that a pure dharma is later pure and was formerly defiled.

Chinese Source

曼殊室利菩薩復白佛言：唯願世尊為諸菩薩略說契經、調伏、本母不共外道陀羅尼義，由此不共陀羅尼義，令諸菩薩得入如來所說諸法甚深密意。佛告曼殊室利菩薩曰：善男子！汝今諦聽！吾當為汝略說不共陀羅尼義，令諸菩薩於我所說密意言辭能善悟入。善男子！若雜染法、若清淨法，我說一切皆無作用，亦都無有補特伽羅。以一切種離所為故，非雜染法先染後淨，非清淨法後淨先染。

Ru-Yi Translation

Mañjuśrī the bodhisattva said further to the Buddha: I beseech the World-Honored One to briefly explain, for the sake of the bodhisattvas, the dhāraṇī-meaning of discourse, discipline, and the fundamental matrices that is not shared with the heretics, so that, through this dhāraṇī-meaning not shared with them, the bodhisattvas may enter into the profound hidden intent of all the dharmas the Tathāgata has taught.
The Buddha said to Mañjuśrī the bodhisattva: Good son, listen closely now — I shall briefly explain for you the dhāraṇī-meaning not shared with others, so that the bodhisattvas may skillfully awaken to the words of hidden intent that I speak.
Good son! Whether it is a defiled dharma or a pure dharma, I say that all of them are entirely without real function, and likewise there is no person whatsoever — because, in every respect, they are free of any thing being truly done. It is not that a defiled dharma is first defiled and afterward becomes pure, nor that a pure dharma is later pure and was formerly defiled.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

"Dhāraṇī" here means the total-holding of the entire teaching's essential meaning — discourse, discipline, and the fundamental matrices condensed into a single insight not available to any outsider. That insight strikes at the two pillars of every heretical system at once: real function, the belief that dharmas genuinely act and produce effects on their own account, and a real person, the belief in a substantial self that undergoes defilement and purification. Both, the Buddha says, are simply absent — not because cause and effect don't operate at all, but because "function" and "person" describe the imagined nature (遍計所執), which was never real to begin with. This is why it is wrong to picture liberation as a real defiled thing turning into a real pure thing across time, as though a substance called "the self" started out stained and ended up clean; there is no such continuous substance to begin with, only a mistaken construction imposed on the merely dependent flow of causes and conditions.

125. Section: How the False View Arises, How It Is Cut Off, and the Closing Verse — The Dhāraṇī-Meaning, and the Third Great Topic on the Varieties of Speech, Complete

Ordinary beings, clinging to their own coarse body, grasp at dharmas and persons as possessing an own-nature and distinguishing features; taking the latent view born of that false view as condition, they reckon a self and what belongs to a self, and on this basis falsely suppose "I see," "I hear," "I smell," "I taste," "I touch," "I know," "I eat," "I act," "I am defiled," "I am pure" — and so turn toward wrongful conduct of this kind. If one truly knows things as they are, one is then able to forever cut off the coarse body, attaining a state where no affliction abides, utterly purified, free of all discursive elaboration, resting on the unconditioned, without any effort whatsoever. Good son! This, know it, is called the briefly-explained dhāraṇī-meaning not shared with the heretics. At that time the World-Honored One, wishing to restate this meaning, spoke a verse, saying: All defiled and purified dharmas are without function and without a self that takes up rebirth; by my teaching they are free of any thing being done — defilement and purity are not before or after. In the coarse body, through the latent view, self and what is mine are reckoned as condition; from this the false claims "I see" and so forth arise — "I eat," "I act," "I am defiled or pure." If one truly knows things as they are, one can then forever cut off the coarse body, attaining what is free of defilement and purity, free of discursive elaboration, resting on the unconditioned, without effort.

Chinese Source

凡夫異生於麤重身執著諸法補特伽羅自性、差別，隨眠妄見以為緣故，計我我所，由此妄謂我見、我聞、我嗅、我嘗、我觸、我知、我食、我作、我染、我淨，如是等類邪加行轉。若有如實知如是者，便能永斷麤重之身，獲得一切煩惱不住，最極清淨，離諸戲論，無為依止，無有加行。善男子！當知是名略說不共陀羅尼義。爾時世尊欲重宣此義而說頌曰：一切雜染清淨法　皆無作用數取趣　由我宣說離所為　染汙清淨非先後　於麤重身隨眠見　為緣計我及我所　由此妄謂我見等　我食我為我染淨　若如實知如是者　乃能永斷麤重身　得無染淨無戲論　無為依止無加行

Ru-Yi Translation

Ordinary beings, clinging to their own coarse body, grasp at dharmas and persons as possessing an own-nature and distinguishing features; taking the latent view born of that false view as condition, they reckon a self and what belongs to a self, and on this basis falsely suppose "I see," "I hear," "I smell," "I taste," "I touch," "I know," "I eat," "I act," "I am defiled," "I am pure" — and so turn toward wrongful conduct of this kind.
If one truly knows things as they are, one is then able to forever cut off the coarse body, attaining a state where no affliction abides, utterly purified, free of all discursive elaboration, resting on the unconditioned, without any effort whatsoever.
Good son! This, know it, is called the briefly-explained dhāraṇī-meaning not shared with the heretics.
At that time the World-Honored One, wishing to restate this meaning, spoke a verse, saying:
All defiled and purified dharmas / are without function and without a self that takes up rebirth;
by my teaching they are free of any thing being done — / defilement and purity are not before or after.
In the coarse body, through the latent view, / self and what is mine are reckoned as condition;
from this the false claims "I see" and so forth arise — / "I eat," "I act," "I am defiled or pure."
If one truly knows things as they are, / one can then forever cut off the coarse body,
attaining what is free of defilement and purity, / free of discursive elaboration, resting on the unconditioned, without effort.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

The passage traces a single causal chain from delusion to liberation. Ordinary beings, clinging to their own psychophysical body — heavy with the latent seeds of afflictive and cognitive obstruction, hence "coarse" — take the dormant seeds of self-view and mistaken thinking as their condition, and so construct a self and what belongs to it out of what is, in truth, only the five aggregates in motion. Every ordinary act of perception then gets narrated in the first person — I see, I hear, I eat, I am pure, I am defiled — each pronoun smuggling in exactly the substantial self the previous section already showed to be empty. The remedy is simply seeing through the construction: once a practitioner truly knows that neither dharmas nor persons ever possessed real function or a real self to begin with, the coarse body woven from that false view is cut off forever, and what remains is undefiled, unconditioned, effortless — free of the very discursive elaboration (戲論) that manufactured the self in the first place. The closing verse restates the whole teaching in six couplets for memorization, exactly mirroring the prose. With this verse, the dhāraṇī-meaning not shared with the heretics is complete, and with it the entire third great topic of the Chapter Distinguishing Accomplished Activities — the varieties of speech (言音差別), spanning discourse, discipline, the fundamental matrices in all eleven marks, and now this closing dhāraṇī-teaching — closes in full.

126. Section: How the Tathāgata's Mind Arises Without Effort — Like One Waking from Dreamless Sleep, or Rising from Cessation

At that time Mañjuśrī the bodhisattva said further to the Buddha: World-Honored One! How should the mark of the arising of the mind of the Tathāgatas be known? The Buddha said to Mañjuśrī the bodhisattva: Good son! The Tathāgata is not manifested through the arising of mind, mentation, or consciousness; and yet the Tathāgatas have an arising of mental dharmas that is without effort — know that this is just like a magical transformation. World-Honored One! If the Dharma-body of the Tathāgatas is far removed from all effort, then, being without effort, how can there be an arising of mental dharmas? Good son! Because of the power of the skillful-means-and-wisdom effort previously cultivated, there is an arising of mind. Good son! It is like one who has entered dreamless sleep — one does not exert effort toward waking, and yet, through the force of effort previously made, one wakes again; or it is like one who abides in the attainment of cessation — one does not exert effort toward rising from that attainment, and yet, through the force of effort previously made, one still rises from it. Just as mind arises again from sleep and from the attainment of cessation, so too the Tathāgata, because of the power of the skillful-means-and-wisdom effort previously cultivated, know that there is likewise an arising of mental dharmas.

Chinese Source

爾時曼殊室利菩薩復白佛言：世尊！云何應知諸如來心生起之相？佛告曼殊室利菩薩曰：善男子！夫如來者，非心、意、識生起所顯，然諸如來有無加行心法生起，當知此事猶如變化。世尊！若諸如來法身遠離一切加行，既無加行，云何而有心法生起？善男子！先所修習方便般若加行力故，有心生起。善男子！譬如正入無心睡眠，非於覺悟而作加行，由先所作加行勢力，而復覺悟。又如正在滅盡定中，非於起定而作加行，由先所作加行勢力，還從定起。如從睡眠及滅盡定心更生起，如是如來由先修習方便般若加行力故，當知復有心法生起。

Ru-Yi Translation

At that time Mañjuśrī the bodhisattva said further to the Buddha: World-Honored One! How should the mark of the arising of the mind of the Tathāgatas be known?
The Buddha said to Mañjuśrī the bodhisattva: Good son! The Tathāgata is not manifested through the arising of mind, mentation, or consciousness; and yet the Tathāgatas have an arising of mental dharmas that is without effort — know that this is just like a magical transformation.
World-Honored One! If the Dharma-body of the Tathāgatas is far removed from all effort, then, being without effort, how can there be an arising of mental dharmas?
Good son! Because of the power of the skillful-means-and-wisdom effort previously cultivated, there is an arising of mind.
Good son! It is like one who has entered dreamless sleep — one does not exert effort toward waking, and yet, through the force of effort previously made, one wakes again; or it is like one who abides in the attainment of cessation — one does not exert effort toward rising from that attainment, and yet, through the force of effort previously made, one still rises from it. Just as mind arises again from sleep and from the attainment of cessation, so too the Tathāgata, because of the power of the skillful-means-and-wisdom effort previously cultivated, know that there is likewise an arising of mental dharmas.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

The fourth great topic opens on a genuine puzzle: a buddha's Dharma-body is famously "without discursive elaboration, without anything left to do" (established already in this chapter's opening section on the marks of the Dharma-body), so how can any mind-activity — even the compassionate activity of teaching and transforming beings — still arise from it? The resolution turns on distinguishing two very different senses of "effort." What the Dharma-body lacks is present, deliberate, moment-to-moment effort — the buddha does not have to decide, right now, to act. What it does not lack is the momentum of effort already completed in the causal ground, back when this buddha was still a bodhisattva cultivating skillful means joined to wisdom (方便般若) across the four kinds of transformation named in juan 74 — ripening the unripe, liberating the ripe, and manifesting as śrāvaka or pratyekabuddha teachers as needed. That accumulated momentum keeps yielding activity automatically, the way a sleeper who resolved the night before to wake at a set hour needs no fresh effort to actually open his eyes when the hour comes, or the way a practitioner in the cessation of perception and feeling — where none of the first seven consciousnesses is functioning at all — still rises from that absorption at the appointed signal because of an intention set before ever entering it. In both analogies, nothing is being done in the moment of arising itself; what arises simply completes a causal arc set in motion long before.

127. Section: Neither Possessing Mind Nor Without It — The Transformation-Body's Paradox

World-Honored One! Should the Tathāgata's transformation-body be said to possess mind, or to be without mind? Good son! It is neither that it possesses mind, nor that it is without mind. Why is this? Because it has no mind that depends on itself, yet it has a mind that depends on another.

Chinese Source

世尊！如來化身當言有心，為無心耶？善男子！非是有心，亦非無心。何以故？無自依心故，有依他心故。

Ru-Yi Translation

World-Honored One! Should the Tathāgata's transformation-body be said to possess mind, or to be without mind?
Good son! It is neither that it possesses mind, nor that it is without mind. Why is this? Because it has no mind that depends on itself, yet it has a mind that depends on another.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

The 披尋記 glosses the two terms precisely against the passage just completed: "no mind that depends on itself" repeats that the Tathāgata is not manifested through the arising of mind, mentation, or consciousness — the transformation-body has no ripened-karma mind of its own to depend on, since a buddha has already exhausted all ripening within the three realms; "a mind that depends on another" repeats that the Tathāgatas nonetheless have an effortless arising of mental dharmas, "just like a magical transformation" — dependent, that is, on the great compassion that never abandons sentient beings, and on the momentum of the causal-ground effort just explained. So the transformation-body is not simply mindless, an empty puppet with nothing behind it; nor does it possess mind in the ordinary sense of a self-sustaining stream of ripened consciousness. It occupies a third position altogether — an activity that functions precisely like mind, arising exactly when needed to benefit beings, while depending on nothing but a vow and a momentum set in motion long ago, the way a sleeper rises from cessation at the appointed hour with nothing left to decide.

128. Section: What the Tathāgata Enjoys, and the Five Realms of the Tathāgata's Domain

World-Honored One! What is the difference between what the Tathāgata enjoys and the Tathāgata's domain, these two? Good son! What the Tathāgata enjoys is: the pure buddha-field, adorned with every kind of boundless and inconceivable virtue, held in common by all the Tathāgatas of every kind. The Tathāgata's domain is: the differentiation of the five realms of every kind. What are the five? First, the realm of sentient beings; second, the realm of worlds; third, the realm of dharmas; fourth, the realm of what is to be tamed; and fifth, the realm of the skillful means for taming. This is called the difference between these two.

Chinese Source

世尊！如來所行、如來境界，此之二種有何差別？善男子！如來所行，謂一切種如來共有、不可思議、無量功德眾所莊嚴清淨佛土。如來境界，謂一切種五界差別。何等為五？一者、有情界，二者、世界，三者、法界，四者、調伏界，五者、調伏方便界。如是名為二種差別。

Ru-Yi Translation

World-Honored One! What is the difference between what the Tathāgata enjoys and the Tathāgata's domain, these two?
Good son! What the Tathāgata enjoys is: the pure buddha-field, adorned with every kind of boundless and inconceivable virtue, held in common by all the Tathāgatas of every kind.
The Tathāgata's domain is: the differentiation of the five realms of every kind. What are the five? First, the realm of sentient beings; second, the realm of worlds; third, the realm of dharmas; fourth, the realm of what is to be tamed; and fifth, the realm of the skillful means for taming.
This is called the difference between these two.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

The fifth great topic distinguishes two senses in which a buddha "occupies" reality. What a Tathāgata enjoys names the pure buddha-field itself, held only among buddhas — the inconceivable field of self-enjoyment no bodhisattva below full awakening can share. The Tathāgata's domain, by contrast, names the field of buddha-activity turned outward toward beings — the boundless realm of sentient beings to be taught, the worlds they inhabit, the full range of wholesome, unwholesome, and neutral dharmas that make up reality, the beings still to be tamed in all their variety of faculty and disposition, and the boundless skillful means by which taming is accomplished, one method fitted to each being who needs it.

129. Section: Perfect Awakening, Turning the Wheel, and Great Nirvāṇa — All Three Without Duality

World-Honored One! The Tathāgata's attainment of perfect awakening, turning of the wheel of the true Dharma, and entry into great nirvāṇa — these three, what mark should be known of them? Good son! Know that these three are all without a mark of duality. That is: it is not that perfect awakening is attained, nor that it is not attained; not that the wheel of the true Dharma is turned, nor that it is not turned; not that great nirvāṇa is entered, nor that it is not entered. Why is this? Because the Tathāgata's Dharma-body is ultimately pure, and because the Tathāgata's transformation-body is constantly manifest.

Chinese Source

世尊！如來成等正覺、轉正法輪、入大涅槃，如是三種當知何相？善男子！當知此三皆無二相。謂非成等正覺，非不成等正覺；非轉正法輪，非不轉正法輪；非入大涅槃，非不入大涅槃。何以故？如來法身究竟淨故，如來化身常示現故。

Ru-Yi Translation

World-Honored One! The Tathāgata's attainment of perfect awakening, turning of the wheel of the true Dharma, and entry into great nirvāṇa — these three, what mark should be known of them?
Good son! Know that these three are all without a mark of duality. That is: it is not that perfect awakening is attained, nor that it is not attained; not that the wheel of the true Dharma is turned, nor that it is not turned; not that great nirvāṇa is entered, nor that it is not entered.
Why is this? Because the Tathāgata's Dharma-body is ultimately pure, and because the Tathāgata's transformation-body is constantly manifest.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

The sixth topic applies the same法身/化身 distinction already established to the three events tradition marks as a buddha's career: awakening beneath the tree, the first turning of the wheel at Sārnāth, and the final passing at Kuśinagara. From the Dharma-body's side — ultimately pure, having attained the transformed basis of the Great Vehicle, free of every trace of the two obstructions' habitual residue, free of discursive elaboration, resting on the unconditioned, without effort — none of these three events can be said to happen at all, since the Dharma-body was never unawakened, never silent, and never bound to begin with. From the transformation-body's side — constantly manifesting for the sake of every being's benefit — none of the three can be said not to happen, since the transformation appears precisely as awakening, teaching, and passing, again and again, wherever beings need it. Both statements are true at once, which is why the text insists on "no mark of duality" rather than choosing one side: this same twofold reading applies equally whether the tradition counts eight aspects of a buddha's career, or eleven, or thirteen.

130. Section: Why Even the Transformation-Body Generates Merit, and How the Dharma-Body Radiates Like Sun and Moon

World-Honored One! Beings of every kind generate their various merits only through seeing, hearing, and serving the transformation-body — what cause and condition does the Tathāgata bear toward them in this? Good son! The Tathāgata is, for them, the dominant objective cause and condition; and further, that transformation-body is upheld by the Tathāgata's own power. World-Honored One! Being equally without effort, for what cause and condition does the Tathāgata's Dharma-body emit great light of wisdom for the sake of sentient beings, and send forth boundless images of transformation-bodies, while the liberated bodies of śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas have no such thing? Good son! It is like this: being equally without effort, great light is emitted from the two kinds of crystal-gem of water and fire — the disks of the moon and the sun — but not from any other water- or fire-crystal. This is because it is upheld by a being of great majestic virtue, and because of the dominant power of sentient beings' own karma.

Chinese Source

世尊！諸有情類但於化身見聞奉事生諸功德，如來於彼有何因緣？善男子！如來是彼增上所緣之因緣故，又彼化身是如來力所住持故。世尊！等無加行，何因緣故，如來法身為諸有情放大智光，及出無量化身影像，聲聞、獨覺解脫之身無如是事？善男子！譬如等無加行，從日月輪水火二種頗胝迦寶放大光明，非餘水火頗胝迦寶。謂大威德有情所住持故，諸有情業增上力故。

Ru-Yi Translation

World-Honored One! Beings of every kind generate their various merits only through seeing, hearing, and serving the transformation-body — what cause and condition does the Tathāgata bear toward them in this?
Good son! The Tathāgata is, for them, the dominant objective cause and condition; and further, that transformation-body is upheld by the Tathāgata's own power.
World-Honored One! Being equally without effort, for what cause and condition does the Tathāgata's Dharma-body emit great light of wisdom for the sake of sentient beings, and send forth boundless images of transformation-bodies, while the liberated bodies of śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas have no such thing?
Good son! It is like this: being equally without effort, great light is emitted from the two kinds of crystal-gem of water and fire — the disks of the moon and the sun — but not from any other water- or fire-crystal. This is because it is upheld by a being of great majestic virtue, and because of the dominant power of sentient beings' own karma.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

The seventh topic explains why merit accrues even to beings who only ever encounter the transformation-body, never the Dharma-body directly: the Dharma-body still functions as the transformation-body's dominant condition and support, so honoring the visible transformation genuinely reaches, and is upheld by, what stands behind it. The eighth topic then explains why only a buddha's Dharma-body — never a liberated śrāvaka's or pratyekabuddha's — radiates wisdom-light and projects transformation-bodies, opening with the first of two analogies: sun and moon alike shine without any effort of their own, yet only these two particular crystal disks blaze with great light while ordinary water- and fire-crystal do not, because a being of great majestic power upholds them, and because beings' own accumulated karma gives them the capacity to see that light when it shines. Neither factor is optional — the light must genuinely be there to see, and the seer must have the karmic capacity to perceive it.

131. Section: The Carved Gem, and Why Only a Buddha's Dharma-Body Casts Light and Transformation — Closing the Difference Between the Dharma-Body and the Liberation-Body

And it is also like how, from a wish-fulfilling gem carved by a skilled craftsman, an imprinted image emerges — not from any other, uncarved gem. In just this way, the Tathāgata's Dharma-body — brought to completion by taking the boundless dharma-realm as its object, through the most excellent cultivation and polishing of skillful means joined to wisdom — is able, from this, to emit great light of wisdom and to send forth every kind of image of the transformation-body; this is not a thing that belongs to the liberation-body alone.

Chinese Source

又如從彼善工業者之所雕飾末尼寶珠出印文像，不從所餘不雕飾者。如是緣於無量法界方便般若極善修習磨瑩集成如來法身，從是能放大智光明，及出種種化身影像，非唯從彼解脫之身有如斯事。

Ru-Yi Translation

And it is also like how, from a wish-fulfilling gem carved by a skilled craftsman, an imprinted image emerges — not from any other, uncarved gem.
In just this way, the Tathāgata's Dharma-body — brought to completion by taking the boundless dharma-realm as its object, through the most excellent cultivation and polishing of skillful means joined to wisdom — is able, from this, to emit great light of wisdom and to send forth every kind of image of the transformation-body; this is not a thing that belongs to the liberation-body alone.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

The second analogy makes the same point from the side of preparation rather than power: only a wish-fulfilling gem that a skilled craftsman has actually carved will cast a clear imprinted image when light passes through it; an uncarved gem, however precious its raw material, casts nothing. A buddha's mind, polished across three incalculable eons of taking the entire dharma-realm as its object and cultivating skillful means joined to wisdom, is the carved gem; a liberated śrāvaka's or pratyekabuddha's mind, genuinely free of affliction yet never so cultivated, is the uncarved one — which is why a śrāvaka can reach arhatship in as few as three lifetimes (sixty at the outside) and a pratyekabuddha in four (a hundred at the outside), yet neither ever radiates the Dharma-body's light or projects its transformation-bodies. With this, the difference between the Dharma-body and the liberation-body — why only the former casts light and sends forth transformation, closing the eighth great topic of the chapter — is fully explained.

132. Section: The Hidden Intent Behind the Majestic Virtue of Buddhas and Bodhisattvas — Wealth Both Given and Withheld

World-Honored One! As the World-Honored One has said: through the majestic virtue upheld by Tathāgatas and bodhisattvas, sentient beings are caused, within the desire realm, to be born into great and wealthy families such as those of kings and brahmins, their human bodies and riches in every way perfected; or to obtain, in the heavens of the desire realm and in the form and formless realms, every kind of bodily and material perfection. World-Honored One, what hidden intent lies within this? Good son! Wherever the majestic virtue upheld by Tathāgatas and bodhisattvas — whether through a path or through a practice — is able to cause sentient beings, in every place, to obtain the perfection of both body and wealth, it is precisely in accordance with this that this path and this practice are proclaimed for their sake; and whoever is able to practice rightly in accordance with this path and this practice obtains, in every place, nothing but perfection of body and wealth. But if there are sentient beings who turn against and despise this path and this practice, and who, toward me, give rise to a mind of injury and a mind of hatred, then, after life ends, in every place they obtain nothing but inferior body and wealth. Good son! For this cause and condition, know that the majestic virtue upheld by Tathāgatas and bodhisattvas not only can cause the perfection of body and wealth — the majestic virtue upheld by Tathāgatas and bodhisattvas can also cause sentient beings' body and wealth to become inferior.

Chinese Source

世尊！如世尊說：如來菩薩威德住持，令諸眾生於欲界中，生剎帝利、婆羅門等大富貴家，人身財寶無不圓滿；或欲界天、色無色界一切身財圓滿可得。世尊！此中有何密意？善男子！如來菩薩威德住持，若道、若行，於一切處能令眾生獲得身財皆圓滿者，即隨所應，為彼宣說此道、此行。若有能於此道、此行正修行者，於一切處所獲身財無不圓滿。若有眾生於此道、此行違背輕毀，又於我所起損惱心及瞋恚心，命終已後，於一切處所得身財無不下劣。善男子！由是因緣，當知如來及諸菩薩威德住持，非但能令身財圓滿；如來菩薩住持威德，亦令眾生身財下劣。

Ru-Yi Translation

World-Honored One! As the World-Honored One has said: through the majestic virtue upheld by Tathāgatas and bodhisattvas, sentient beings are caused, within the desire realm, to be born into great and wealthy families such as those of kings and brahmins, their human bodies and riches in every way perfected; or to obtain, in the heavens of the desire realm and in the form and formless realms, every kind of bodily and material perfection. World-Honored One, what hidden intent lies within this?
Good son!
