Wherever the majestic virtue upheld by Tathāgatas and bodhisattvas — whether through a path or through a practice — is able to cause sentient beings, in every place, to obtain the perfection of both body and wealth, it is precisely in accordance with this that this path and this practice are proclaimed for their sake; and whoever is able to practice rightly in accordance with this path and this practice obtains, in every place, nothing but perfection of body and wealth.
But if there are sentient beings who turn against and despise this path and this practice, and who, toward me, give rise to a mind of injury and a mind of hatred, then, after life ends, in every place they obtain nothing but inferior body and wealth.
Good son! For this cause and condition, know that the majestic virtue upheld by Tathāgatas and bodhisattvas not only can cause the perfection of body and wealth — the majestic virtue upheld by Tathāgatas and bodhisattvas can also cause sentient beings' body and wealth to become inferior.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

The ninth topic dissolves an apparent puzzle: why does encountering a buddha or bodhisattva's majestic presence sometimes correlate with worldly flourishing, when Buddhism plainly does not teach that mere proximity to holiness confers wealth automatically? The hidden intent is that it is never proximity alone that matters, but the response it draws out. Buddhas and bodhisattvas, upheld by their majestic virtue, teach whatever path or practice — the five precepts, the ten wholesome courses of action, worldly or transcendent cultivation, virtuous bodily, verbal, and mental conduct — will in fact yield bodily and material perfection wherever it is genuinely practiced; a being who takes up that teaching in earnest reaps exactly what it promises. But the same encounter cuts the opposite way for a being who meets that teaching with contempt, or worse, with active malice toward the teacher — rejecting good counsel and generating hostility toward a genuine guide is itself a karmically potent act, ripening into inferior circumstances after death. Master Cháng Bǎi notes this is also why some noble ones, foreseeing that their evident sanctity might provoke exactly this reaction in the unready, choose to keep their attainment hidden rather than announce it.

133. Section: Impure and Pure Lands — Eight Things Easily Had and Two Things Rare, Reversed — Closing All Ten Great Topics of the Chapter

World-Honored One! In impure lands, what things are easily obtained, and what things are hard to obtain? In pure lands, what things are easily obtained, and what things are hard to obtain? Good son! In impure lands, eight things are easily obtained, and two things are hard to obtain. What are the eight things easily obtained? First, non-Buddhist teachers; second, sentient beings in suffering; third, the differences of decline and flourishing among lineages and families; fourth, the practice of every kind of evil conduct; fifth, the violation of the moral precepts; sixth, the lower destinies; seventh, the lesser vehicles; and eighth, bodhisattvas of inferior aspiration and effort. What are the two things hard to obtain? First, the gathering-together of bodhisattvas of superior aspiration and effort; and second, a Tathāgata's appearance in the world. Good son! In pure lands the reverse holds: know that these same eight things are exceedingly hard to obtain, and the two things are easily obtained.

Chinese Source

世尊！諸穢土中何事易得，何事難得？諸淨土中何事易得，何事難得？善男子！諸穢土中，八事易得，二事難得。何等名為八事易得？一者、外道，二者、有苦眾生，三者、種姓家世興衰差別，四者、行諸惡行，五者、毀犯尸羅，六者、惡趣，七者、下乘，八者、下劣意樂加行菩薩。何等名為二事難得？一者、增上意樂加行菩薩之所遊集，二者、如來出現於世。善男子！諸淨土中與上相違，當知八事甚為難得，二事易得。

Ru-Yi Translation

World-Honored One! In impure lands, what things are easily obtained, and what things are hard to obtain? In pure lands, what things are easily obtained, and what things are hard to obtain?
Good son! In impure lands, eight things are easily obtained, and two things are hard to obtain. What are the eight things easily obtained? First, non-Buddhist teachers; second, sentient beings in suffering; third, the differences of decline and flourishing among lineages and families; fourth, the practice of every kind of evil conduct; fifth, the violation of the moral precepts; sixth, the lower destinies; seventh, the lesser vehicles; and eighth, bodhisattvas of inferior aspiration and effort.
What are the two things hard to obtain? First, the gathering-together of bodhisattvas of superior aspiration and effort; and second, a Tathāgata's appearance in the world.
Good son! In pure lands the reverse holds: know that these same eight things are exceedingly hard to obtain, and the two things are easily obtained.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

The tenth and final topic closes the chapter by contrasting our own impure world with a pure land such as Sukhāvatī along a single consistent axis: an impure land's very harshness — its wrong teachers, its suffering beings, its unstable fortunes, its rampant misconduct and broken precepts, its ready descent into the lower destinies, its crowds eager only for personal liberation, and its many bodhisattvas of inferior aspiration still too fragile for the task — is exactly what makes genuinely superior-aspiration bodhisattvas and a buddha's own appearance so rare here, and so precious when they do occur. A pure land inverts every term: with none of the eight harsh conditions to contend with, both a critical mass of superior-aspiration bodhisattvas and the sustained presence of a living buddha become the norm rather than the exception. Master Yǎn Péi's gloss, cited here, reframes the choice this presents to a practitioner: one whose wisdom and resolve are already firm is urged to return to this impure world and labor here, doing what is hard to do; one whose resolve is not yet firm does better first seeking further training in a pure land, then returning once genuinely equal to the task — which is exactly why the text notes bodhisattvas of inferior aspiration are so plentiful in a world like this one, still gathering the strength for what harder work still lies ahead. With this, all ten great topics of the Chapter Distinguishing Accomplished Activities — the marks of the Dharma-body, the marks of its arising, the varieties of speech, the arising of mind, the domain enjoyed and inhabited, the non-duality of awakening and teaching and passing, the cause of generating merit, the difference between the Dharma-body and the liberation-body, the hidden intent behind majestic virtue, and the difference between impure and pure lands — are now complete in full.

134. Section: Naming the Teaching, and Seventy-Five Thousand Bodhisattvas Attain the Perfected Dharma-Body — The Entire Saṃdhinirmocana Citation, and Fascicle 78, Complete

World-Honored One! Within this Dharma-door of Saṃdhinirmocana, by what name should this teaching be called? How should I uphold it? Good son! This is called the teaching of the definitive meaning of the Tathāgata's accomplished activities; you should uphold this teaching of the definitive meaning of the Tathāgata's accomplished activities. When this teaching of the definitive meaning of the Tathāgata's accomplished activities was spoken, within the great assembly, seventy-five thousand great bodhisattvas all attained the perfected realization of the Dharma-body.

Chinese Source

世尊！於此解深密法門中，此名何教？我當云何奉持？善男子！此名如來成所作事了義之教，於此如來成所作事了義之教，汝當奉持。說是如來成所作事了義教時，於大會中，有七十五千菩薩摩訶薩，皆得圓滿法身證覺。

Ru-Yi Translation

World-Honored One! Within this Dharma-door of Saṃdhinirmocana, by what name should this teaching be called? How should I uphold it?
Good son! This is called the teaching of the definitive meaning of the Tathāgata's accomplished activities; you should uphold this teaching of the definitive meaning of the Tathāgata's accomplished activities.
When this teaching of the definitive meaning of the Tathāgata's accomplished activities was spoken, within the great assembly, seventy-five thousand great bodhisattvas all attained the perfected realization of the Dharma-body.

Reading note — after Master Cháng Bǎi

The chapter closes exactly as its six companion chapters have each closed in turn, Mañjuśrī asking the Buddha to name what has just been taught so that it may be rightly upheld and transmitted: "the teaching of the definitive meaning of the Tathāgata's accomplished activities" (如來成所作事了義之教), definitive rather than provisional because it states directly and completely how a buddha's Dharma-body, transformation-bodies, and liberating activity actually relate, with nothing further to unpack. The assembly's harvest this time is reported in a single figure rather than the fourfold tally that closed the Chapter Distinguishing Yoga — seventy-five thousand great bodhisattvas attaining the perfected realization of the Dharma-body itself, the tenth ground's culmination, the very attainment this whole chapter has been describing from the outside now actually reached by those who heard it taught. Master Cháng Bǎi's own final note observes that the citation begun back in fascicle 75, carrying the Saṃdhinirmocana Sūtra's teaching on ultimate truth's five characteristics through the secret meaning of mind, the three natures, the chapter distinguishing yoga's twenty-six doors, the chapter distinguishing the ground-pāramitās, and now this closing chapter on accomplished activities, has here run its full course.

With this, the entire citation of the Saṃdhinirmocana Sūtra's seven chapters within the Determination (攝決擇分) of the Bodhisattva Ground — the chapter on the marks of ultimate truth, the chapter on the secret meaning of mind, intellect, and consciousness, the chapter on the characteristics of all dharmas, the chapter on all dharmas' lack of an own-nature, the chapter distinguishing yoga, the chapter distinguishing the ground-pāramitās, and this chapter distinguishing accomplished activities — closes in full, spanning fascicles 75 through 78. And with this final section, fascicle 78 itself is complete: the Chapter Distinguishing the Ground-Pāramitās in its entirety (the ten bodhisattva grounds, their obstructions and excellences, and the six pāramitās across every angle the treatise brings to bear), followed by the Chapter Distinguishing Accomplished Activities in its entirety (the Dharma-body and its arising, the three kinds of speech culminating in the eleven marks of the fundamental matrices, the dhāraṇī-meaning not shared with heretics, the arising of mind without effort, and all ten topics on a buddha's domain and activity, closing on impure and pure lands) — the longest fascicle yet translated in this series, and the close of the treatise's seven-chapter citation of one of Mahāyāna Buddhism's most important scriptures.
